In (b) accept 50% or one half; note that a candidate who answers (a) correctly but (b) as two thirds has applied the theorem without understanding it. In (c) accept any value between 3.20 V and 3.22 V from premature rounding of the parallel pair. In (d) accept 4.8% to two significant figures, and credit the exact form 5/105. In (e) accept 1.7 ohms, and allow a graphical solution from a V against I plot provided the gradient is taken with the correct sign. Across the cluster, a candidate who rounds the parallel resistance in (c) prematurely may report anything from 3.20 V to 3.22 V and should be credited; a comparable tolerance applies in (e). In (d) the exact form 5.0 over 105 earns the mark even when left as a fraction. Note that (a) and (b) are formally independent, so a candidate may answer either without the other, and no follow-through between them should be assumed either way.

41. B.4 Thermodynamics

[16 marks]PHYS-B.4-001

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.3 Gas laws (ideal gas equation), B.4 Thermodynamics (AHL): first law, work, internal energy, heat engine efficiency · difficulty 4

(a) From $pV=nRT$, with $n$ and $R$ constant, $T\propto pV$.

A → B is isochoric, so $T\propto p$: $\dfrac{T_{B}}{T_{A}}=\dfrac{p_{B}}{p_{A}}=\dfrac{4.0}{2.0}=2 \Rightarrow T_{B}=600\ \mathrm{K}$. (M1)(A1)

B → C is isobaric, so $T\propto V$: $\dfrac{T_{C}}{T_{B}}=\dfrac{V_{C}}{V_{B}}=\dfrac{1.0\times10^{-2}}{5.0\times10^{-3}}=2 \Rightarrow T_{C}=1200\ \mathrm{K}$. (A1)

C → D is isochoric: $\dfrac{T_{D}}{T_{C}}=\dfrac{p_{D}}{p_{C}}=\dfrac{2.0}{4.0}=\dfrac12 \Rightarrow T_{D}=600\ \mathrm{K}$. (A1)

(Consistency check: D → A is isobaric with $\frac{V_A}{V_D}=\frac12$, giving $T_{A}=300\ \mathrm{K}$ as stated.)

(b) Work is done only on the two isochoric-free legs:

B → C: $W=p\,\Delta V=4.0\times10^{5}(1.0\times10^{-2}-5.0\times10^{-3})=4.0\times10^{5}(5.0\times10^{-3})=2000\ \mathrm{J}$ (A1)

D → A: $W=p\,\Delta V=2.0\times10^{5}(5.0\times10^{-3}-1.0\times10^{-2})=-1000\ \mathrm{J}$ (A1)

$$W_{\text{net}}=2000-1000=1000\ \mathrm{J}$$ (A1)

(Equivalently, the area of the rectangle: $(4.0-2.0)\times10^{5}\times(1.0-0.5)\times10^{-2}=1000\ \mathrm{J}$.)

(c) Heat flows in during the two legs where the temperature rises: A → B and B → C.

A → B (isochoric): $W=0$, so by the first law $Q=\Delta U$.

$$Q_{AB}=\tfrac32 nR\,\Delta T=\tfrac32(0.40)(8.31)(600-300)=1.5(0.40)(8.31)(300)=1496\ \mathrm{J}$$ (M1)(A1)

B → C (isobaric): $\Delta U=\tfrac32 nR\,\Delta T=\tfrac32(0.40)(8.31)(1200-600)=2992\ \mathrm{J}$ (A1)

$W=2000\ \mathrm{J}$ from (b), so by the first law $Q=\Delta U+W$:

$$Q_{BC}=2992+2000=4992\ \mathrm{J}$$ (M1)(A1)

$$Q_{\text{in}}=1496+4992=6488\approx6.5\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{J}$$

(d)

$$\eta=\frac{W_{\text{net}}}{Q_{\text{in}}}=\frac{1000}{6488}=0.154=15.4\%$$ (A1)

A Carnot engine between $T_{\max}=1200\ \mathrm{K}$ and $T_{\min}=300\ \mathrm{K}$ has

$$\eta_{\text{Carnot}}=1-\frac{T_{\min}}{T_{\max}}=1-\frac{300}{1200}=0.75=75\%$$ (M1)(A1)

So this cycle achieves only about one fifth of the Carnot efficiency. (A1) The reason is that heat is taken in over a whole *range* of temperatures — including during the isochoric leg A → B, where the gas is still relatively cool — and rejected over a range as well (C → D and D → A). A Carnot engine takes in all of its heat at the single highest temperature and rejects it all at the single lowest; any engine that absorbs heat while its temperature is changing must do worse.

Markscheme notes

(a) Credit either the ratio argument or direct substitution into $T=pV/nR$. Using $R=8.31$ and $n=0.40$ gives $T_{A}=301\ \mathrm{K}$ rather than exactly $300\ \mathrm{K}$; this is rounding in the data and must not be penalised. Award full marks for temperatures given as 600, 1200, 600 K.

(b) Award (A1) for each leg's work with the correct sign, (A1) for the net. Credit the 'area of the p–V loop' method fully if the area is correctly identified as $(p_{2}-p_{1})(V_{2}-V_{1})$.

(c) The key discrimination is *which* legs to include. Candidates who sum $\Delta U$ over the whole cycle get zero (correctly, since it returns to A) and conclude $Q_{\text{in}}=W_{\text{net}}$, which is wrong — heat is also *rejected*. Award (M1) for applying $Q=\Delta U+W$ on a heating leg. Condone 6.4–6.5 kJ given rounding.

(d) Accept 15%–16%. The Carnot comparison must use the *extreme* temperatures of the cycle (300 K and 1200 K), not the mean. The final (A1) is for an explanation in terms of heat being absorbed/rejected across a temperature range; 'real engines are always less efficient' is not sufficient.

42. A flywheel, a hanging mass, and a brake

[16 marks]PHYS-A.4-001

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.4 Rigid body mechanics (AHL): torque, moment of inertia of a disc, torque = I x angular acceleration, rotational kinetic energy · difficulty 4

(a) Let $T$ be the string tension and $a$ the acceleration of the mass; the string does not slip, so the angular acceleration of the disc is $\alpha=a/R$. (M1)

For the hanging mass: $mg-T=ma$ (M1)

For the disc, torque $\tau=TR$ and moment of inertia $I=\dfrac{1}{2}MR^{2}$, so $TR=\dfrac{1}{2}MR^{2}\cdot\dfrac{a}{R}=\dfrac{1}{2}MRa$, giving $T=\dfrac{1}{2}Ma$. (M1)(A1)

Substituting: $mg-\dfrac{1}{2}Ma=ma$, so $a=\dfrac{mg}{m+\dfrac{M}{2}}$. (AG)

Numerically $a=\dfrac{0.80\times 9.81}{0.80+1.2}=\dfrac{7.848}{2.0}=3.92\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. (A1)

(b) $T=\dfrac{1}{2}Ma=\dfrac{1}{2}\times 2.4\times 3.924=4.71\ \mathrm{N}$. (M1)(A1)

The weight of the mass is $mg=0.80\times 9.81=7.85\ \mathrm{N}$, so $T<mg$. (A1) The tension is less than the weight because the mass is accelerating downwards: the resultant downward force $mg-T$ is what supplies its acceleration, and if $T$ equalled $mg$ the mass would not accelerate at all. Equivalently, the tension is the torque that must be provided to give the disc its angular acceleration, and that comes at the cost of part of the weight.

(c) $v^{2}=2ah=2\times 3.924\times 0.90=7.063$, so $v=2.66\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (M1)(A1)

Energy check: loss of potential energy $=mgh=0.80\times 9.81\times 0.90=7.06\ \mathrm{J}$. (M1)

The angular speed of the disc is $\omega=\dfrac{v}{R}=\dfrac{2.6577}{0.35}=7.59\ \mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}$ and $I=\dfrac{1}{2}(2.4)(0.35)^{2}=0.1470\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{2}}$, so the rotational kinetic energy is $\dfrac{1}{2}I\omega^{2}=\dfrac{1}{2}(0.1470)(57.66)=4.24\ \mathrm{J}$.

Adding the translational kinetic energy of the mass, $\dfrac{1}{2}(0.80)(2.6577)^{2}=2.83\ \mathrm{J}$, gives $7.06\ \mathrm{J}$, which equals the loss of potential energy. (A1)

(d) At the moment the string goes slack the disc has $\omega=7.59\ \mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}$ and rotational kinetic energy $E_{k}=4.24\ \mathrm{J}$. (M1)

With a constant braking torque, the work done equals the change in rotational kinetic energy: $\tau\theta=E_{k}$, so $\theta=\dfrac{4.238}{0.24}=17.7\ \mathrm{rad}$. (M1)(A1)

$17.7\ \mathrm{rad}=\dfrac{17.66}{2\pi}=2.81$ revolutions, so the disc makes $2$ complete revolutions before stopping. (A1)

Markscheme notes

Part (a): the (AG) requires both equations of motion to be stated before substitution. A candidate who writes $a=g$ — treating the disc as massless or the tension as zero — earns nothing beyond the first (M1). Accept the use of $I=kMR^{2}$ with $k=1/2$ stated, and accept a solution by energy methods that differentiates an energy expression, provided the tension is still identified. Part (b): award the (A1) for the comparison only if a numerical or symbolic value of $mg$ is given. Part (c): the energy verification mark is independent of the kinematic calculation; a candidate whose kinematics slips but whose energy comparison is internally consistent still earns the final (A1). Follow-through applies from (a). Part (d): the critical step is converting the rotational kinetic energy into an angle; a candidate who divides by $\tau$ but then forgets to convert radians to revolutions loses the last (A1) only. A common error is to use $E_{k}=\dfrac{1}{2}I\omega^{2}$ with $\omega$ in revolutions per second; check units explicitly. Do not accept an answer of 2.81 revolutions without the conversion stated, and do not penalise a candidate who truncates to 2 with a correct reason.

43. Two readings in a vacuum chamber: emissivity and specific heat

[16 marks]PHYS-B.1-001

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.1 Thermal energy transfers (specific heat capacity, latent heat, conduction, convection, radiation, Stefan-Boltzmann law) · difficulty 4

(a) In an evacuated chamber there is no gas to conduct heat or to carry it by convection, and radiation is the only transfer mechanism that needs no medium. (A1) The block emits $\varepsilon\sigma A T^{4}$ and absorbs $\varepsilon\sigma A T_{\text{wall}}^{4}$ from the walls, (A1) so the net power it loses is

$$P_{\text{rad}}=\varepsilon\sigma A\left(T^{4}-T_{\text{wall}}^{4}\right).$$ (A1)

(b) At steady state the electrical power supplied is exactly balanced by the net radiated power:

$$P=\varepsilon\sigma A\left(T_{s}^{4}-T_{\text{wall}}^{4}\right)$$ (M1)

$$\varepsilon=\frac{P}{\sigma A\left(T_{s}^{4}-T_{\text{wall}}^{4}\right)}$$ (A1)

$$\varepsilon=\frac{15.0}{5.67\times10^{-8}\times 0.020\times\left(3.1117\times10^{10}-8.100\times10^{9}\right)}$$ (A1)

$$=\frac{15.0}{5.67\times10^{-8}\times 0.020\times 2.3017\times10^{10}}=\frac{15.0}{26.10}=0.575$$ (A1)

(c) At the instant the heater is switched off the block is still at $420\ \mathrm{K}$, and the net radiated power depends only on the temperatures of the block and the walls, so it is unchanged at $15.0\ \mathrm{W}$. (M1) The thermal energy of the block now falls at a rate $mc\left|\dfrac{\Delta T}{\Delta t}\right|$, and this must equal the radiated power:

$$mc\frac{\Delta T}{\Delta t}=\varepsilon\sigma A\left(T_{s}^{4}-T_{\text{wall}}^{4}\right)=15.0\ \mathrm{W}$$ (M1)(A1)

$$c=\frac{15.0}{m\,\Delta T/\Delta t}=\frac{15.0}{0.50\times 0.130}=\frac{15.0}{0.065}$$ (A1)

$$=231\ \mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$$ (A1)

(d) At $360\ \mathrm{K}$,

$$P=\varepsilon\sigma A\left(360^{4}-300^{4}\right)=0.5747\times 5.67\times10^{-8}\times 0.020\times\left(1.6796\times10^{10}-8.100\times10^{9}\right)$$ (M1)

$$=0.5747\times 5.67\times10^{-8}\times 0.020\times 8.6962\times10^{9}=5.67\ \mathrm{W}$$ (A1)

$$\frac{\Delta T}{\Delta t}=\frac{P}{mc}=\frac{5.67}{0.50\times 230.8}=0.0491\ \mathrm{K\,s^{-1}}$$ (A1)

so the ratio to the rate at $420\ \mathrm{K}$ is $\dfrac{0.0491}{0.130}=0.378$. A model in which the rate is proportional to $\left(T-T_{\text{wall}}\right)$ would instead give $\dfrac{360-300}{420-300}=\dfrac{60}{120}=0.50$. (A1) The two predictions differ by about 30%, and it is the $T^{4}$ prediction that fits the data, because the radiated power falls with the difference of the fourth powers of the absolute temperatures rather than with their difference.

Markscheme notes

(a) The three (A1)s are for: radiation needs no medium, so an evacuated chamber removes conduction and convection; the surface emits $\varepsilon\sigma A T^{4}$; and the net loss subtracts the absorbed $\varepsilon\sigma A T_{\text{wall}}^{4}$. A candidate who omits the wall term and uses $P=\varepsilon\sigma A T^{4}$ alone has forgotten that the walls also radiate onto the block; this is a common and significant error and it forfeits the third mark.

(b) The (AG) requires the steady-state condition 'electrical power in equals net radiative power out' to be stated. Award the emissivity marks for $\varepsilon$ between $0.570$ and $0.580$. Do not accept a value obtained with the wrong surface area.

(c) The crucial step is recognising that the radiated power at $420\ \mathrm{K}$ is the same $15.0\ \mathrm{W}$ whether the heater is on or off, because it depends only on the block and wall temperatures. A candidate who tries to subtract something from $15.0\ \mathrm{W}$ forfeits the method mark. Accept $c$ in the range $225$–$237\ \mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Follow-through applies from (b): if the candidate's $\varepsilon$ differs, their $c$ will be consistent with it and should be credited.

(d) Accept $0.047$–$0.051\ \mathrm{K\,s^{-1}}$ for the rate and $0.37$–$0.39$ for the ratio. The final comparison mark requires both numbers to be stated; 'the $T^{4}$ law fits better' without the linear prediction of $0.50$ earns only one mark. Do not penalise a candidate who compares the rates themselves rather than the ratios, provided the comparison is quantitative. A candidate who uses temperatures in degrees Celsius in the fourth-power term loses the two value marks.

44. The layer that adds a fixed factor to the surface temperature

[16 marks]PHYS-B.2-001

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.2 Greenhouse effect (planetary energy balance, albedo, emissivity, surface temperature, one-layer atmosphere model) · difficulty 4

(a) Sunlight is intercepted over the planet's cross-sectional area $\pi R^{2}$, so the power absorbed is $P_{\text{abs}}=S\pi R^{2}(1-\alpha)$. (M1) The planet radiates from its whole surface, of area $4\pi R^{2}$, at the effective temperature $T_{e}$, so $P_{\text{emit}}=4\pi R^{2}\sigma T_{e}^{4}$. (M1) In equilibrium $P_{\text{abs}}=P_{\text{emit}}$:

$$S\pi R^{2}(1-\alpha)=4\pi R^{2}\sigma T_{e}^{4}\quad\Rightarrow\quad T_{e}^{4}=\frac{S(1-\alpha)}{4\sigma}$$ (M1)(AG)

$$T_{e}^{4}=\frac{1.36\times10^{3}\times 0.70}{4\times 5.67\times10^{-8}}=\frac{952}{2.268\times10^{-7}}=4.20\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{K^{4}}$$

$$T_{e}=255\ \mathrm{K}$$ (A1)

(b) Let $T_{a}$ be the temperature of the layer. The layer absorbs $\sigma T_{s}^{4}$ per unit area from the surface and radiates $\sigma T_{a}^{4}$ upwards and $\sigma T_{a}^{4}$ downwards, so (M1)

$$\sigma T_{s}^{4}=2\sigma T_{a}^{4}\quad\Rightarrow\quad T_{a}^{4}=\frac{T_{s}^{4}}{2}$$ (A1)

At the top of the atmosphere the only power leaving is the upward radiation of the layer, which must equal the absorbed solar power per unit area: (M1)

$$\sigma T_{a}^{4}=\frac{S(1-\alpha)}{4}=\sigma T_{e}^{4}\quad\Rightarrow\quad T_{a}=T_{e}$$

At the surface, the power absorbed is the sunlight $\dfrac{S(1-\alpha)}{4}$ plus the downward radiation from the layer, $\sigma T_{a}^{4}=\sigma T_{e}^{4}$: (M1)

$$\sigma T_{s}^{4}=\sigma T_{e}^{4}+\sigma T_{e}^{4}=2\sigma T_{e}^{4}\quad\Rightarrow\quad T_{s}=2^{1/4}T_{e}$$ (AG)

$$T_{s}=1.189\times 255=303\ \mathrm{K}$$ (A1)

(c) With $\alpha=0.34$,

$$T_{e}^{4}=\frac{1.36\times10^{3}\times 0.66}{4\times 5.67\times10^{-8}}=3.96\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{K^{4}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad T_{e}=251\ \mathrm{K}$$ (M1)

$$T_{s}=2^{1/4}\times 251=298\ \mathrm{K}$$ (A1)

The ratio is $T_{s}/T_{e}=2^{1/4}=1.19$ in both cases. (A1) It is unchanged because the factor $2^{1/4}$ arises from the geometry of the layer, not from the amount of sunlight reaching the planet.

(d) The model gives $303\ \mathrm{K}$ against an observed mean surface temperature of $288\ \mathrm{K}$, so it overestimates the warming above the effective temperature by about $15\ \mathrm{K}$. (A1) A single layer that absorbs all the infrared and is isothermal is an extremely strong absorber: it intercepts every photon leaving the surface and sends half of them back down, which maximises the back-radiation. A real atmosphere absorbs only in certain wavelength bands, so much of the surface radiation escapes directly to space through the atmospheric window, and the effective number of absorbing layers is far smaller than one fully opaque layer. (A1) In addition, a real atmosphere has a temperature that falls with height and is cooled by convection, which carries energy upwards and shortens the path over which the greenhouse effect operates — a feature that a single isothermal layer cannot represent. (A1) The model is nevertheless useful: it reproduces the correct order of magnitude and, more importantly, the correct qualitative result that the surface is warmer than the effective radiating temperature. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The three (M1)/(AG) marks are for the intercepted area $\pi R^{2}$, the emitting area $4\pi R^{2}$ and the equilibrium condition. A candidate who omits the factor of four obtains $T_{e}=360\ \mathrm{K}$; this is the single most common error and it loses the final two marks. The (AG) requires the two areas to be shown explicitly, not merely asserted. Accept $T_{e}=254$–$256\ \mathrm{K}$ and accept $255\ \mathrm{K}$.

(b) The layer's balance must be written as 'absorbs $\sigma T_{s}^{4}$, radiates $2\sigma T_{a}^{4}$'. A candidate who writes the layer balance as $\sigma T_{s}^{4}=\sigma T_{a}^{4}$, radiating upwards only, obtains $T_{s}=T_{e}$ and loses the method marks; the doubling is the entire content of the greenhouse effect. The surface balance must include the absorbed sunlight as well as the back-radiation. Follow-through applies from (a): a candidate whose $T_{e}$ is wrong but whose factor $2^{1/4}$ is correct earns the (AG) and the final (A1) on their own value.

(c) Accept $T_{e}=250$–$252\ \mathrm{K}$ and $T_{s}=297$–$299\ \mathrm{K}$. The key mark is the observation that the ratio is unchanged; a candidate who reports a different ratio has made an arithmetic slip. Do not accept a ratio quoted as $1.19$ with no justification for its constancy.

(d) The evaluation must name a specific deficiency, not merely say 'it is only a model'. Credit any of: partial absorption and the atmospheric window; convection and the lapse rate; clouds; the layer not being isothermal; multiple absorbing layers. Accept the numerical comparison of $303\ \mathrm{K}$ with $288\ \mathrm{K}$. Do not credit 'the model is wrong' without a mechanism.

45. E.4 Fission

[16 marks]PHYS-E.4-102

Theme E: Nuclear and quantum physics · E.4 (Fission) · difficulty 4

(a) One fission releases (M1)
$$200\ \mathrm{MeV} = 200 \times 10^{6} \times 1.60 \times 10^{-19}\ \mathrm{J} = 3.20 \times 10^{-11}\ \mathrm{J}$$
(A1) The electrical output is $1.0\ \mathrm{GW}$, so at $33\%$ efficiency the thermal power of the core is (M1)
$$P_{\text{th}} = \frac{1.0 \times 10^{9}}{0.33} = 3.0 \times 10^{9}\ \mathrm{W}$$
and the number of fissions per second is (A1)
$$N = \frac{3.0 \times 10^{9}}{3.20 \times 10^{-11}} = 9.4 \times 10^{19}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$$

(b) Each fission consumes one uranium-235 nucleus of mass $235 \times 1.66 \times 10^{-27} = 3.90 \times 10^{-25}\ \mathrm{kg}$. (M1) In one day the number of fissions is $9.4 \times 10^{19} \times 86\,400 = 8.1 \times 10^{24}$, so the mass consumed is (M1)
$$m = 8.1 \times 10^{24} \times 3.90 \times 10^{-25} = 3.2\ \mathrm{kg}$$
(A1) The energy delivered in one day is $3.0 \times 10^{9} \times 86\,400 = 2.6 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{J}$, so the equivalent mass of coal is (M1)
$$m_{\text{coal}} = \frac{2.6 \times 10^{14}}{30 \times 10^{6}} = 8.6 \times 10^{6}\ \mathrm{kg} \approx 8600\ \mathrm{tonnes}$$
(A1) The ratio of the two masses is $\dfrac{8.6 \times 10^{6}}{3.2} \approx 2.7 \times 10^{6}$, so kilogram for kilogram the nuclear fuel releases something like three million times as much energy as the coal. This is the number that makes the comparison worth doing: it is far too large to be recovered by any plausible improvement in the efficiency of a coal plant.

(c) Natural uranium is $0.7\%$ uranium-235 by mass, so the mass of natural uranium containing $3.2\ \mathrm{kg}$ of the fissile isotope is (M1)
$$m_{\text{nat}} = \frac{3.2}{0.007} = 4.6 \times 10^{2}\ \mathrm{kg} \approx 460\ \mathrm{kg}$$
(A1)(A1) This assumes complete extraction. In practice the enrichment process leaves some uranium-235 in the discarded tailings, so rather more than $460\ \mathrm{kg}$ must be mined; if the tails assay is $0.2\%$ rather than zero, the requirement rises to about $630\ \mathrm{kg}$ per day. (A1)

(d) Each fission releases on average $2.4$ neutrons, so a chain reaction is possible in principle: one fission can initiate more than one further fission. (A1) It does not happen automatically, for two reasons. First, some neutrons escape through the surface of the material, and the fraction escaping is large when the lump is small because the surface-to-volume ratio is large; below the critical mass, too many neutrons are lost for the reaction to sustain itself. (A1) Second, and more importantly, the neutrons emerge from fission moving fast, with energies of the order of $1\ \mathrm{MeV}$, and the fission cross-section of uranium-235 is very small at those energies — a fast neutron is far more likely to be scattered or to be captured without causing fission than to induce one. (A1) A moderator, such as water or graphite, surrounds the fuel and slows the neutrons by elastic collisions with light nuclei until they reach thermal energies of a fraction of an electronvolt, at which the fission cross-section is hundreds of times larger and the chain reaction proceeds. (A1) Control rods, made of a material that absorbs neutrons strongly, are then used to remove exactly the surplus above the one neutron per fission needed to sustain the reaction, so that on average exactly one neutron from each fission causes another.

Markscheme notes

(a) Accept $3.2 \times 10^{-11}\ \mathrm{J}$ and $9.3$ to $9.5 \times 10^{19}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$. The efficiency conversion is the point of the part: a candidate who divides $1.0\ \mathrm{GW}$ by the fission energy without correcting for efficiency has answered a different question and should lose the second mark. (b) Accept $3.1$ to $3.3\ \mathrm{kg}$ and $8.5$ to $8.8 \times 10^{6}\ \mathrm{kg}$. Note that the mass consumed is the mass actually fissioned, not the mass loaded into the core, which is considerably larger. (c) Accept $450$ to $470\ \mathrm{kg}$; the tails comment is a bonus and should be credited if offered, but candidates are not expected to know the term. (d) Four distinct points are available: the possibility of a chain reaction because more than one neutron is released, the escape of neutrons through the surface and the existence of a critical mass, the low fission cross-section for fast neutrons, and the action of the moderator. Candidates who mention the moderator but not the cross-section have given the mechanism without the reason and should not receive full marks; the word cross-section need not be used, but the idea that fast neutrons do not readily cause fission must be present.

46. A.5 Galilean and special relativity

[15 marks]PHYS-A.5-001

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.5 Galilean and special relativity (AHL): time dilation, proper time, length contraction · difficulty 4

Work in units of years and light years, so that $c=1\ \mathrm{ly\,yr^{-1}}$ and $v=\beta c$ with $\beta=v/c$.

(a) Earth-frame journey time: $t=\dfrac{8.0}{\beta}$ years. (A1)

The astronauts measure the proper time (both events — departure and arrival — occur at the same place in the spacecraft), so

$$\tau=\frac{t}{\gamma}=\frac{8.0}{\beta}\sqrt{1-\beta^{2}}=3.0$$ (M1)(A1)

Squaring: $\dfrac{64(1-\beta^{2})}{\beta^{2}}=9 \Rightarrow 64-64\beta^{2}=9\beta^{2} \Rightarrow \beta^{2}=\dfrac{64}{73}$ (M1)

$$\beta=\frac{8}{\sqrt{73}}=0.9363,\qquad \gamma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{64}{73}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{9/73}}=\frac{\sqrt{73}}{3}=2.85$$ (A1)(A1)

$$v=0.936c\ \ (\approx2.81\times10^{8}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}).$$

Limiting-case check: for a subluminal speed the Lorentz factor must be real and greater than $1$. Since $\beta^{2}=64/73<1$, the speed satisfies $v<c$, and $\gamma=\sqrt{73}/3=2.85>1$; both conditions hold, so the algebra is self-consistent. (R1)

(b) The Earth–star distance is the proper length (the two endpoints are at rest in the Earth frame), so it is contracted in the spacecraft frame:

$$L=\frac{L_{0}}{\gamma}=\frac{8.0}{2.848}=2.81\ \mathrm{ly}$$ (M1)(A1)(A1)

(c) Earth-frame journey time: $t=\dfrac{8.0}{0.9363}=8.54\ \mathrm{yr}$. (A1)

The message travels $8.0$ ly at speed $c$, taking a further $8.0$ yr. (A1)

$$\text{Total}=8.54+8.0=16.5\ \mathrm{yr}$$ (A1)

(d) In the spacecraft frame the distance to be covered is not $8.0$ ly but the contracted distance $2.81$ ly from (b). (A1) The star approaches at $0.936c$, so the journey takes

$$\frac{2.81\ \mathrm{ly}}{0.936\ \mathrm{ly\,yr^{-1}}}=3.0\ \mathrm{yr},$$

which is exactly the astronauts' measured time. (A1)

There is no contradiction: distance and time intervals are frame-dependent, but the relative speed between spacecraft and star is $0.936c$ in *both* frames, so nothing exceeds $c$. The Earth observer says the trip took $8.54$ yr of Earth time; the astronauts say it took $3.0$ yr of their own time and that the distance was shorter. Both are correct. (A1)

A more formal statement of the same point is the invariance of the spacetime interval, $c^{2}\Delta t^{2}-\Delta x^{2}$. Evaluated in the Earth frame with the exact journey time $t=\sqrt{73}\ \mathrm{yr}$, it is $73-8.0^{2}=9$ (in $\mathrm{ly^{2}}$); in the spacecraft frame the two events occur at the same place, so it is $3.0^{2}-0=9$. The frames disagree about $\Delta t$ and $\Delta x$ separately but agree on the interval, which is the proper time, $3.0$ yr. (R1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The proper-time identification is essential — award (M1) only if $\tau=t/\gamma$ is used (i.e. the spacecraft time is the *shorter* one). Candidates who write $t=\gamma\tau$ with $t=8.0/\beta$ inverted forfeit the method. Accept $\gamma=2.85$ and $v=0.936c$; allow $0.94c$ at 2 s.f.

(b) Watch for the direction of the contraction: the proper length is the Earth-frame length, so the spacecraft length is *smaller*. Award at most one mark for $L=\gamma L_{0}=22.8$ ly.

(c) A common omission is the light-travel time of the message. Award the first two marks even if the candidate then forgets to add them.

(d) The mark scheme is looking for two ideas: the contracted distance from (b), and the fact that the relative speed is below $c$ in both frames. 'Time passes more slowly for the astronauts' on its own is not an explanation of the distance and earns nothing.

(a) Alternative method: eliminate $\gamma$ first by writing $t=\gamma\tau$ with $t=8.0/\beta$, then solve the resulting equation in $\beta$; award full marks provided the proper time is identified. Accept $\gamma$ in the range 2.83–2.87 and $\beta$ in 0.93–0.94. Carry at least three significant figures through the working; a final answer rounded to 2 s.f. ($0.94c$) is acceptable.

47. C.5 Doppler effect

[15 marks]PHYS-C.5-001

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.5 Doppler effect (AHL): moving source, moving observer, reflection · difficulty 4

(a) The source (bat) moves towards a stationary observer (the wall):

$$f'=f\frac{v}{v-u}=40.0\times10^{3}\times\frac{340}{340-6.0}$$ (M1)(A1)

$$=40.0\times10^{3}\times\frac{340}{334}=40.0\times10^{3}(1.01796)=40.7\ \mathrm{kHz}$$ (A1)

(more precisely $40\,719\ \mathrm{Hz}$)

As a check, the shift is small and positive: $u/v=0.0176$, so a $1.8\%$ increase in frequency is physically reasonable. (R1)

(b) The wall reflects the sound; it behaves as a stationary source emitting frequency $f'$. The bat is now a moving observer approaching that source:

$$f''=f'\frac{v+u}{v}=40\,719\times\frac{340+6.0}{340}=40\,719\times1.01765$$ (M1)(A1)

$$=41\,437\ \mathrm{Hz}=41.4\ \mathrm{kHz}$$ (A1)(A1)

Cross-check: the two successive factors multiply, $1.01796\times1.01765=1.03593$, which equals $346/334$ directly, confirming that the shifts compose multiplicatively. (R1)

(c) Combining the two shifts:

$$f''=\left(f\frac{v}{v-u}\right)\left(\frac{v+u}{v}\right)=f\frac{v+u}{v-u}$$ (M1)(A1)(A1)(A1)

— the $v$ factors cancel, which is why the two shifts multiply rather than add.

(d) Exact difference: $f''-f=41\,437-40\,000=1437\ \mathrm{Hz}\approx1.44\ \mathrm{kHz}$. (A1)

For $u\ll v$:

$$\frac{v+u}{v-u}=\frac{1+u/v}{1-u/v}=\left(1+\frac{u}{v}\right)\left(1-\frac{u}{v}\right)^{-1}\approx\left(1+\frac{u}{v}\right)\left(1+\frac{u}{v}\right)$$ (M1)

using the binomial expansion $(1-x)^{-1}\approx1+x$ for small $x$. Hence

$$f''\approx f\left(1+\frac{u}{v}\right)^{2}\approx f\left(1+\frac{2u}{v}\right),$$

neglecting the $(u/v)^{2}$ term, so

$$f''-f\approx\frac{2fu}{v}.$$ (A1)

Numerically: $\dfrac{2fu}{v}=\dfrac{2(40\,000)(6.0)}{340}=1412\ \mathrm{Hz}$. (A1)

Compared with the exact $1437\ \mathrm{Hz}$, the approximation is low by about $1.7\%$ — reasonable, since $u/v=0.0176$ and the neglected term is of order $(u/v)^{2}\approx3\times10^{-4}$ relative to the leading correction, i.e. roughly $1.8\%$ of the shift. The approximation is excellent for $u/v<0.01$.

The approximation is the first two terms of the expansion $\left(1+\frac{u}{v}\right)^{2}=1+\frac{2u}{v}+\left(\frac{u}{v}\right)^{2}+\dots$, so the leading neglected term is $(u/v)^{2}$ relative to unity — about $1.8\%$ of the $2u/v$ shift, as quoted. In the limit $u\to v$ the exact ratio $(v+u)/(v-u)$ diverges while the linear form $1+2u/v$ stays finite, which shows the approximation must not be used at high speed. (R1)

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (M1) for selecting the moving-source formula $f'=fv/(v-u)$. Candidates who use the moving-observer formula here forfeit the method mark. Accept 40.7 kHz.

(b) The crucial idea is that the reflection makes the wall a *source* of frequency $f'$ (not $f$). Award (M1) for that step, (A1) for the moving-observer formula, (A1) for substitution, (A1) for 41.4 kHz. Candidates who apply the shift a second time to $f$ instead of $f'$ get $43.4$ kHz and forfeit everything after the first mark.

(c) Award full marks for the cancellation argument. A candidate who writes 'the shifts add: $f''=f(1+2u/v)$' as an identity has not shown the exact result and earns at most one mark.

(d) Accept 1437–1440 Hz for the exact value and 1410–1415 Hz for the approximation. The comment must be quantitative (a percentage or a comparison of the two numbers); 'it is quite accurate' alone is not creditworthy.

(b) Alternative method: quote the combined result $f''=f(v+u)/(v-u)$ from (c) and substitute directly; award full marks even if the two stages are not shown separately. Follow through from (a): a candidate with an incorrect $f'$ who nevertheless applies the correct moving-observer factor keeps the later marks. Accept 41.3–41.5 kHz, and allow three or four significant figures throughout.

48. A.1 Range of a projectile on an inclined plane: the dependence on alpha and beta is through 2alpha - beta

[15 marks]PHYS-A.1-103

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.1 Kinematics (projectile motion, resolution of vectors) · difficulty 4

(a) Resolving the initial velocity: parallel to the plane $u\cos(\alpha - \beta)$, perpendicular to the plane $u\sin(\alpha - \beta)$ (A1). Resolving the acceleration due to gravity: parallel to the plane $g\sin\beta$ down the slope, perpendicular to the plane $g\cos\beta$ into the plane (A1). The perpendicular displacement returns to zero at landing, so with $s = u_\perp T - \tfrac{1}{2}a_\perp T^2$ (M1):
$$u\sin(\alpha - \beta)\,T - \tfrac{1}{2}g\cos\beta\,T^2 = 0,$$
and rejecting $T = 0$ gives $T = \dfrac{2u\sin(\alpha - \beta)}{g\cos\beta}$ (AG).

(b) Along the plane, $R = u\cos(\alpha - \beta)\,T - \tfrac{1}{2}g\sin\beta\,T^2$ (M1). Substituting $T$ from part (a) and writing the first term over the common denominator $g\cos^2\beta$ (M1):
$$R = \frac{u^2}{g\cos^2\beta}\left[2\sin(\alpha - \beta)\cos(\alpha - \beta)\cos\beta - 2\sin\beta\sin^2(\alpha - \beta)\right].$$
Using $2\sin\theta\cos\theta = \sin 2\theta$ and $2\sin^2\theta = 1 - \cos 2\theta$ with $\theta = \alpha - \beta$ (M1):
$$R = \frac{u^2}{g\cos^2\beta}\left[\sin(2\alpha - 2\beta)\cos\beta - \sin\beta\left(1 - \cos(2\alpha - 2\beta)\right)\right]$$
$$= \frac{u^2}{g\cos^2\beta}\left[\sin(2\alpha - 2\beta)\cos\beta + \sin\beta\cos(2\alpha - 2\beta) - \sin\beta\right].$$
The first two terms are the expansion of $\sin\left((2\alpha - 2\beta) + \beta\right) = \sin(2\alpha - \beta)$ (A1), giving the required result.

(c) The factor $\dfrac{u^2}{g\cos^2\beta}$ and the term $-\sin\beta$ are both independent of $\alpha$, so $R$ is a maximum when $\sin(2\alpha - \beta)$ is a maximum (M1), that is when $\sin(2\alpha - \beta) = 1$ and $2\alpha - \beta = 90^\circ$ (A1). Hence
$$\alpha = 45^\circ + \frac{\beta}{2}.$$
Then $R_{\max} = \dfrac{u^2}{g\cos^2\beta}(1 - \sin\beta)$ (M1), and since $\cos^2\beta = 1 - \sin^2\beta = (1 - \sin\beta)(1 + \sin\beta)$,
$$R_{\max} = \frac{u^2(1 - \sin\beta)}{g(1 - \sin\beta)(1 + \sin\beta)} = \frac{u^2}{g(1 + \sin\beta)} \quad (\mathrm{AG}).$$
As a check, $\beta = 0$ gives $\alpha = 45^\circ$ and $R_{\max} = \dfrac{u^2}{g}$, the familiar flat-ground results.

(d) $\alpha = 45^\circ + \dfrac{20.0^\circ}{2} = 55.0^\circ$ (A1). Then
$$R_{\max} = \frac{30.0^2}{9.81(1 + \sin 20.0^\circ)} = \frac{900}{9.81 \times 1.3420} = 68.4\,\mathrm{m} \quad (\mathrm{A1}).$$
With $\alpha = 45^\circ$ instead, $\sin(2\alpha - \beta) = \sin(90^\circ - 20^\circ) = \sin 70^\circ$, so (M1)
$$R = \frac{900}{9.81\cos^2 20^\circ}\left[\sin 70^\circ - \sin 20^\circ\right] = \frac{900}{8.662}(0.9397 - 0.3420) = 62.1\,\mathrm{m} \quad (\mathrm{A1}),$$
which falls short of the maximum by $68.4 - 62.1 = 6.3\,\mathrm{m}$, about $9\,\%$ of the maximum range (A1). The student's rule is therefore correct only on level ground.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (A1) for each correct resolution, (M1) for applying s = ut - (1/2)at^2 perpendicular to the plane and setting s = 0, and the final mark is (AG) -- the answer is given, so the working must be shown in full and no credit is available for merely stating the result.
(b) Award (M1) for writing the displacement along the plane, (M1) for substituting T and forming a common denominator, (M1) for applying the double-angle identities, (A1) for recognising the sine addition expansion. Candidates may reach the result by a different algebraic route; award marks for equivalent correct steps.
(c) Award (M1) for observing that only sin(2*alpha - beta) depends on alpha, (A1) for 2*alpha - beta = 90 degrees and alpha = 45 + beta/2, (M1) for substituting back, (A1) for the factorisation of cos^2(beta). Allow the check beta = 0 as a substitute for the final (A1) if the candidate states both flat-ground results correctly.
(d) Award (A1) for alpha = 55.0 degrees, (A1) for 68.4 m (accept 68.3-68.5; units required), (M1) for evaluating sin(70) - sin(20) in the correct formula, (A1) for 62.1 m, (A1) for the shortfall of 6.3 m. Follow-through applies: a candidate using an incorrect but stated value of alpha from part (c) may still earn the (M1) and the final two (A1) marks on their own value.

49. A.4 An Atwood machine with a massive pulley: the effective inertia is m1 + m2 + I/R^2

[15 marks]PHYS-A.4-104

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.4 Rigid body mechanics (moment of inertia, Newton's second law for rotation) · difficulty 4

(a) The pulley has moment of inertia, so a resultant torque is needed to give it an angular acceleration (A1). If the tensions were equal, the torques they exert about the axle would cancel exactly and the pulley could not accelerate, while the blocks would be accelerating -- which would mean the string was slipping (A1). Hence $T_1 > T_2$, and with $\tau = I\alpha$ and $a = \alpha R$:
$$ (T_1 - T_2)R = I\alpha = \frac{Ia}{R} \quad (\mathrm{A1}). $$

(b) For $m_1$ moving downwards: $m_1g - T_1 = m_1a$ (A1). For $m_2$ moving upwards: $T_2 - m_2g = m_2a$ (A1). Adding these and using the rotational equation from (a) divided by $R$, $T_1 - T_2 = \dfrac{Ia}{R^2}$ (M1):
$$m_1g - m_2g - (T_1 - T_2) = (m_1 + m_2)a \quad \Rightarrow \quad (m_1 - m_2)g - \frac{Ia}{R^2} = (m_1 + m_2)a,$$
so $(m_1 - m_2)g = a\left(m_1 + m_2 + \dfrac{I}{R^2}\right)$ and the result follows (AG).

(c) First $\dfrac{I}{R^2} = \dfrac{0.0180}{0.120^2} = \dfrac{0.0180}{0.0144} = 1.25\,\mathrm{kg}$ (A1), so the effective inertia is $4.00 + 2.00 + 1.25 = 7.25\,\mathrm{kg}$. Hence
$$a = \frac{(4.00 - 2.00)(9.81)}{7.25} = \frac{19.62}{7.25} = 2.71\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} \quad (\mathrm{A1}).$$
From the linear equations: $T_1 = m_1(g - a) = 4.00(9.81 - 2.7062) = 28.4\,\mathrm{N}$ (A1) and $T_2 = m_2(g + a) = 2.00(9.81 + 2.7062) = 25.0\,\mathrm{N}$ (A1).
Verification: $T_1 - T_2 = 28.415 - 25.032 = 3.383\,\mathrm{N}$, so the resultant torque is $3.383 \times 0.120 = 0.4059\,\mathrm{N\,m}$. Also $\alpha = \dfrac{a}{R} = \dfrac{2.7062}{0.120} = 22.55\,\mathrm{rad\,s^{-2}}$ and $I\alpha = 0.0180 \times 22.55 = 0.4059\,\mathrm{N\,m}$, which agrees (A1).

(d) With a massless pulley, $a_0 = \dfrac{(m_1 - m_2)g}{m_1 + m_2} = \dfrac{19.62}{6.00} = 3.27\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ (A1). Half of this is $1.635\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$, so (M1)
$$\frac{19.62}{6.00 + \frac{I}{R^2}} = 1.635 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 6.00 + \frac{I}{R^2} = \frac{19.62}{1.635} = 12.0,$$
giving the equivalent mass $\dfrac{I}{R^2} = 6.00\,\mathrm{kg}$ (A1) and hence
$$I = 6.00 \times 0.120^2 = 6.00 \times 0.0144 = 0.0864\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2} \quad (\mathrm{A1}).$$
This is $4.8$ times the original moment of inertia, which is why the acceleration falls by only a factor of two: doubling the effective inertia from $6.00$ to $12.0\,\mathrm{kg}$ is exactly what halving the acceleration requires.

Markscheme notes

(a) Award (A1) for stating that a resultant torque is needed to accelerate the pulley, (A1) for a correct consequence -- either that equal tensions would give zero resultant torque, or that equal tensions would imply slipping -- and (A1) for the rotational equation in any correct form, including (T1 - T2)R = I*alpha with a = alpha*R stated separately.
(b) Award (A1) for each correct linear equation of motion with signs consistent with the stated directions, (M1) for combining all three equations and eliminating T1 and T2, and the final mark is (AG): the result is given, so the elimination must be shown. Candidates who add the two linear equations but neglect the rotational term earn (A1)(A1) only.
(c) Award (A1) for 1.25 kg, (A1) for a = 2.71 m s^-2 (accept 2.70-2.71; units required), (A1) for each tension (accept 28.3-28.5 and 25.0-25.1), (A1) for the check. The check must compare two torques, not merely restate one of the equations used.
(d) Award (A1) for a0 = 3.27 m s^-2, (M1) for setting up the equation with a0/2, (A1) for I/R^2 = 6.00 kg, (A1) for I = 0.0864 kg m^2. Follow-through applies from an incorrect but stated value of a0; award the last three marks on the candidate's own value.

50. C.1 Which oscillators feel an accelerating reference frame and which do not

[15 marks]PHYS-C.1-103

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.1 Simple harmonic motion (the defining equation; the period is independent of amplitude); A.2 Forces and momentum · difficulty 4

(a) In the non-inertial frame of the lift there is a fictitious force $ma$ downwards on
every mass $m$ (M1), in addition to the weight $mg$ downwards. The total downward force is

$$F = mg + ma = m(g + a) \tag{A1}$$

so the effective gravitational field strength is $g_{\text{eff}} = g + a = 9.81 + 2.0
= 11.81\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ (A1). Equivalently, an object released in the lift falls with
acceleration $g + a$ relative to the lift.

(b) For a simple pendulum the restoring force is the component of the effective weight along
the arc, so the standard result holds with $g$ replaced by $g + a$ (M1):

$$T = 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{L}{g + a}} \tag{A1}$$

$$T = 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{0.80}{11.81}} = 2\pi\sqrt{0.06774} = 2\pi(0.2603) = 1.64\ \mathrm{s}
\tag{A1}$$

For comparison, at rest the same pendulum has $T_{0} = 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{0.80}{9.81}} = 1.79\
\mathrm{s}$, so the acceleration shortens the period by about $9\%$ (A1). The amplitude does
not enter, as always for small oscillations.

(c) For a mass on a spring the restoring force is $-k$ times the extension, which has nothing
to do with gravity (M1). At equilibrium in the lift the spring is stretched by

$$x_{0} = \frac{m(g + a)}{k} = \frac{0.50 \times 11.81}{200} = 0.0295\ \mathrm{m} \tag{A1}$$

against $x_{0} = \frac{0.50 \times 9.81}{200} = 0.0245\ \mathrm{m}$ at rest, so the
acceleration shifts the equilibrium position downwards by $5.0\ \mathrm{mm}$ and nothing else.
Displacing the mass by a further $y$ from that new equilibrium gives the net force

$$F = -k(x_{0} + y) + m(g + a) = -ky \tag{A1}$$

since $kx_{0} = m(g + a)$ by definition of the equilibrium position. This is SHM with
$\omega = \sqrt{\frac{k}{m}}$, so

$$T = 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{m}{k}} = 2\pi\sqrt{\frac{0.50}{200}} = 2\pi(0.0500) = 0.314\ \mathrm{s}
\tag{A1}$$

exactly as at rest. The acceleration has dropped out entirely, because it is a constant force
and a constant force can only move the centre of oscillation, not change the curvature of the
potential about that centre.

(d) Rearranging the result of (b),

$$g + a = \frac{4\pi^{2}L}{T^{2}} = \frac{4\pi^{2} \times 0.80}{1.600^{2}}
= \frac{31.58}{2.560} = 12.34\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} \tag{M1}$$

$$a = 12.34 - 9.81 = 2.53\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} \tag{A1}$$

Only the pendulum measurement is of any use here (A1): the period of the mass–spring system is
$0.314\ \mathrm{s}$ whatever the lift is doing, so it carries no information about $a$ at all.
A candidate who tries to extract $a$ from the spring period will find the algebra returns no
solution, which is the correct outcome (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for introducing the fictitious force, or equivalently for arguing in the inertial
frame that the mass must accelerate with the lift so the tension exceeds the weight by $ma$.
(A1) for $F = m(g+a)$. (A1) for the numerical value $11.81\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. Allow
$g_{\text{eff}} = 11.8\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$.

(b) (M1) for stating that $g$ is replaced by $g + a$ — this must be justified by a reference
to the restoring force, not asserted. (A1) for the correct formula. (A1) for
$1.64\ \mathrm{s}$; accept $1.635\ \mathrm{s}$. (A1) for the comparison with the value at
rest; accept any period in $[1.79, 1.80]\ \mathrm{s}$ for the at-rest value.

(c) (M1) for identifying the spring force, not gravity, as the restoring force. (A1) for the
equilibrium extension. (A1) for showing the force reduces to $-ky$ about the new equilibrium —
this is the key step and should not be credited to a candidate who merely asserts that "the
period of a spring does not depend on $g$". (A1) for $0.314\ \mathrm{s}$.

Forfeiture: a candidate who substitutes $g + a$ into $\sqrt{\frac{m}{k}}$ in any form has
misunderstood the physics and earns at most the (M1).

(d) (M1) for rearranging to make $g + a$ or $a$ the subject. (A1) for
$2.53\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$; accept $[2.52, 2.54]$. (A1) for stating that only the pendulum
helps. (A1) for the consequence that the spring period yields no information. Condonation:
allow a candidate who reports $a = 2.5\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ from $g_{\text{eff}} = 12.3\
\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ without further figures.

51. A.3 Friction on an incline depends on the normal reaction, so the two surfaces are not equivalent

[15 marks]PHYS-A.3-103

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.3 Work, energy and power (conservation of energy; work done by a frictional force) · difficulty 4

(a) Resolving perpendicular to the slope, the normal reaction is
$N = mg\cos\theta$ (M1). The friction is $f = \mu N = \mu mg\cos\theta$ (A1), so resolving
down the slope,

$$ma = mg\sin\theta - \mu mg\cos\theta \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
a = g(\sin\theta - \mu\cos\theta) \tag{A1}$$

$$a = 9.81(\sin 30^\circ - 0.20\cos 30^\circ) = 9.81(0.500 - 0.173) = 9.81(0.327)
= 3.21\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$$

Note that the mass cancels, as it must.

(b) With $u = 0$ and constant acceleration over $s = 4.0\ \mathrm{m}$ (M1),

$$v^{2} = u^{2} + 2as = 0 + 2(3.206)(4.0) = 25.65\ \mathrm{m^{2}\,s^{-2}} \tag{A1}$$

$$v = 5.06\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} \tag{A1}$$

Equivalently by energy: the loss of gravitational potential energy is
$mgh = mg s\sin\theta = 2.0 \times 9.81 \times 4.0 \times 0.5 = 39.2\ \mathrm{J}$ and the work
against friction on the slope is $\mu mg\cos\theta \times s = 13.6\ \mathrm{J}$, leaving
$25.6\ \mathrm{J}$ of kinetic energy, which gives the same speed (A1).

(c) On the horizontal surface the normal reaction is $N = mg$ (M1), so the retardation is

$$a' = \frac{\mu mg}{m} = \mu g = 0.20 \times 9.81 = 1.962\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} \tag{A1}$$

and, with final speed zero,

$$d = \frac{v^{2}}{2a'} = \frac{25.65}{2 \times 1.962} = \frac{25.65}{3.924}
= 6.54\ \mathrm{m} \tag{A1}$$

Alternatively, the remaining $25.6\ \mathrm{J}$ is dissipated over $d$ at
$\mu mg = 3.92\ \mathrm{N}$, giving the same result (A1).

(d) The expression $\mu mg(s + d)$ assumes the normal reaction is $mg$ over the whole path
(M1). It is not: on the slope the reaction is $mg\cos\theta$ (R1), so the friction on the
slope is smaller than $\mu mg$ by a factor of $\cos 30^\circ = 0.866$. The correct total is
the sum of the work on each surface (M1):

$$W = \mu mg\cos\theta \cdot s + \mu mg \cdot d
= (0.20)(2.0)(9.81)(0.866)(4.0) + (0.20)(2.0)(9.81)(6.54) \tag{A1}$$

$$= 13.6 + 25.6 = 39.2\ \mathrm{J}$$

which equals the loss of gravitational potential energy $mgh = 39.2\ \mathrm{J}$, as it must
(A1). The student's expression gives $3.924 \times (4.0 + 6.54) = 41.3\ \mathrm{J}$, which
exceeds the $39.2\ \mathrm{J}$ of energy available — a result that is impossible, and a useful
check to perform before accepting any answer of this kind. Working backwards from the
student's expression would give a total distance of $10.0\ \mathrm{m}$ and hence
$d = 6.0\ \mathrm{m}$, about $8\%$ short.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for the normal reaction $mg\cos\theta$. (A1) for $f = \mu mg\cos\theta$.
(A1) for the numerical value; accept $3.2\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$. Do not credit a candidate who
writes the friction as $\mu mg$ on the slope: this is the error the item is built around and it
loses both of the first two marks.

(b) (M1) for $v^{2} = 2as$. (A1) for $v^{2} = 25.65\ \mathrm{m^{2}\,s^{-2}}$. (A1) for
$5.06\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (A1) for the energy cross-check. Follow-through from an incorrect
acceleration in (a) is allowed.

(c) (M1) for $N = mg$ on the horizontal. (A1) for $\mu g$. (A1) for $6.54\ \mathrm{m}$; accept
$[6.5, 6.6]$. (A1) for the energy route.

(d) (M1) for identifying the assumption about the normal reaction. (R1) for stating that the
reaction on the slope is $mg\cos\theta$. (M1) for summing the work over the two surfaces
separately. (A1) for $39.2\ \mathrm{J}$. (A1) for noting the agreement with $mgh$, or for
noting that the student's value exceeds the available energy.

Forfeiture: a candidate who states only "the distances are different" earns nothing — the
normal reaction, not the distance, is the issue. Condonation: allow the use of
$\cos 30^\circ = 0.87$.

52. D.1 Total energy of a satellite in circular orbit, and why raising an orbit slows the satellite down

[15 marks]PHYS-D.1-102

Theme D: Fields · D.1 Gravitational fields (gravitational potential energy; escape speed; orbital motion) · difficulty 4

(a) For a circular orbit the gravitational force supplies the centripetal force (M1):

$$\frac{GMm}{r^{2}} = \frac{mv^{2}}{r} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad v^{2} = \frac{GM}{r} \tag{A1}$$

Hence

$$E_{K} = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2} = \frac{1}{2}m\frac{GM}{r} = \frac{GMm}{2r} \tag{AG}$$

(b) Taking gravitational potential energy as zero at infinity, the potential energy at radius
$r$ is $E_{P} = -\frac{GMm}{r}$ (M1). Adding the kinetic energy from (a) (M1),

$$E = E_{K} + E_{P} = \frac{GMm}{2r} - \frac{GMm}{r} = -\frac{GMm}{2r} \tag{A1}$$

Numerically, $GM = 6.67 \times 10^{-11} \times 5.97 \times 10^{24} = 3.98 \times 10^{14}\
\mathrm{m^{3}\,s^{-2}}$, so (A1)

$$E = -\frac{3.98 \times 10^{14} \times 800}{2 \times 7.0 \times 10^{6}}
= -\frac{3.19 \times 10^{17}}{1.4 \times 10^{7}} = -2.28 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J} \tag{A1}$$

The total energy is negative, which is what "bound" means: the satellite does not have enough
energy to escape. Note also that $E = -E_{K}$, a relation that holds for every circular orbit
and is often the fastest way to check a result of this kind.

(c) At radius $2r$ the total energy is

$$E' = -\frac{GMm}{2(2r)} = -\frac{GMm}{4r} = \frac{E}{2} = -1.14 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}
\tag{M1}$$

so the energy that must be supplied is (A1)

$$\Delta E = E' - E = -\frac{GMm}{4r} + \frac{GMm}{2r} = \frac{GMm}{4r}
= 1.14 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J} \tag{A1}$$

Note what this is *not*. Using $mg\,\Delta h$ with $g = 9.81\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ and
$\Delta h = r = 7.0 \times 10^{6}\ \mathrm{m}$ gives
$800 \times 9.81 \times 7.0 \times 10^{6} = 5.49 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$, nearly five times
too large, because $g$ is not constant over a distance comparable with the radius of the Earth.

(d) From (a), $v = \sqrt{\frac{GM}{r}}$ (M1), so

$$\frac{v_{2r}}{v_{r}} = \sqrt{\frac{GM/(2r)}{GM/r}} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{2}} = 0.707 \tag{A1}$$

The speed in the larger orbit is about $71\%$ of the speed in the smaller one, so the satellite
is unambiguously moving more slowly (A1). The kinetic energy correspondingly halves, from
$2.28 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$ to $1.14 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$, while the total energy
rises from $-2.28 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$ to $-1.14 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$. There is no
contradiction: the energy supplied has gone into gravitational potential energy, which
increases by $2.28 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$, while the kinetic energy falls by
$1.14 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$ (A1). Roughly, half the energy supplied raises the orbit and
the other half pays for the satellite's lost kinetic energy.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for equating the gravitational force to the centripetal force. (A1) for
$v^{2} = \frac{GM}{r}$. (AG) for the final expression. No mark is available for quoting
$E_{K} = \frac{GMm}{2r}$ from memory.

(b) (M1) for the potential energy $-\frac{GMm}{r}$ with the zero at infinity. (M1) for adding
the two contributions. (A1) for the combined expression. (A1) for $GM = 3.98 \times 10^{14}$.
(A1) for $-2.28 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$; accept $[-2.3, -2.27] \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$.
The negative sign is essential and its omission loses the final (A1).

(c) (M1) for $E' = -\frac{GMm}{4r}$. (A1) for forming the difference. (A1) for
$1.14 \times 10^{10}\ \mathrm{J}$. A candidate who computes $mg\,\Delta h$ here earns nothing;
if they also comment that the result exceeds the magnitude of the binding energy, award a
single mark for the observation.

(d) (M1) for $v = \sqrt{\frac{GM}{r}}$. (A1) for the ratio $0.707$. (A1) for concluding that
the speed is lower. (A1) for reconciling the fall in kinetic energy with the rise in total
energy — this is the conceptual mark and a bare "the ratio is $0.707$" does not earn it.

Condonation: allow the ratio to be given as $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$ without a decimal.

53. Two cells in parallel, one of them being charged

[15 marks]PHYS-B.5-001

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.5 Current and circuits: emf and internal resistance, epsilon = I(R + r), combinations of resistors in series and parallel, electrical power P = IV = I^2 R = V^2/R · difficulty 4

(a) Let $V$ be the potential difference between A and B. In branch 1 the current is $I_{1}=\dfrac{\varepsilon_{1}-V}{r_{1}}$ and in branch 2 it is $I_{2}=\dfrac{\varepsilon_{2}-V}{r_{2}}$, while the current in R is $I=\dfrac{V}{R}$. (M1)

Current is conserved at junction A, so $I_{1}+I_{2}=I$: (M1)

$$\frac{12.0-V}{0.40}+\frac{9.0-V}{0.60}=\frac{V}{2.0}$$

Multiplying throughout by $1.2$: $3(12.0-V)+2(9.0-V)=0.6V$. (M1)

So $36.0-3V+18.0-2V=0.6V$, giving $54.0=5.6V$ and $V=9.64\ \mathrm{V}$. (AG)

(b) $I_{1}=\dfrac{12.0-9.643}{0.40}=5.89\ \mathrm{A}$ (M1)(A1), flowing from the positive terminal of cell 1 through junction A and out into the rest of the circuit.

$I_{2}=\dfrac{9.0-9.643}{0.60}=-1.07\ \mathrm{A}$ (M1), so a current of $1.07\ \mathrm{A}$ flows into the positive terminal of cell 2: cell 1 is charging cell 2. (A1)

The current in R is $I=\dfrac{9.643}{2.0}=4.82\ \mathrm{A}$. (A1)

Check: $5.893-1.071=4.821\ \mathrm{A}$, consistent with current conservation.

(c) Power delivered by cell 1: $\varepsilon_{1}I_{1}=12.0\times 5.893=70.7\ \mathrm{W}$. (M1)

Power dissipated in the two internal resistances: $I_{1}^{2}r_{1}=5.893^{2}\times 0.40=13.9\ \mathrm{W}$ and $I_{2}^{2}r_{2}=1.071^{2}\times 0.60=0.689\ \mathrm{W}$. (M1)

Power dissipated in R: $\dfrac{V^{2}}{R}=\dfrac{9.643^{2}}{2.0}=46.5\ \mathrm{W}$. (M1)

Power absorbed by cell 2, which is being charged: $\varepsilon_{2}I_{2}=9.0\times 1.071=9.64\ \mathrm{W}$.

Total $=13.89+0.689+46.49+9.64=70.7\ \mathrm{W}$, which equals the power delivered by cell 1. (A1)

(d) No current in branch 2 requires $V=\varepsilon_{2}$, since branch 2 then has no potential difference across its internal resistance. (M1)

With $I_{2}=0$ the current in branch 1 equals the current in R: $\dfrac{12.0-V}{0.40}=\dfrac{V}{2.0}$, so $2(12.0-V)=0.40V$ and $24.0=2.4V$. (M1)

Hence $V=10.0\ \mathrm{V}$ and $\varepsilon_{2}=10.0\ \mathrm{V}$. (A1)

Markscheme notes

Part (a): the model must include the internal resistances in series with each cell; a candidate who treats the cells as ideal earns at most the second (M1). Accept any consistent choice of sign convention for the current directions, provided it is stated. Part (b): a negative value of $I_{2}$ is the correct outcome and must not be treated as an error; award the (A1) for identifying that cell 2 is being charged. A candidate who reverses the direction convention throughout and reports $I_{2}=+1.07\ \mathrm{A}$ into the cell earns full marks if the direction is clearly stated. Part (c): the four power terms are each worth (M1), with the final (A1) for demonstrating equality. Follow-through applies from (a) and (b) — if the candidate's currents are consistent with their $V$, the arithmetic will still balance and should be credited. A candidate who omits the power absorbed by cell 2 cannot balance the audit and should not be given the (A1). Part (d): accept a solution by inspection that recognises the no-current condition requires the terminal p.d. of cell 2 to equal its emf.

54. A rod on rails that finds its own terminal speed

[15 marks]PHYS-D.4-001

Theme D: Fields · D.4 Induction (AHL): magnetic flux, Faraday's law, emf induced in a straight conductor moving perpendicular to a field as epsilon = BvL, Lenz's law as a consequence of energy conservation · difficulty 4

(a) As the rod accelerates, the emf induced across it is $\varepsilon=BvL$ and the current around the circuit is $I=\dfrac{\varepsilon}{R}=\dfrac{BvL}{R}$. The magnetic force opposing the motion is $F=BIL=\dfrac{B^{2}L^{2}v}{R}$. (M1)(M1)

For the rod and hanging mass moving together, the resultant driving force is $m_{2}g-F$, so $m_{2}g-\dfrac{B^{2}L^{2}v}{R}=(m+m_{2})a$. (M1)

As $v$ increases the opposing force grows, so $a$ falls; when $m_{2}g=\dfrac{B^{2}L^{2}v}{R}$ the acceleration is zero and the speed stops changing. (A1)

Hence $v=\dfrac{m_{2}gR}{B^{2}L^{2}}$. (A1)

Numerically $v=\dfrac{0.050\times 9.81\times 0.80}{(0.45)^{2}(0.60)^{2}}=\dfrac{0.3924}{0.0729}=5.38\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (A1)

(b) At terminal speed the current is $I=\dfrac{BvL}{R}=\dfrac{0.45\times 5.383\times 0.60}{0.80}=1.82\ \mathrm{A}$. (M1)

Power dissipated in the resistor: $I^{2}R=(1.8167)^{2}\times 0.80=2.64\ \mathrm{W}$. (A1)

Rate of loss of gravitational potential energy of the hanging mass: $m_{2}gv=0.050\times 9.81\times 5.383=2.64\ \mathrm{W}$, equal to the electrical power. (A1) This is the content of Lenz's law: the induced current dissipates energy at exactly the rate at which mechanical energy is supplied, so no energy is created in the circuit.

(c) Since $v\propto\dfrac{1}{B^{2}}$, doubling $B$ divides the terminal speed by four: $v=\dfrac{0.3924}{(0.90)^{2}(0.60)^{2}}=\dfrac{0.3924}{0.2916}=1.35\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (M1)(A1)

The effect is more than a factor of two because the field enters twice. Doubling $B$ doubles the induced emf at a given speed, which on its own would double the current; but that doubled current also experiences twice the magnetic force per ampere. The two doublings compound, so the same retarding force is produced at one quarter of the speed. (A1)

(d) Since $v\propto\dfrac{1}{L^{2}}$ as well, doubling $L$ again divides the speed by four: $v=\dfrac{0.3924}{(0.45)^{2}(1.20)^{2}}=\dfrac{0.3924}{0.2916}=1.35\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (M1)(A1)

The two answers agree because $B$ and $L$ appear in the terminal-speed expression only through the product $BL$, which is the quantity that sets both the emf at a given speed and the force at a given current. Doubling either one therefore produces exactly the same change, and the mass of the rod does not enter the expression at all, since at terminal speed the rod's own acceleration is zero. (A1)

Markscheme notes

Part (a): the derivation must include both the emf and the force; a candidate who quotes $F=B^{2}L^{2}v/R$ without showing where it comes from earns one (M1) only. The (A1) for recognising the terminal condition requires the force balance, not simply 'a is zero'. A candidate who neglects the rod's own mass in the acceleration term still reaches the correct terminal speed, since the masses cancel there; award full marks. Do not accept $v=m_{2}gR/(BL)$ — a confusion between $B$ and $B^{2}$ is a common and costly error that loses the final two marks in (a) and, by follow-through, the marks in (c) and (d) unless the method is otherwise correct. Part (b): the numbers must be compared, not merely asserted; accept $2.6\ \mathrm{W}$ on both sides. Part (c): follow-through applies from (a). The explanation mark requires the double role of B — in the emf and in the force — not just the observation that it is squared. Part (d): credit an answer that notices $BL$ is the only combination that matters. A candidate who tries to include the rod's mass in a momentum argument will not reach 1.35 m/s and should not be given the explanation mark.

55. E.5 Two stars of identical apparent brightness whose surface temperatures and radii differ in ways that cancel exactly in the measured quantity

[15 marks]PHYS-E.5-301

Theme E: Nuclear and quantum physics · E.5 Fusion and stars: apparent brightness and luminosity, distance determined from parallax angle, Wien's displacement law and the Stefan-Boltzmann law · difficulty 4

(a) Converting milliarcseconds to arcseconds, $20\ {\rm mas} = 0.020''$ and $10\ {\rm mas} = 0.010''$, so
$$d_{X} = \frac{1}{0.020} = 50\ {\rm pc}, \qquad d_{Y} = \frac{1}{0.010} = 100\ {\rm pc} \;(M1)(A1).$$
In metres, $d_{X} = 50 \times 3.09 \times 10^{16} = 1.55 \times 10^{18}\ {\rm m}$ and $d_{Y} = 100 \times 3.09 \times 10^{16} = 3.09 \times 10^{18}\ {\rm m}$, so star Y is twice as far away as star X (A1).
(b) Using $L = 4\pi d^{2} b$:
$$L_{X} = 4\pi (1.545 \times 10^{18})^{2}(1.00 \times 10^{-9}) = 3.00 \times 10^{28}\ {\rm W} \;(M1)(A1),$$
$$L_{Y} = 4\pi (3.09 \times 10^{18})^{2}(1.00 \times 10^{-9}) = 1.20 \times 10^{29}\ {\rm W} \;(A1),$$
so $L_{Y}/L_{X} = 4.0$. This ratio is simply $\bigl(d_{Y}/d_{X}\bigr)^{2} = 2^{2} = 4$ (A1): since the two apparent brightnesses are equal, the luminosities must scale as the squares of the distances, and no substitution of $b$ is needed to see it.
(c) From $\lambda_{\rm max} T = 2.90 \times 10^{-3}\ {\rm m\,K}$:
$$T_{X} = \frac{2.90 \times 10^{-3}}{290 \times 10^{-9}} = 1.00 \times 10^{4}\ {\rm K}, \qquad T_{Y} = \frac{2.90 \times 10^{-3}}{580 \times 10^{-9}} = 5.00 \times 10^{3}\ {\rm K} \;(M1)(A1).$$
Emitted power per unit area is $\sigma T^{4}$, so the ratio is
$$\frac{\sigma T_{X}^{4}}{\sigma T_{Y}^{4}} = \left(\frac{1.00 \times 10^{4}}{5.00 \times 10^{3}}\right)^{4} = 2^{4} = 16 \;(A1),$$
and X emits sixteen times as much power per square metre of surface as Y does.
(d) With $L = 4\pi R^{2}\sigma T^{4}$, we have $R = \sqrt{L/(4\pi\sigma T^{4})}$.
$$R_{X} = \sqrt{\frac{3.00 \times 10^{28}}{4\pi(5.67 \times 10^{-8})(1.00 \times 10^{4})^{4}}} = \sqrt{\frac{3.00 \times 10^{28}}{7.13 \times 10^{9}}} = 2.05 \times 10^{9}\ {\rm m} \;(M1)(A1),$$
$$R_{Y} = \sqrt{\frac{1.20 \times 10^{29}}{4\pi(5.67 \times 10^{-8})(5.00 \times 10^{3})^{4}}} = \sqrt{\frac{1.20 \times 10^{29}}{4.45 \times 10^{8}}} = 1.64 \times 10^{10}\ {\rm m} \;(A1),$$
giving $R_{Y}/R_{X} = 8.0$ exactly (A1). The integer arises because the two effects compete multiplicatively: from part (c), Y emits only $1/16$ as much per square metre, so to be $4$ times more luminous overall its area must be $4 \times 16 = 64$ times greater, and hence its radius $\sqrt{64} = 8$ times greater (A1). In solar radii these are $R_{X} = 2.05 \times 10^{9}/6.96 \times 10^{8} = 2.9\,R_{\odot}$ and $R_{Y} = 1.64 \times 10^{10}/6.96 \times 10^{8} = 24\,R_{\odot}$ (A1). Star Y, at twenty-four solar radii yet only half the Sun-like temperature class of X, is enormously too large and too luminous to be a main-sequence star for a temperature of $5000\ {\rm K}$; it is a giant (A1). X, at about three solar radii and $10\,000\ {\rm K}$, is consistent with an A-type main-sequence star.
(e) Apparent brightness depends only on the ratio $L/d^{2}$, and both $L$ and $d$ are free to vary by factors that exactly compensate: Y is sixty-four times larger in area but emits one sixteenth per unit area and sits twice as far away, giving $(64/16)/4 = 1$ (A1). The entire structure is invisible in $b$, and becomes visible only through the parallax measurement, which supplies the absolute scale, together with the spectral peak, which supplies $T$ (A1). Brightness alone would place the two stars in the same class entirely.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for converting mas to arcsec, (A1) for both distances in parsecs, (A1) for both distances in metres and the comparison. Candidates who forget that the formula $d = 1/p$ requires arcseconds and use $d = 1/20$ must not be credited: it gives $0.05\ {\rm pc}$, which contradicts the comparison asked for. Follow-through is available for the metre conversions even if the parsec values are wrong.
(b) (M1) for $L = 4\pi d^{2} b$, (A1) for each luminosity (accept $3.0 \times 10^{28}$ and $1.2 \times 10^{29}\ {\rm W}$ to two significant figures), (A1) for the ratio $4.0$ together with the argument from $\bigl(d_{Y}/d_{X}\bigr)^{2}$. If the ratio is stated without argument, award (A1) only if the two luminosity values are themselves correct. Note that one mark is explicitly available for seeing that equal $b$ makes this a pure distance-squared statement; this is meant to reward candidates who reason before computing.
(c) (M1) for applying Wien's law with each wavelength converted to metres, (A1) for both temperatures, (A1) for the factor $16$ with the fourth power shown. Candidates writing the ratio inverted, giving $1/16$, lose the final mark even if the temperatures are right, since part (d) depends on its direction. Accept the factor stated as 'X emits sixteen times as much per unit area'.
(d) (M1) for the rearranged Stefan-Boltzmann expression, (A1) for each radius (accept $2.1 \times 10^{9}$ and $1.6 \times 10^{10}\ {\rm m}$), (A1) for the ratio $8.0$, (A1) for explaining the integer through the product of the area factor and the per-area factor, (A1) for both radii in solar radii, (A1) for identifying Y as a giant with a reason that mentions size, temperature or luminosity rather than merely asserting it. Seven award fields are listed here so that examiners can spread the credit; cap the part at its stated four marks by awarding the strongest four fields achieved. Do not condone a radius ratio worked out from the luminosity ratio alone, which ignores the temperature difference and gives $2$ rather than $8$.
(e) (A1) for the compensation argument with the three factors identified, (A1) for naming parallax (and spectral peak) as the additional measurements. Accept either order. A candidate who says only 'the distance hid it' earns one mark, not two, because the per-unit-area compensation is a separate effect from the distance.

56. D.4 Choosing the turns ratio that delivers most power to a load, where stepping the voltage up further reduces the power delivered because the reflected load falls towards the source resistance

[15 marks]PHYS-D.4-301

Theme D: Fields · D.4 Induction: the ideal transformer, the ratios of turns, voltage and current, and power transfer. With B.5 Current and circuits: a source possessing internal resistance, and maximum power transfer to a load · difficulty 4

(a) Let the potential difference across the primary be $V_{p}$ and across the secondary be $V_{s}$. For an ideal transformer $V_{s} = kV_{p}$ (M1). The secondary current is therefore $I_{s} = V_{s}/R = kV_{p}/R$. Since the transformer is ideal, power is conserved across it, so $V_{p}I_{p} = V_{s}I_{s}$, giving
$$I_{p} = \frac{V_{s}I_{s}}{V_{p}} = \frac{(kV_{p})(kV_{p}/R)}{V_{p}} = \frac{k^{2}V_{p}}{R} \;(A1),$$
and hence the effective resistance presented to the primary is
$$R' = \frac{V_{p}}{I_{p}} = \frac{R}{k^{2}} \;(A1).$$
The primary circuit is then a simple series circuit containing $E$, $r$ and $R'$, so $I_{p} = E/(r + R/k^{2})$ as required.
(b) The power dissipated in the reflected load is the power delivered to the real load, since the transformer is lossless, so
$$P = I_{p}^{2}R' = \left(\frac{E}{r + R'}\right)^{2} R' = \frac{E^{2}R'}{(r + R')^{2}} \;(M1).$$
Treating this as a function of $R'$ and differentiating, or equivalently writing $(r+R')^{2}/R' = r^{2}/R' + 2r + R'$ and minimising the bracket with respect to $R'$, gives a stationary point at $R' = r$ (M1); that $r^{2}/R' + R'$ is minimised at $R' = r$ follows from the symmetry of the two terms under $R' \mapsto r^{2}/R'$, and since $P \to 0$ both as $R' \to 0$ and as $R' \to \infty$, this stationary point is a maximum (A1). Hence the optimum satisfies
$$\frac{R}{k^{2}} = r \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{100}{k^{2}} = 4.0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad k^{2} = 25 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad k = 5.0 \;(A1),$$
and the maximum power delivered is
$$P_{\max} = \frac{E^{2}}{4r} = \frac{12^{2}}{4 \times 4.0} = \frac{144}{16} = 9.0\ {\rm W} \;(A1).$$
(c) With $k = 1$ there is no transformation and $R' = R = 100\ \Omega$, so
$$I_{p} = \frac{12}{4.0 + 100} = 0.115\ {\rm A} \quad\text{and}\quad P = I_{p}^{2}R = (0.115)^{2} \times 100 = 1.33\ {\rm W} \;(M1)(A1).$$
The factor by which the correctly matched transformer improves matters is
$$\frac{9.0}{1.33} = 6.8 \;(A1),$$
so nearly seven times as much power reaches the same $100\ \Omega$ load, coming from the same source.
(d) At $k = 5.0$ we have $R' = 100/25 = 4.0\ \Omega = r$, so
$$I_{p} = \frac{12}{4.0 + 4.0} = 1.5\ {\rm A}, \qquad V_{p} = I_{p}R' = 1.5 \times 4.0 = 6.0\ {\rm V} \;(M1)(A1).$$
Then $V_{s} = kV_{p} = 5.0 \times 6.0 = 30\ {\rm V}$ and $I_{s} = V_{s}/R = 30/100 = 0.30\ {\rm A}$. Checking power: entering the primary, $V_{p}I_{p} = 6.0 \times 1.5 = 9.0\ {\rm W}$; leaving the secondary, $V_{s}I_{s} = 30 \times 0.30 = 9.0\ {\rm W}$ (A1). They agree, as they must for an ideal transformer, and both equal the $9.0\ {\rm W}$ found in part (b). Note that the total power supplied by the source is $EI_{p} = 12 \times 1.5 = 18\ {\rm W}$, the other $9.0\ {\rm W}$ being dissipated in the internal resistance itself.
(e) At $k = 10$ the reflected load is $R' = 100/100 = 1.0\ \Omega$, so
$$I_{p} = \frac{12}{4.0 + 1.0} = 2.4\ {\rm A}, \qquad P_{\rm load} = I_{p}^{2}R' = (2.4)^{2} \times 1.0 = 5.76\ {\rm W} \;(M1),$$
which is below the $9.0\ {\rm W}$ available at $k = 5.0$; the design therefore costs $9.0 - 5.76 = 3.2\ {\rm W}$, a loss of about $36\%$ of the achievable output (A1). The student's argument fails because it tracks the secondary voltage but not the current that can be sustained through it: the power lost in the internal resistance is $I_{p}^{2}r = (2.4)^{2} \times 4.0 = 23\ {\rm W}$, so of the $EI_{p} = 29\ {\rm W}$ that the source supplies, twenty-three watts is dissipated inside the source itself and only 5.76 W survives to the load. Raising $k$ further concentrates more and more of the source's effort into its own internal resistance (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for quoting $V_{s} = kV_{p}$ for an ideal transformer, (A1) for forming $I_{s}$ and then using $V_{p}I_{p} = V_{s}I_{s}$ to reach an expression for $I_{p}$, (A1) for $R' = R/k^{2}$. Candidates may instead quote $V_{s}I_{s} = V_{p}I_{p}$ and $I_{s} = I_{p}/k$; either route earns the marks. Accept a statement that the impedance transformation follows from combining the two transformer ratios.
(b) (M1) for writing $P = E^{2}R'/(r+R')^{2}$ in any equivalent form, (M1) for identifying the maximum by differentiation or by the symmetric-bracket argument, (A1) for justifying that it is a maximum rather than a minimum, (A1) for $k = 5.0$, (A1) for $9.0\ {\rm W}$. Five fields are listed; credit the strongest four. Note particularly that $P_{\max} = E^{2}/(4r)$ is independent of $R$: a candidate may legitimately observe that the $100\ \Omega$ value fixes $k$ but not the ceiling. Follow-through applies from a wrong $k$ provided part (d) is consistent with it.
(c) (M1) for substituting $R' = 100\ \Omega$ into the series circuit, (A1) for $1.33\ {\rm W}$ (accept $1.3\ {\rm W}$), (A1) for the factor $6.8$. A candidate who computes the ratio upside down, giving $0.15$, loses the final mark; the working should make the direction unambiguous since part (d) uses $k = 5$.
(d) (M1) for $I_{p} = 1.5\ {\rm A}$, (A1) for $V_{p} = 6.0\ {\rm V}$ together with $V_{s} = 30\ {\rm V}$ and $I_{s} = 0.30\ {\rm A}$, (A1) for the two power products agreeing at $9.0\ {\rm W}$. The remark about the source supplying $18\ {\rm W}$ is not required but may be credited where a candidate has otherwise failed to justify why half the power is lost.
(e) (M1) for computing $R' = 1.0\ \Omega$ and $I_{p} = 2.4\ {\rm A}$ at $k = 10$, (A1) for the resulting $5.76\ {\rm W}$ and the quantified shortfall, (A1) for explaining that the missing power is dissipated in $r$ rather than anywhere in the transformer. Candidates who simply assert 'the current gets smaller' without tying it to the reflected resistance lose the final mark; the transformer is ideal and no power is lost inside it, so any explanation invoking losses there is wrong.

57. D.2 Recovering two unknown point charges from a zero-field position and one potential reading, then finding where each quantity vanishes once one charge changes sign

[15 marks]PHYS-D.2-301

Theme D: Fields · D.2 Electric and magnetic fields: electric field strength and electric potential due to point charges; the inverse-square law for field strength and the inverse law for potential, and the addition of potentials as scalars against the addition of field strengths as vectors · difficulty 4

(a) At $x = 0.20\ {\rm m}$ the distances to the two charges are $0.20\ {\rm m}$ and $0.60 - 0.20 = 0.40\ {\rm m}$. Between two positive charges the field strengths oppose each other, so for the resultant to vanish their magnitudes must be equal (M1):
$$\frac{kq_{1}}{(0.20)^{2}} = \frac{kq_{2}}{(0.40)^{2}} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{q_{1}}{0.040} = \frac{q_{2}}{0.160} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad q_{2} = 4q_{1} \;(A1).$$
(b) The potential at that point is the scalar sum of the two contributions:
$$V = \frac{kq_{1}}{0.20} + \frac{kq_{2}}{0.40} = k\left(\frac{q_{1}}{0.20} + \frac{4q_{1}}{0.40}\right) = k(5q_{1} + 10q_{1}) = 15kq_{1} \;(M1),$$
using the relation from part (a) before any substitution. Hence
$$q_{1} = \frac{2.70 \times 10^{4}}{15 \times 8.99 \times 10^{9}} = 2.00 \times 10^{-7}\ {\rm C} \;(A1),$$
and therefore $q_{2} = 4q_{1} = 8.00 \times 10^{-7}\ {\rm C}$ (A1). Both are positive, as stated. Note that using part (a) first is what made this possible: had the candidate instead written the two potential contributions as two separate unknowns, the single potential reading would have been one equation in two unknowns.
(c) With both charges positive, the potential at a point is $V = k q_{1}/r_{1} + k q_{2}/r_{2}$, and since every $q$ and every $r$ is positive, both terms are positive everywhere on the axis (M1). A sum of two positive terms cannot be zero, so there is no point on the $x$-axis at which $V = 0$ (A1). The potential falls monotonically as a point moves outwards from either charge towards infinity, so $V \to 0$ asymptotically; the zero is reached only in the limit of infinite distance (A1). Note the contrast with the field strength: there, two opposing contributions can cancel at a finite point, and did.
(d) With unlike charges, between them the field strengths point in the same direction -- away from the positive charge and towards the negative one, both along $-x$ for a point between them -- so no cancellation is possible there. Cancellation must therefore occur outside the pair, on the side of the smaller magnitude, that is at $x < 0$. Equating magnitudes (M1),
$$\frac{k|q_{1}|}{x^{2}} = \frac{kq_{2}}{(0.60 - x)^{2}} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{0.60 - x}{|x|} = \sqrt{\frac{8.00}{2.00}} = 2 ,$$
and with $x < 0$ this gives $0.60 - x = -2x$, so $x = -0.60\ {\rm m}$ (A1). For the potential, each region must be treated separately because $|x|$ and $|x - 0.60|$ change form:
$$V = 0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{2.00 \times 10^{-7}}{|x|} = \frac{8.00 \times 10^{-7}}{|x - 0.60|} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad |x - 0.60| = 4|x| \;(M1).$$
Between the charges, $0 < x < 0.60$: $0.60 - x = 4x$, so $x = 0.12\ {\rm m}$ (A1).
To the left of both charges, $x < 0$: $0.60 - x = -4x$, so $x = -0.20\ {\rm m}$ (A1).
To the right of both charges, $x > 0.60$: $x - 0.60 = 4x$ gives $x = -0.20$, which contradicts the assumption, so that region contributes nothing. There are therefore two positions, $x = 0.12\ {\rm m}$ and $x = -0.20\ {\rm m}$, at which the potential vanishes, against one where the field strength vanishes. Checking: at $x = 0.12$, $k(8.00 \times 10^{-7}/0.48 - 2.00 \times 10^{-7}/0.12) = k(1.667 \times 10^{-6} - 1.667 \times 10^{-6}) = 0$; at $x = -0.20$, $k(8.00 \times 10^{-7}/0.80 - 2.00 \times 10^{-7}/0.20) = k(1.00 \times 10^{-6} - 1.00 \times 10^{-6}) = 0$.
(e) Field strength is a vector: at each point the two contributions are added with their directions, so a zero requires two contributions of equal magnitude and opposite direction (M1). With unlike charges that happens only outside the pair, on the side of the smaller charge, giving exactly one place on the line (A1). Potential is a scalar: contributions are added as signed numbers, so a zero requires only that the positive and negative amounts happen to cancel in size (M1). The scalar $V$ changes sign on crossing either charge, so there is a zero between them, and there is a second zero outside on the side of the smaller magnitude where the inverse-distance contributions can again balance (A1). One fewer piece of information -- direction -- is being matched, which is precisely why there is more than one place it can happen (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for noting that between two positive charges the field strengths oppose and therefore equating magnitudes, (A1) for $q_{2} = 4q_{1}$. A candidate who equates $kq_{1}/0.20^{2} = kq_{2}/0.40^{2}$ without any statement about direction scores (M1) but loses (A1) if the distances are taken as $0.20$ and $0.60$.
(b) (M1) for forming the potential sum and substituting $q_{2} = 4q_{1}$ so that it collapses to one unknown, (A1) for $q_{1} = 2.00 \times 10^{-7}\ {\rm C}$, (A1) for $q_{2} = 8.00 \times 10^{-7}\ {\rm C}$. Accept $0.200\ \mu{\rm C}$ and $0.800\ \mu{\rm C}$. Follow-through from part (a) is available. Note that a potential of exactly $2.70 \times 10^{4}\ {\rm V}$ corresponds to $15kq_{1} = 2.697 \times 10^{4}\ {\rm V}$; rounding of the stated value is condoned and the charges are round numbers, which is the intended reading.
(c) (M1) for noting both terms in the potential sum are positive, (A1) for concluding there is no zero, (A1) for locating the limit at infinity and noting the contrast with the field strength. A candidate who says 'the potential approaches zero at infinity' without explaining why no finite point works loses the first two marks.
(d) (M1) for identifying that cancellation must be outside the pair and, for the potential, writing the scalar equation with modulus signs, (A1) for $x = -0.60\ {\rm m}$, (A1) for $x = 0.12\ {\rm m}$, (A1) for $x = -0.20\ {\rm m}$. Four marks total; candidates who treat only one region for the potential lose marks for the missing answer, and it is expected that most will find the inner zero and not the outer one. Candidates who obtain an $x$ inside the region they assumed should be credited only if they check their answer against the assumption. Note especially that any answer giving two zeros of the field strength, or one zero of the potential, should be treated as a failure to carry out the case analysis rather than an arithmetic slip.
(e) (M1) for naming field strength as a vector addition, (A1) for the conclusion that a zero needs opposition and occurs in one place, (M1) for naming potential as a scalar addition, (A1) for the two resulting regions and the sign change across each charge, (A1) for the summary that less information is being matched. Five fields; credit three. Accept any argument reaching 'vector versus scalar' as the reason.

58. E.1 The distance of closest approach in Rutherford scattering, the energy at which that distance falls to within the nucleus and the alpha-scattering picture therefore breaks down, and the de Broglie wavelength that decides whether a trajectory picture was ever appropriate

[15 marks]PHYS-E.1-301

Theme E: Nuclear and quantum physics · E.1 Structure of the atom: the nuclear model, the scattering of alpha particles, and the use of electrostatic potential energy to find a distance of closest approach. With E.2 Quantum physics: the de Broglie wavelength of a massive particle · difficulty 4

(a) As the alpha particle approaches, electrostatic repulsion does work against it, converting kinetic energy into electrostatic potential energy. At the distance of closest approach the alpha particle has momentarily stopped, so all of its original kinetic energy has become potential energy (M1):
$$E = \frac{k(2e)(Ze)}{d} = \frac{2kZe^{2}}{d} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad d = \frac{2kZe^{2}}{E} \;(A1).$$
At that instant the velocity is zero: it is the turning point of the motion, at which the alpha particle reverses direction.
(b) Converting the energy to joules, $E = 5.0 \times 10^{6} \times 1.60 \times 10^{-19} = 8.0 \times 10^{-13}\ {\rm J}$ (M1). Then
$$d = \frac{2(8.99 \times 10^{9})(79)(1.60 \times 10^{-19})^{2}}{8.0 \times 10^{-13}} = \frac{3.64 \times 10^{-26}}{8.0 \times 10^{-13}} = 4.55 \times 10^{-14}\ {\rm m} \;(M1)(A1),$$
which is $45.5\ {\rm fm}$. So even a head-on approach turns the alpha particle around some forty-five femtometres from the centre of the gold nucleus.
(c) The nuclear radius is
$$R = 1.2 \times 10^{-15} \times 197^{1/3} = 1.2 \times 10^{-15} \times 5.82 = 6.98 \times 10^{-15}\ {\rm m} = 6.98\ {\rm fm} \;(M1)(A1),$$
and the ratio is
$$\frac{d}{R} = \frac{4.55 \times 10^{-14}}{6.98 \times 10^{-15}} = 6.5 \;(A1).$$
The alpha particle therefore turns around at about six and a half nuclear radii, comfortably short of the nuclear surface (A1). Its closest approach is not to nuclear matter, but to a point in empty space but empty space: it never touches nuclear matter, and consequently nothing except the inverse-square electrostatic repulsion is responsible for its deflection. This is precisely the condition under which Rutherford's analysis is valid, and it is why the experiment could be interpreted as evidence for a small dense nucleus at all.
(d) Setting $d = R$ in the relation from part (a) and rearranging,
$$E = \frac{2kZe^{2}}{R} = \frac{3.64 \times 10^{-26}}{6.98 \times 10^{-15}} = 5.21 \times 10^{-12}\ {\rm J} \;(M1)(A1),$$
which in electronvolts is
$$\frac{5.21 \times 10^{-12}}{1.60 \times 10^{-19}} = 3.26 \times 10^{7}\ {\rm eV} = 32.6\ {\rm MeV} \;(A1).$$
