Above roughly $33\ {\rm MeV}$ the alpha particle reaches the nuclear surface, and the strong nuclear force -- attractive and of range only a few femtometres, so utterly negligible at forty-five femtometres but dominant once contact is made -- begins to act. Scattering angles would then depart from the Rutherford formula, which assumes pure Coulomb repulsion, and the simple picture breaks down.
(e) The momentum follows from $E = p^{2}/(2m_{\alpha})$, so
$$p = \sqrt{2m_{\alpha}E} = \sqrt{2(6.64 \times 10^{-27})(8.0 \times 10^{-13})} = 1.03 \times 10^{-19}\ {\rm kg\,m\,s^{-1}} \;(M1),$$
and the de Broglie wavelength is
$$\lambda = \frac{h}{p} = \frac{6.63 \times 10^{-34}}{1.03 \times 10^{-19}} = 6.43 \times 10^{-15}\ {\rm m} = 6.43\ {\rm fm} \;(A1).$$
Comparing this with part (c), the de Broglie wavelength of $6.4\ {\rm fm}$ is very nearly equal to the nuclear radius of $7.0\ {\rm fm}$ (A1). A particle is well described by a classical trajectory only when its wavelength is small compared with the distances that characterise the situation it moves through; here the wavelength is comparable to the very object doing the scattering, though still about seven times smaller than the distance of closest approach of $45\ {\rm fm}$. The classical picture is therefore serviceable but not secure: at higher incident energies both the distance of closest approach and the wavelength shrink together, and the assumptions behind part (a) fail at much the same place.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for equating the initial kinetic energy to the electrostatic potential energy at the turning point, (A1) for the stated relation with both factors of $e$ included via $(2e)(Ze)$. Do not accept an expression missing the factor of two, which arises from the alpha particle's charge being $2e$; this is the commonest error in this calculation. The statement that the velocity is zero at that instant should be credited within these marks.
(b) (M1) for converting MeV to joules, (M1) for substituting correctly into the relation from part (a), (A1) for $4.55 \times 10^{-14}\ {\rm m}$ (accept $4.5 \times 10^{-14}\ {\rm m}$ or $45\ {\rm fm}$ to two significant figures). Condoned: using $E = 8.0 \times 10^{-13}\ {\rm J}$ directly if shown. Follow-through from a missing factor of two in part (a) is allowed, giving $2.3 \times 10^{-14}\ {\rm m}$ in that case.
(c) (M1) for evaluating $A^{1/3}$ and multiplying by $R_{0}$, (A1) for $6.98 \times 10^{-15}\ {\rm m}$ (accept $7.0\ {\rm fm}$), (A1) for the ratio $6.5$, (A1) for the conclusion that the alpha particle does not reach the nuclear surface and that only the Coulomb interaction is involved. A candidate who reaches the ratio but draws no conclusion from it loses this mark: the ratio is what licenses the whole Rutherford interpretation, not a decoration on it.
(d) (M1) for setting $d = R$ and rearranging to $E = 2kZe^{2}/R$, (A1) for $5.21 \times 10^{-12}\ {\rm J}$ or its equivalent, (A1) for $32.6\ {\rm MeV}$ (accept $33\ {\rm MeV}$), together with the identification of the strong nuclear force and its short range. A candidate who identifies the effect but does not note that the range is negligible at forty-five femtometres loses the mark: the whole point of the threshold is that a force present all along becomes decisive only at contact.
(e) (M1) for obtaining $p$ from $E = p^{2}/2m$, (A1) for $6.43 \times 10^{-15}\ {\rm m}$ or $6.4\ {\rm fm}$, (A1) for the quantitative comparison against the nuclear radius and against the distance of closest approach, with a statement about the validity of the trajectory picture. Credit candidates who note both that the wavelength is comparable to the nucleus and that it is still smaller than the turning distance; a judgement of 'borderline but usable' is the most honest reading and deserves full marks, whereas 'classical picture is fine' or 'must be treated as a wave' without qualification does not.

59. A.2 Hooke's law and A.3 the force constant from a gradient

[15 marks]PHYS-A.3-102

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.2 (Forces: Hooke's law, the force constant of a spring) and A.3 (Work, energy and power: elastic potential energy) · difficulty 4

(a) The line passes through the origin and through the reading at $m = 300$ g, where the line gives $x = 12.15$ cm. (M1) The gradient is therefore $12.15/300 = 0.0405$ cm g$^{-1}$. (A1)

(b) The line predicts $x = 0.0405(250) = 10.1$ cm at $m = 250$ g, but the recorded reading is 11.4 cm, so X lies 1.3 cm above the line. (M1) The other five readings lie within about 0.05 cm of the line, which is the scale of the random scatter, so the deviation of X is roughly twenty-five times larger than the scatter of the rest of the data. (A1) A single reading that far off the pattern is an anomalous reading, most likely a misread scale or a mass recorded wrongly, and it should be excluded from the line of best fit and investigated. (A1)

(c) Converting the gradient into SI units, $0.0405$ cm g$^{-1} = 0.0405 \times 10^{-2}$ m per $10^{-3}$ kg $= 0.405$ m kg$^{-1}$. (M1) In equilibrium $mg = kx$, so $x/m = g/k$ and hence $k = g/(x/m)$. (M1) Therefore $k = 9.81/0.405 = 24.2$ N m$^{-1}$. (A1)

(d) The gradient depends on how accurately the extension was read against the scale, and the smallest scale division limits how precisely each point can be placed. (A1) Reading each extension too large by a fixed amount, for example by taking the scale from the wrong mark, makes the gradient too large. (M1) Since $k = g/(x/m)$, an over-large gradient gives a force constant that is too small, so the uncertainty in $k$ is in the opposite direction to the error in the gradient. (A1)

(e) Each spring now supports the same force $mg$, so each extends by the same amount as the single spring did on its own. (M1) The total extension is therefore twice the extension of one spring for the same hanging mass, so the gradient doubles to 0.0810 cm g$^{-1}$. (A1)(A1) Since $k = g/(x/m)$ and the extension per unit mass has doubled, the force constant of the combination is $24.2/2 = 12.1$ N m$^{-1}$, which is the expected result for two identical springs in series. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) Award M1 for reading a pair of coordinates from the line, A1 for $0.0405$ cm g$^{-1}$ with the unit. Accept 0.040 to 0.041; accept the gradient quoted in m kg$^{-1}$ as 0.405 only if the unit is given. (b) M1 for comparing X with the line numerically, A1 for quantifying that comparison against the scatter of the other readings, A1 for a conclusion that X should be excluded and investigated. A candidate who simply writes that X is anomalous without any number scores M1 at most. Do not accept a conclusion that X should be kept because it is a real reading; the question asks for a judgement about the line of best fit. (c) M1 for converting the gradient to m kg$^{-1}$, M1 for using $mg = kx$ or equivalently $k = g/(x/m)$, A1 for 24.2 N m$^{-1}$. Follow through from the candidate's own gradient in (a). A candidate who omits the conversion and answers 9.81/0.0405 = 242 N m$^{-1}$ has the method right and the unit wrong, and scores 2 of the 3. (d) A1 for a source that is genuinely a source of uncertainty in the gradient, such as the resolution of the scale, the parallax in reading it, or the finite mass of the spring; M1 for connecting the sign of the effect on the gradient to the effect on $k$; A1 for the inverse relationship stated correctly. (e) M1 for stating that each spring carries the same force, A1 for the total extension doubling, A1 for the gradient doubling to 0.0810 cm g$^{-1}$, A1 for the force constant halving to 12.1 N m$^{-1}$.

60. D.1 Gravitational fields

[15 marks]PHYS-D.1-001

Theme D: Fields · D.1 Gravitational fields (AHL): orbital speed, total energy of an orbiting satellite; A.1 kinematics · difficulty 4

(a) For a circular orbit the gravitational force provides the centripetal force:

$$\frac{GMm}{r^{2}}=\frac{mv^{2}}{r}\ \Rightarrow\ v=\sqrt{\frac{GM}{r}}.$$ (M1)

$GM=6.67\times10^{-11}\times5.97\times10^{24}=3.98\times10^{14}\,\mathrm{m^{3}\,s^{-2}}$.

Low orbit: $v_{1}=\sqrt{\dfrac{3.98\times10^{14}}{6.87\times10^{6}}}=\sqrt{5.80\times10^{7}}=7.61\times10^{3}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ (A1)

Geostationary: $v_{2}=\sqrt{\dfrac{3.98\times10^{14}}{4.23\times10^{7}}}=\sqrt{9.41\times10^{6}}=3.07\times10^{3}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ (A1)

(b) $a=\dfrac{r_{1}+r_{2}}{2}=\dfrac{6.87\times10^{6}+4.23\times10^{7}}{2}=2.46\times10^{7}\,\mathrm{m}$ (A1)

On the ellipse, $E=\tfrac12mv^{2}-\dfrac{GMm}{r}$ (M1) (kinetic + gravitational potential energy)

Equating to the given total energy:

$$\tfrac12mv^{2}-\frac{GMm}{r}=-\frac{GMm}{2a}\ \Rightarrow\ \tfrac12v^{2}=\frac{GM}{r}-\frac{GM}{2a}$$ (M1)

$$\Rightarrow\ v^{2}=GM\left(\frac{2}{r}-\frac{1}{a}\right).\ \ \textbf{(A1)}$$ *(AG)*

(c) At perigee, $r=r_{1}=6.87\times10^{6}\,\mathrm{m}$:

$$v_{\text{per}}^{2}=3.98\times10^{14}\left(\frac{2}{6.87\times10^{6}}-\frac{1}{2.4585\times10^{7}}\right)=3.98\times10^{14}\left(2.911\times10^{-7}-4.068\times10^{-8}\right)=9.97\times10^{7}$$

$v_{\text{per}}=9.99\times10^{3}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ (A1)

$$\Delta v_{1}=9.99-7.61=2.37\times10^{3}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\ \ (2.37\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}})$$ (A1)

At apogee, $r=r_{2}=4.23\times10^{7}\,\mathrm{m}$:

$$v_{\text{apo}}^{2}=3.98\times10^{14}\left(\frac{2}{4.23\times10^{7}}-\frac{1}{2.4585\times10^{7}}\right)=3.98\times10^{14}\left(4.728\times10^{-8}-4.068\times10^{-8}\right)=2.63\times10^{6}$$

$v_{\text{apo}}=1.62\times10^{3}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ (A1)

$$\Delta v_{2}=3.07-1.62=1.45\times10^{3}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}},\qquad \Delta v_{\text{total}}=2.37+1.45=3.82\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$$ (A1)

(d) Period of the transfer ellipse: $T=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{a^{3}}{GM}}$

$$T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{(2.4585\times10^{7})^{3}}{3.98\times10^{14}}}=2\pi\sqrt{3.73\times10^{7}}=3.84\times10^{4}\,\mathrm{s}$$ (M1)

Time of flight $=\tfrac12T=1.92\times10^{4}\,\mathrm{s}=5.33\,\mathrm{h}$ (A1)

Period of the geostationary orbit: $T_{\text{geo}}=2\pi\sqrt{\dfrac{(4.23\times10^{7})^{3}}{3.98\times10^{14}}}=8.66\times10^{4}\,\mathrm{s}\;(=24.1\,\mathrm{h})$

$$\theta=360^{\circ}\times\frac{1.92\times10^{4}}{8.66\times10^{4}}=79.8^{\circ}\approx80^{\circ}$$ (A1)

The spacecraft travels through $180^{\circ}$ around Earth, so the target must be $180^{\circ}-80^{\circ}\approx100^{\circ}$ ahead of the launch position at the instant of the first burn, so that both arrive at the same point at the same time. (A1)

Markscheme notes

(a) The 'show that' requires $\frac{GMm}{r^{2}}=\frac{mv^{2}}{r}$ stated explicitly; the cancellation of $m$ should be seen. Allow 7.6 km s⁻¹ and 3.1 km s⁻¹ to 2 s.f.

(b) Award (M1) for writing $E=\tfrac12mv^{2}-\frac{GMm}{r}$ — a candidate who writes only $\tfrac12mv^{2}$ cannot proceed. The final line is AG, so the algebraic route must be visible; do not award (A1) for quoting the result.

(c) Accept values in the range 9.9–10.0 km s⁻¹ and 1.60–1.64 km s⁻¹ given rounding of $GM$. Follow through on a candidate's $a$. The second $\Delta v$ must be computed as $v_{2}-v_{\text{apo}}$ and not as $v_{\text{apo}}-v_{2}$; a negative answer here is a physics error, not an arithmetic slip, and forfeits the mark.

(d) Allow $T_{\text{geo}}=24\,\mathrm{h}$ if stated from the definition of a geostationary orbit; then $\theta=360^{\circ}\times5.33/24=80^{\circ}$. Accept 99°–101° for the lead angle. The final (A1) is for the lead angle *and* the statement that the two must arrive together; a bare '100°' with no explanation earns no mark.

61. B.5 Systematic error from meter resistance, and why two wrong readings bracket the truth

[14 marks]PHYS-B.5-103

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.5 Current and circuits (practical circuits; ideal and non-ideal ammeters and voltmeters) · difficulty 4

(a) In circuit 1 the voltmeter and $X$ are in parallel, so the voltage $V$ across them is
the same and the currents add (M1). The ammeter reads

$$I = I_{X} + I_{V} = \frac{V}{X} + \frac{V}{R_{V}} = V\left(\frac{1}{X} + \frac{1}{R_{V}}\right)
\tag{A1}$$

so the ratio of the readings is

$$R_{1} = \frac{V}{I} = \frac{1}{\dfrac{1}{X} + \dfrac{1}{R_{V}}} = \frac{X R_{V}}{X + R_{V}}
\tag{AG}$$

which is the parallel combination of $X$ and $R_{V}$. It is therefore always less than $X$.

(b) In circuit 2 the ammeter is in series with $X$, so it reads the true current $I$ through
$X$ (M1), while the voltmeter is across both and reads

$$V = I X + I R_{A} = I(X + R_{A}) \tag{A1}$$

so $R_{2} = \dfrac{V}{I} = X + R_{A}$ (AG). This reading is always greater than $X$, because
it includes the resistance of the ammeter itself.

(c) From (b), $X = R_{2} - R_{A} = 102 - 2.0 = 100\ \Omega$ (M1)(A1). Substituting back into
(a) as a check,

$$R_{1} = \frac{100 \times 4000}{100 + 4000} = \frac{400000}{4100} = 97.56\ \Omega \tag{A1}$$

which agrees with the reading of $97.6\ \Omega$ to the precision quoted. The systematic
errors are therefore

$$\text{circuit 1: } 97.56 - 100 = -2.44\ \Omega \qquad
\text{circuit 2: } 102 - 100 = +2.00\ \Omega \tag{A1}$$

so the two readings bracket the true value, the first low and the second high.

(d) The magnitude of the error in circuit 1 is

$$X - \frac{X R_{V}}{X + R_{V}} = \frac{X(X + R_{V}) - X R_{V}}{X + R_{V}} = \frac{X^{2}}{X + R_{V}}
\tag{M1}$$

and the magnitude of the error in circuit 2 is $R_{A}$ (A1). Setting them equal,

$$\frac{X^{2}}{X + R_{V}} = R_{A} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad X^{2} - R_{A}X - R_{A}R_{V} = 0
\tag{M1}$$

$$X = \frac{R_{A} + \sqrt{R_{A}^{2} + 4R_{A}R_{V}}}{2}
= \frac{2.0 + \sqrt{4.0 + 4(2.0)(4000)}}{2} = \frac{2.0 + 178.9}{2} = 90.4\ \Omega \tag{A1}$$

The negative root is rejected as unphysical. Below about $90\ \Omega$ circuit 1 is the more
accurate of the two; above it, circuit 2 is. Note that the crossover is not where the two
readings are equal — it is where their *errors* are, and the two conditions are different.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for recognising the parallel arrangement and that the currents add. (A1) for
$I = V\left(\frac{1}{X} + \frac{1}{R_{V}}\right)$. (AG) for the result. Allow a candidate who
simply writes "$R_{1}$ is the parallel combination of $X$ and $R_{V}$" to earn (M1) and (AG)
but not the (A1), since the mark is for showing where the expression comes from.

(b) (M1) for noting that the ammeter now reads the true current through $X$. (A1) for
$V = I(X + R_{A})$. (AG) for the result.

(c) (M1) for using $X = R_{2} - R_{A}$ in preference to manipulating the expression in (a);
either route is acceptable but the (a) route requires solving a quadratic and is slower.
(A1) for $100\ \Omega$. (A1) for the numerical check against $97.56\ \Omega$ — this mark is
for confirming consistency and should not be given to a candidate who does not perform it.
(A1) for both errors with correct signs. Follow-through: a candidate who correctly obtains
$X$ from (b) but miscalculates the check loses only the check mark.

(d) (M1) for the error expression $\frac{X^{2}}{X + R_{V}}$. (A1) for identifying the error in
circuit 2 as $R_{A}$, independent of $X$. (M1) for forming and solving the quadratic. (A1) for
$90.4\ \Omega$; accept $90\ \Omega$.

Common error to penalise: equating the two *readings*, $\frac{XR_{V}}{X+R_{V}} = X + R_{A}$,
which gives a different and meaningless answer. This earns neither of the last two marks.
Condonation: allow the approximation $X \ll R_{V}$, giving the error as
$\frac{X^{2}}{R_{V}}$ and the crossover at $\sqrt{R_{A}R_{V}} = 89.4\ \Omega$, with full
marks, since the difference from the exact value is just over one per cent.

62. C.5 An echo from a moving reflector is Doppler shifted twice, not once

[14 marks]PHYS-C.5-102

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.5 Doppler effect (the Doppler effect for sound; moving source and moving observer) · difficulty 4

(a) The source (the bat) is stationary relative to the air, so the wavelength in the air is
$\lambda = \frac{c}{f}$ (M1). The wall moves towards the source, so it meets wavefronts at the
relative speed $c + u$ (M1) and receives

$$f_{1} = \frac{c + u}{\lambda} = \frac{c + u}{c/f} = f\frac{c + u}{c} \tag{A1}$$

$$f_{1} = 40.0 \times \frac{340 + 12.0}{340} = 40.0 \times \frac{352}{340}
= 41.4\ \mathrm{kHz} \tag{A1}$$

(b) The wall now acts as a source of frequency $f_{1}$ — the frequency at which it receives
and re-radiates — and it moves towards the bat, so the wavefronts are compressed (M1). The
wavelength of the reflected sound in the air ahead of the wall is

$$\lambda' = \frac{c - u}{f_{1}} \tag{M1}$$

because the wall advances by $u$ during each period. The bat, stationary in the air, receives

$$f_{2} = \frac{c}{\lambda'} = \frac{c f_{1}}{c - u} = f\frac{c + u}{c - u} \tag{A1}$$

$$f_{2} = 40.0 \times \frac{352}{328} = 40.0 \times 1.0732 = 42.9\ \mathrm{kHz} \tag{A1}$$

Equivalently, $f_{2} = f_{1} \times \frac{340}{328} = 41.41 \times 1.0366 = 42.9\
\mathrm{kHz}$ (A1).

(c) The bat hears both the emitted $40.0\ \mathrm{kHz}$ and the returned $42.9\
\mathrm{kHz}$ simultaneously, so the beat frequency is

$$f_{\text{beat}} = f_{2} - f = 42.93 - 40.0 = 2.93\ \mathrm{kHz} \tag{A1}$$

which is well within a bat's audible range (A1).

(d) The student's $f' = f\frac{c+u}{c}$ is the frequency at the *wall*, not the frequency back
at the bat (A1). The shift happens twice because the wall changes role between the two legs of
the journey: it is a moving observer on the way out and a moving source on the way back (R1).
Each stage multiplies the frequency by a factor greater than one, so the two-stage result
exceeds the one-stage result by the factor $\frac{c}{c-u} = 1.037$, about $3.7\%$ (A1). The
student's value $41.4\ \mathrm{kHz}$ against the true $42.9\ \mathrm{kHz}$ understates the beat
frequency by more than half: $1.41\ \mathrm{kHz}$ instead of $2.93\ \mathrm{kHz}$.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for the wavelength in air, (M1) for the relative speed $c + u$ at which the wall
meets wavefronts, (A1) for the formula, (A1) for $41.4\ \mathrm{kHz}$. Accept $41.41\
\mathrm{kHz}$. A candidate who uses the moving-source formula here has the roles reversed and
loses both (M1)s.

(b) (M1) for recognising the wall as a moving source on the return leg, (M1) for
$\lambda' = \frac{c-u}{f_{1}}$, (A1) for the combined formula, (A1) for $42.9\ \mathrm{kHz}$,
(A1) for the numerical check using $f_{1}$. Accept $42.93\ \mathrm{kHz}$.

Forfeiture: a candidate who writes $f_{2} = f\frac{c+u}{c}$ again — that is, who applies the
same formula twice with the same sign convention — has not noticed the change of role and earns
at most the first (M1).

(c) (A1) for $2.93\ \mathrm{kHz}$. (A1) for noting it is audible to the bat, or for any
correct remark about its size. Follow-through from (b) is allowed.

(d) (A1) for identifying the student's value as the frequency at the wall. (R1) for the change
of role between the two legs. (A1) for the numerical comparison. Do not credit a bare
statement that "it is shifted twice" without identifying what the wall is in each stage.

63. B.3 Recovering an unknown compartment volume from two pressure readings and one known volume, with the temperature and the amount of gas never being given

[14 marks]PHYS-B.3-301

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.3 Gas laws: the equation of state for an ideal gas pV = nRT, and its use when a fixed amount of gas occupies a region whose volume is initially unknown · difficulty 4

(a) Applying $pV = nRT$ to each compartment before the valve is opened gives $n_{A} = p_{A}V_{A}/RT$ and $n_{B} = p_{B}V_{B}/RT$, where $T$ is the common initial temperature (M1). The total amount of gas is conserved when the valve opens, so $n = n_{A} + n_{B}$, and applying the equation of state once more to the whole vessel of volume $V_{A} + V_{B}$ at the final pressure $p$:
$$\frac{p(V_{A} + V_{B})}{RT} = \frac{p_{A}V_{A}}{RT} + \frac{p_{B}V_{B}}{RT} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad p(V_{A} + V_{B}) = p_{A}V_{A} + p_{B}V_{B} \;(A1),$$
since $RT$ is common to every term. The single assumption is that the final temperature equals the initial temperature, which the thermal contact with the large surroundings ensures (A1). Note that no numerical value of $T$ was used.
(b) Substituting, and working in ${\rm dm^{3}}$ since the units of pressure cancel between the two sides,
$$2.4\,(V_{A} + 3.0) = 3.0\,V_{A} + 2.0(3.0) \;(M1)$$
$$2.4V_{A} + 7.2 = 3.0V_{A} + 6.0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 1.2 = 0.6V_{A} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad V_{A} = 2.0\ {\rm dm^{3}} \;(A1),$$
so the total internal volume is $V_{A} + V_{B} = 2.0 + 3.0 = 5.0\ {\rm dm^{3}}$ (A1). Checking: $p_{A}V_{A} + p_{B}V_{B} = 3.0(2.0) + 2.0(3.0) = 12.0$ and $p(V_{A}+V_{B}) = 2.4(5.0) = 12.0$, which agree.
(c) With $T = 300\ {\rm K}$ and $R = 8.31\ {\rm J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$:
$$n_{A} = \frac{p_{A}V_{A}}{RT} = \frac{3.0 \times 10^{5} \times 2.0 \times 10^{-3}}{8.31 \times 300} = \frac{600}{2493} = 0.241\ {\rm mol} \;(M1)(A1),$$
$$n_{B} = \frac{p_{B}V_{B}}{RT} = \frac{2.0 \times 10^{5} \times 3.0 \times 10^{-3}}{8.31 \times 300} = \frac{600}{2493} = 0.241\ {\rm mol} \;(A1),$$
to three significant figures. The two amounts are identical. This is not a coincidence: both compartments have the same product $pV$, equal to $600\ {\rm J}$, and by the equation of state equal values of $pV$ at equal $T$ imply equal amounts of substance (A1). The total is therefore $0.481\ {\rm mol}$.
(d) The molar mass is total mass divided by total amount of substance:
$$M = \frac{m}{n} = \frac{13.8 \times 10^{-3}}{0.481} = 2.87 \times 10^{-2}\ {\rm kg\,mol^{-1}} = 28.7\ {\rm g\,mol^{-1}} \;(M1)(A1),$$
where $n = 1200/2493 = 0.481\ {\rm mol}$ has been used in full rather than rounded. The value lies closest to nitrogen at $28.0\ {\rm g\,mol^{-1}}$ (A1), with the remaining $2.5\%$ difference attributable to the uncertainty in the measured mass and pressures; oxygen at $32.0$ and carbon dioxide at $44.0$ are too heavy, and helium at $4.0$ far too light. The gas is therefore most plausibly nitrogen, or air, whose mean molar mass is about $29\ {\rm g\,mol^{-1}}$.
(e) The recovery worked because $RT$ was a common factor of every term in part (a) and therefore cancelled before any substitution was made: the relation $p(V_{A}+V_{B}) = p_{A}V_{A} + p_{B}V_{B}$ is a statement about the conservation of $nRT$, and dividing through by $RT$ removes both unknowns at once (A1). The method did not need $T$ because the quantity being conserved is not $n$ but $nRT$ divided by a temperature that is the same before and after (A1). If the vessel had been thermally isolated and the final temperature had risen, then the final state would carry a different $RT'$, giving $p(V_{A}+V_{B}) = RT'\bigl(n_{A}+n_{B}\bigr) = \tfrac{T'}{T}\bigl(p_{A}V_{A} + p_{B}V_{B}\bigr)$, and the simple relation of part (a) would no longer hold: with $T' > T$ the candidate using part (a) unchanged would obtain too large a value for $V_{A}$, since the measured final pressure is then higher than it would have been under isothermal conditions for the same total volume (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for writing the equation of state for each compartment or for the total, (A1) for deducing the stated relation with the cancellation of $RT$ evident, (A1) for identifying that the final temperature must equal the initial one. A candidate who merely asserts conservation of $pV$ without referring to the equation of state scores (A1) for the relation only. Note that candidates may work in ${\rm m^{3}}$ or in ${\rm dm^{3}}$ provided they are consistent; no mark is awarded for the conversion itself.
(b) (M1) for substituting correctly into the relation from part (a), (A1) for $V_{A} = 2.0\ {\rm dm^{3}}$, (A1) for $5.0\ {\rm dm^{3}}$ total. Follow-through is available for candidates who use the relation from part (a) but make an arithmetic slip, provided they then check against $p(V_{A}+V_{B}) = p_{A}V_{A} + p_{B}V_{B}$. Forfeits: using ratios of pressures alone, which implicitly assumes the two volumes are equal and yields $V_{A} = 2.5\ {\rm dm^{3}}$, and losing a factor of ten in the conversion between ${\rm dm^{3}}$ and ${\rm m^{3}}$ in one direction only, which gives a manifestly inconsistent check.
(c) (M1) for applying $n = pV/RT$ with each compartment separately, (A1) for both $0.241\ {\rm mol}$ values, (A1) for the comment that they are equal and for linking that to equal values of $pV$. Accept $0.24\ {\rm mol}$ if consistent. A candidate who reports the values without noticing they are identical loses the comment mark; this is a designed observation, not an incidental one.
(d) (M1) for using total mass over total amount, (A1) for $28.7\ {\rm g\,mol^{-1}}$ (accept $28.6$ to $28.8$ from rounding differences), (A1) for identifying nitrogen and ruling out the alternatives explicitly. Condoned: identifying the gas as air rather than nitrogen, since $29\ {\rm g\,mol^{-1}}$ is within $1\%$ of the value found; do not condone leaving the identification open when four candidates were listed. Do not award the final mark for naming nitrogen without mentioning the comparison against the other three values.
(e) (A1) for the cancellation argument, (A1) for the further point that it is $nRT$, not $n$, that is conserved and common, (A1) for the consequence for an isolated vessel, including the direction of the error. Accept any clear statement that a higher final temperature would inflate the apparent $V_{A}$ under the isothermal formula. Mark generously for the physical reasoning here and strictly for arithmetic -- no numerical example is expected in this part.

64. A.2 A one-dimensional elastic collision whose simultaneous conservation equations factorise, so that one of the two mathematical solutions is the collision that never happened

[14 marks]PHYS-A.2-301

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.2 Forces and momentum: momentum and impulse in one dimension, conservation of momentum in elastic collisions. With A.3 Work, energy and power: conservation of kinetic energy in an elastic collision · difficulty 4

(a) With $u_{2} = 0$, conservation of momentum gives
$$2.0(4.0) = 2.0v_{1} + 1.0v_{2} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 2v_{1} + v_{2} = 8 \;(M1),$$
and conservation of kinetic energy gives
$$\tfrac{1}{2}(2.0)(4.0)^{2} = \tfrac{1}{2}(2.0)v_{1}^{2} + \tfrac{1}{2}(1.0)v_{2}^{2} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 2v_{1}^{2} + v_{2}^{2} = 32 \;(M1).$$
Substituting $v_{2} = 8 - 2v_{1}$ into the second equation,
$$2v_{1}^{2} + (8 - 2v_{1})^{2} = 32 \;\Longrightarrow\; 6v_{1}^{2} - 32v_{1} + 32 = 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; 3v_{1}^{2} - 16v_{1} + 16 = 0 ,$$
which factorises as $(3v_{1} - 4)(v_{1} - 4) = 0$. Hence either $v_{1} = 4.0\ {\rm m\,s^{-1}}$, giving $v_{2} = 0$, or $v_{1} = \tfrac{4}{3} = 1.33\ {\rm m\,s^{-1}}$, giving $v_{2} = 8 - \tfrac{8}{3} = \tfrac{16}{3} = 5.33\ {\rm m\,s^{-1}}$ (A1)(A1). The second pair describes the actual collision. The first pair describes no collision at all: every body continues unchanged, which conserves momentum and energy trivially. Checking the accepted pair: momentum afterwards is $2.0(1.33) + 1.0(5.33) = 8.0\ {\rm kg\,m\,s^{-1}}$, equal to the initial $2.0 \times 4.0 = 8.0$, and the kinetic energy afterwards is $\tfrac{1}{2}(2.0)(1.33)^{2} + \tfrac{1}{2}(1.0)(5.33)^{2} = 16\ {\rm J}$, equal to the initial $\tfrac{1}{2}(2.0)(4.0)^{2} = 16\ {\rm J}$.
(b) Conservation of momentum gives $m_{1}(u_{1} - v_{1}) = m_{2}(v_{2} - u_{2})$, call this equation (i). Conservation of kinetic energy gives $m_{1}(u_{1}^{2} - v_{1}^{2}) = m_{2}(v_{2}^{2} - u_{2}^{2})$, which factorises as
$$m_{1}(u_{1} - v_{1})(u_{1} + v_{1}) = m_{2}(v_{2} - u_{2})(v_{2} + u_{2}) \;(M1)(A1),$$
call this (ii). Using (i) to replace $m_{2}(v_{2} - u_{2})$ by $m_{1}(u_{1} - v_{1})$ on the right-hand side of (ii) and bringing everything to one side,
$$m_{1}(u_{1} - v_{1})\bigl[(u_{1} + v_{1}) - (v_{2} + u_{2})\bigr] = 0 \;(A1),$$
as required. The first factor vanishing, $u_{1} = v_{1}$, together with (i) forces $u_{2} = v_{2}$: this is the solution in which neither body changes its velocity, i.e. no interaction took place (A1). For a genuine collision that factor is non-zero, so the second must vanish, giving $u_{1} + v_{1} = v_{2} + u_{2}$, that is
$$u_{1} - u_{2} = -(v_{1} - v_{2}) \;(A1).$$
The quantity $u_{1} - u_{2}$ is the velocity of 1 relative to 2 before the collision, and $v_{1} - v_{2}$ the same relative velocity afterwards; the result states that for a perfectly elastic collision the relative speed of approach equals the relative speed of separation (A1). It says nothing about the speeds individually, only about the rate at which the gap between the two bodies closes and then reopens.
(c) Using the result of part (b) together with conservation of momentum, or solving the two equations with $u_{2} = 0$, the velocity acquired by the struck glider is
$$v_{2} = \frac{2m_{1}}{m_{1} + m_{2}}\,u_{1} \;(M1).$$
The kinetic energy gained by glider 2 is $\tfrac{1}{2}m_{2}v_{2}^{2}$ and the initial kinetic energy is $\tfrac{1}{2}m_{1}u_{1}^{2}$, so the fraction transferred is
$$\frac{\tfrac{1}{2}m_{2}\left(\frac{2m_{1}u_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}\right)^{2}}{\tfrac{1}{2}m_{1}u_{1}^{2}} = \frac{4m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2}m_{1}} = \frac{4m_{1}m_{2}}{(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2}} \;(A1)(A1),$$
the factors $u_{1}^{2}$ cancelling, so that the fraction depends on the masses alone and not on the speed. For the data, $4(2.0)(1.0)/(3.0)^{2} = 8/9 = 0.889$, and independently the energy of glider 2 afterwards is $\tfrac{1}{2}(1.0)(5.33)^{2} = 14.2\ {\rm J}$ against an initial $16\ {\rm J}$, giving $14.2/16 = 0.89$ (A1), which agrees.
(d) The fraction $\dfrac{4m_{1}m_{2}}{(m_{1}+m_{2})^{2}}$ is maximised when $m_{1} = m_{2}$, that is for the ratio $1 : 1$ (A1); writing $m_{1} = m_{2} = m$ gives $4m^{2}/(2m)^{2} = 1$, so all of the kinetic energy is transferred and the incident glider comes to a complete stop while the struck glider moves off with the velocity the incident one had (A1). This is why a Newton's cradle transmits motion through a line of identical elastic spheres with such apparent tidiness.
(e) Both conservation laws are statements about totals, and totals are conserved equally well by an interaction and by its absence: if no contact occurs, nothing changes and both totals are trivially preserved (A1). Nothing inside the algebra distinguishes the two cases, because the algebra only knows that the sums before and after agree -- it has no way of encoding that a force actually acted. The unwanted solution is removed only by the physical premise that the bodies did collide, which is expressed mathematically by requiring $u_{1} \neq v_{1}$ so that the first factor is non-zero (A1). This is why the relative-speed result of part (b) is a consequence for collisions and not a general property of the two conservation laws.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for each of the two conservation equations written correctly with the data substituted, (A1) for reaching the quadratic and both roots, (A1) for the accepted pair $1.33$ and $5.33\ {\rm m\,s^{-1}}$ together with the statement that the other pair describes no interaction. Three fields; credit three marks. Candidates who obtain only one root by using the standard formula lose nothing provided they then identify the second pair by inspection, since the item is precisely about the second pair existing.
(b) (M1) for writing both conservation equations in general form, (A1) for factorising the difference of squares, (A1) for substituting (i) into (ii) to reach the stated product, (A1) for identifying the meaning of the vanishing first factor, (A1) for the relative-speed statement and its verbal interpretation. Five fields; cap at four. Accept any equivalent algebraic route, including combining the equations by dividing one by the other, provided the candidate notes that this division is legitimate only when the common factor is non-zero. A candidate who divides without comment loses the interpretation mark.
(c) (M1) for obtaining $v_{2} = 2m_{1}u_{1}/(m_{1}+m_{2})$, (A1) for forming the ratio of the two energies and simplifying, (A1) for reaching the stated expression with the cancellation of $u_{1}$ shown, (A1) for the numerical check. Three fields; credit three. Note that the independence of speed is a substantive observation and should be credited if stated even without the numerical check.
(d) (A1) for the ratio $1:1$, (A1) for maximum fraction $1$ together with the statement that the incident glider stops. Accept 'equal masses' in place of the ratio notation. No credit for stating the answer without any indication of why the fraction peaks there.
(e) (A1) for observing that absent interactions also conserve both totals, (A1) for the point that only the physical premise removes the second solution. Do not accept answers claiming that the second solution is 'unphysical energy gain' or anything similar: nothing is gained or lost in that solution, which is exactly why it sits there.

65. B.5 Current and circuits: e.m.f., internal resistance and power

[14 marks]PHYS-B.5-102

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.5 (Current and circuits: electromotive force, internal resistance, terminal potential difference, series circuits, power dissipation) · difficulty 4

(a) The cell drives current through $R_1$, $R_2$ and its own internal resistance in series, so the total resistance is $4.0 + 6.0 + 0.50 = 10.5$ $\Omega$. (M1) Applying $I = E/R_{total}$ gives $I = 6.0/10.5 = 0.571$ A. (A1)

(b) The voltmeter is connected across $R_2$ only, so it reads the potential difference across that resistor alone. (M1) $V = IR_2 = 0.5714 \times 6.0 = 3.43$ V. (A1)

(c) A real voltmeter of resistance 1.0 k$\Omega$ is in parallel with $R_2$, so the resistance of that branch is slightly less than 6.0 $\Omega$, not more. (M1) The parallel combination is $\frac{(6.0)(1000)}{1006} = 5.964$ $\Omega$, so the total resistance falls to 10.46 $\Omega$, the current rises to 0.5734 A, and the reading becomes $0.5734 \times 5.964 = 3.420$ V. (A1) The reading therefore decreases by about 0.009 V, which is 0.26%, because the voltmeter draws a small current that was not being drawn before. (A1)

(d) The power supplied by the cell is $P_{total} = EI = 6.0 \times 0.5714 = 3.429$ W. (M1) The power dissipated in the two resistors is $I^2(R_1+R_2) = (0.5714)^2(10.0) = 3.265$ W. (M1) The efficiency is therefore $3.265/3.429 = 0.952$, i.e. 95.2%. (A1) Equivalently, the efficiency is $\frac{R_1+R_2}{R_1+R_2+r} = \frac{10.0}{10.5}$, since the common factor $I^2$ cancels.

(e) With $R$ in place of $R_2$ the current is $I = \frac{E}{R + R_1 + r}$, so the power in $R$ is $P = I^2R = \frac{E^2R}{(R + 4.5)^2}$, writing $4.5$ for $R_1 + r$. (M1) Dividing numerator and denominator by $R$, $P = \frac{E^2}{R + 9.0 + 20.25/R}$, and the denominator is least when $R = 20.25/R$, that is $R = 4.5$ $\Omega$. (M1)(A1) The maximum power is $\frac{E^2}{4(R_1+r)} = \frac{36}{18.0} = 2.0$ W. (A1) Equivalently, $I = 6.0/9.0 = 0.667$ A and $P = (0.667)^2(4.5) = 2.0$ W.

Markscheme notes

(a) M1 for the total resistance including $r$, A1 for 0.571 A. A candidate who omits the internal resistance obtains 0.600 A and scores 0 of 2; this is the single commonest error in the question. (b) M1 for recognising that the voltmeter reads the p.d. across $R_2$ alone and not the terminal p.d., A1 for 3.43 V. Follow through from the candidate's own current. A candidate who gives 5.71 V has calculated the terminal potential difference, which is a different quantity, and scores 0 of 2. (c) M1 for stating that the voltmeter is in parallel with $R_2$ and reduces the resistance of that branch; A1 for a numerical demonstration, for which any of the new current, the new branch resistance or the new reading is acceptable; A1 for the conclusion that the reading decreases, with the direction explicitly stated. Do not award the third mark for an answer that says the reading is unchanged or increases. (d) M1 for the total power supplied, M1 for the useful power, A1 for 95.2%. Accept the ratio $10.0/10.5$ with no intermediate powers. (e) M1 for expressing the power in $R$ as a function of $R$ alone; M1 for identifying the condition for the maximum, whether by calculus, by completing the square, or by quoting the matched-load result; A1 for $R = 4.5$ $\Omega$; A1 for 2.0 W. A candidate who states the standard result that the power in a load is greatest when the load resistance equals the resistance of the rest of the circuit, and applies it correctly with $R_1 + r = 4.5$ $\Omega$, earns full marks.

66. B.5 A potential divider designed from the unloaded ratio: the load appears in parallel with the lower resistor, the output falls by forty per cent, and with the upper resistor fixed the target output is an unattainable limit rather than a mistuning

[13 marks]PHYS-B.5-202

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.5 Current and circuits: combinations of resistors in series and parallel, the potential divider, and the effect of connecting a load across the output of a divider · difficulty 4

(a) With no load the two resistors are in series across the supply, so the current is $12.0/(8.0 + 4.0) = 1.00\ \mathrm{mA}$ and the potential difference across $R_{2}$ is (M1)
$$V_{\text{out}} = 12.0 \times \frac{4.0}{8.0 + 4.0} = 12.0 \times \frac{1}{3} = 4.00\ \mathrm{V} \;(A1),$$
which is one third of the supply voltage, in the same ratio as the two resistances (A1).
(b) With the load connected, $R_{2}$ and $R_{L}$ are in parallel (M1):
$$R_{p} = \frac{R_{2}R_{L}}{R_{2} + R_{L}} = \frac{4.0 \times 4.0}{4.0 + 4.0} = 2.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega} \;(A1).$$
The total resistance of the circuit is now $8.0 + 2.0 = 10.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$, so the current through $R_{1}$ is
$$I_{1} = \frac{12.0}{10.0 \times 10^{3}} = 1.20\ \mathrm{mA} \;(A1),$$
and the output falls to
$$V_{\text{out}} = 12.0 \times \frac{2.0}{8.0 + 2.0} = 2.40\ \mathrm{V},$$
a drop of forty per cent from the designed value (A1). The current splits equally between $R_{2}$ and the load, since both are $4.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ at the same potential difference: $I_{2} = I_{L} = 2.40/4.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega} = 0.60\ \mathrm{mA}$, and $0.60 + 0.60 = 1.20\ \mathrm{mA} = I_{1}$, as Kirchhoff's first law requires.
(c) For the output to be $4.00\ \mathrm{V}$ with $R_{1} = 8.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ and a supply of $12.0\ \mathrm{V}$, the divider ratio must be one third, which requires the parallel combination to satisfy (M1)
$$\frac{R_{p}}{8.0 + R_{p}} = \frac{1}{3} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 3R_{p} = 8.0 + R_{p} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad R_{p} = 4.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega} \;(A1).$$
But $R_{p} = \dfrac{R_{2} \times 4.0}{R_{2} + 4.0}$ with $R_{2}$ in kilohms, and setting this equal to $4.0$ gives $4.0R_{2} = 4.0R_{2} + 16$, which is false for every value of $R_{2}$ (A1). The equation has no finite solution because the parallel combination of a $4.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ load with any finite resistance is strictly less than $4.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$: it approaches $4.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ from below as $R_{2} \to \infty$, and reaches it only in that limit. So the output approaches $4.00\ \mathrm{V}$ as $R_{2}$ is made larger and larger, but never attains it; at $R_{2} = 8.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ it is $3.00\ \mathrm{V}$, at $40\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ it is $3.75\ \mathrm{V}$, and at $400\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ it is $3.97\ \mathrm{V}$. The specification is unreachable with this $R_{1}$, not merely mistuned.
(d) The power delivered to the load is $V^{2}/R_{L} = 2.40^{2}/4.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega} = 1.44\ \mathrm{mW}$, and the power drawn from the supply is $\varepsilon I_{1} = 12.0 \times 1.20\ \mathrm{mA} = 14.4\ \mathrm{mW}$ (M1), so the fraction reaching the load is
$$\frac{1.44}{14.4} = 0.10 \;(A1).$$
Nine tenths of the power is dissipated in the two resistors of the divider, which is the penalty for choosing divider resistors comparable with the load. The remedy is to make both divider resistors small compared with the load, so that the load draws only a small fraction of the divider current. Keeping the one-third ratio and choosing $R_{2} = 2.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ gives $R_{p} = 1.33\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$, and the upper resistor must then be $R_{1} = 2R_{p} = 2.67\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$ (A1):
$$V_{\text{out}} = 12.0 \times \frac{1.33}{2.67 + 1.33} = 4.00\ \mathrm{V}.$$
The load is then twice $R_{2}$ rather than equal to it, and the loading error is smaller by the same factor. An alternative acceptable answer is to keep the divider and insert a buffer amplifier, or to replace the divider with a voltage regulator; both remove the dependence of the output on the load rather than reducing it.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for the series divider ratio, (A1) for $4.00\ \mathrm{V}$, (A1) for the statement that this is one third of the supply. A candidate who writes $4\ \mathrm{V}$ without units or with only one significant figure loses the second (A1).
(b) (M1) for recognising the parallel combination, (A1) for $2.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$, (A1) for the total current $1.20\ \mathrm{mA}$, (A1) for $2.40\ \mathrm{V}$. The three branch currents are not separately marked but must appear for the fourth mark to be available: a candidate who gives $2.40\ \mathrm{V}$ without any currents scores (M1)(A1) and (A1) for the voltage is not awarded because the part asks for the currents.
(c) (M1) for setting up the condition $R_{p}/(8.0 + R_{p}) = 1/3$, (A1) for $R_{p} = 4.0\ \mathrm{k\Omega}$, (A1) for showing that $4.0R_{2} = 4.0R_{2} + 16$ is false for all finite $R_{2}$ and that the limit is $4.00\ \mathrm{V}$. A candidate who merely tries two or three values of $R_{2}$ and finds them all too small has given evidence but not a proof, and loses the third mark; the part says \"show that\". A candidate who answers that $R_{2}$ should be made infinite scores the last mark.
(d) (M1) for both powers or for their ratio, (A1) for the fraction $0.10$ or ten per cent, (A1) for a specific change with a supporting calculation. The recommendation must be quantitative: \"use smaller resistors\" without a pair of values, or \"use an amplifier\" without saying what it does, scores at most two of the three marks. Accept any pair $(R_{1}, R_{2})$ for which $R_{1} = 2R_{p}$ and the output is $4.00\ \mathrm{V}$.
Follow-through: a candidate whose parallel combination is wrong but who then divides the supply in the ratio of the resistances consistently keeps the method marks. Forfeits: using $R_{2}$ instead of $R_{p}$ in the divider ratio; treating the load as drawing no current; and concluding that a larger $R_{2}$ will eventually give exactly $4.00\ \mathrm{V}$.

67. B.3 A hot-air balloon treated as a body immersed in the surrounding air: the density of the hot air is set by the absolute temperature, the temperature needed for a stated payload follows from the required density, and the payload has an upper bound equal to the mass of air displaced because the density of the hot air cannot fall below zero

[13 marks]PHYS-B.3-202

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.3 Gas laws: the ideal gas equation pV = nRT applied to the air in a balloon envelope, the relation between density and absolute temperature at constant pressure, and the upward force on a body immersed in a fluid · difficulty 4

(a) For a gas of molar mass $M$ the density is $\rho = pM/(RT)$ (M1), so
$$\rho_{\text{out}} = \frac{1.01 \times 10^{5} \times 0.0290}{8.31 \times 288} = \frac{2929}{2393} = 1.22\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} \;(A1).$$
Inside the envelope the pressure is the same as outside, because the envelope is open at the bottom, and the molar mass is the same. So the ratio of the densities is
$$\frac{\rho_{\text{in}}}{\rho_{\text{out}}} = \frac{pM/(RT)}{pM/(R \times 288)} = \frac{288}{T} \;(A1),$$
and hence $\rho_{\text{in}} = 1.22 \times 288/T$ with $T$ in kelvin. The relation is between absolute temperatures: using degrees Celsius here would be meaningless, since the ratio $288/T$ would change with the zero of the scale.
(b) The upward force on the balloon is the weight of the air it displaces less the weight of the hot air inside, and for the balloon to lift a total mass $m_{\text{tot}}$ that force must at least balance the weight of that mass (M1):
$$(\rho_{\text{out}} - \rho_{\text{in}})Vg = m_{\text{tot}}g \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \rho_{\text{in}} = \rho_{\text{out}} - \frac{m_{\text{tot}}}{V} \;(A1).$$
With $m_{\text{tot}} = 180 + 400 = 580\ \mathrm{kg}$,
$$\rho_{\text{in}} \le 1.22 - \frac{580}{2200} = 1.22 - 0.264 = 0.960\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} \;(A1),$$
and from part (a), $T = 288 \times \dfrac{1.22}{0.960} = 367\ \mathrm{K}$, that is $367 - 273 = 94\ \mathrm{{}^{\circ}C}$ (A1). This is comfortably within the range of a propane burner.
(c) The bound follows from part (b) without any new physics. Since the density of the air inside is $\rho_{\text{in}} = 1.22 \times 288/T$ and $T$ is an absolute temperature, $T > 0$ always, so $\rho_{\text{in}} > 0$ always (M1). The lift per unit volume is $\rho_{\text{out}} - \rho_{\text{in}}$, and it is largest when $\rho_{\text{in}}$ is smallest, which happens as $T \to \infty$ and $\rho_{\text{in}} \to 0$ (A1). So the greatest lift available is $\rho_{\text{out}}Vg$, the weight of the air displaced, and the greatest total mass that can be carried is
$$m_{\text{max}} = \rho_{\text{out}}V = 1.22 \times 2200 = 2692\ \mathrm{kg} \;(A1),$$
of which $180\ \mathrm{kg}$ is the balloon itself, leaving a maximum payload of about $2510\ \mathrm{kg}$. No burner, however powerful, can exceed this, because the envelope cannot displace more air than its own volume and the hot air inside always has some mass.
(d) Doubling the inside temperature from $367\ \mathrm{K}$ gives $T = 734\ \mathrm{K}$, and the inside density becomes
$$\rho_{\text{in}} = 1.22 \times \frac{288}{734} = 0.480\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}} \;(M1).$$
The lift is then $(1.22 - 0.480) \times 2200 = 1636\ \mathrm{kg}$, so the payload is $1636 - 180 = 1456\ \mathrm{kg}$ (A1). Doubling the absolute temperature from 367 K to 734 K has raised the payload from 400 kg to 1456 kg, which is more than a doubling but far from unlimited; 734 K is $461\ \mathrm{{}^{\circ}C}$, hot enough to threaten most envelope fabrics, and it has bought less than four times the original payload.
The claim that a $3000\ \mathrm{kg}$ payload could be lifted is false, and part (c) says why before any calculation is attempted: $3000\ \mathrm{kg}$ exceeds the $2510\ \mathrm{kg}$ bound. If the required density is computed anyway it comes out negative, $\rho_{\text{in}} = 1.22 - 3000/2200 = -0.14\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, and substituting that into $T = 288 \rho_{\text{out}}/\rho_{\text{in}}$ gives a negative absolute temperature, which is physically impossible (A1). A negative density is not a very hot gas; it is a sign that the equation has been asked for an answer that does not exist.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for $\rho = pM/(RT)$, (A1) for $1.22\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, (A1) for the inverse relation between density and absolute temperature with the reason that the pressure and molar mass are the same inside and out. A candidate who states the ratio but does not justify it from the shared pressure loses the third mark; the shared pressure is the physical content, since it follows from the envelope being open.
(b) (M1) for the force balance between the displaced air and the total mass, (A1) for $\rho_{\text{in}} = \rho_{\text{out}} - m_{\text{tot}}/V$, (A1) for $0.960\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, (A1) for $367\ \mathrm{K}$ or $94\ \mathrm{{}^{\circ}C}$. Accept 1.2238 and 0.9602 from unrounded working, and accept $367\ \mathrm{K}$ to three significant figures. A candidate who uses only the payload of $400\ \mathrm{kg}$ and omits the $180\ \mathrm{kg}$ of the balloon loses the fourth mark.
(c) (M1) for the observation that the inside density is positive for every positive absolute temperature, (A1) for the lift being greatest as $T \to \infty$, (A1) for $\rho_{\text{out}}V = 2692\ \mathrm{kg}$ and the payload bound of about $2510\ \mathrm{kg}$. A candidate who asserts that the payload is unlimited because the temperature can be made arbitrarily large scores nothing; a candidate who gives the bound but calls it the payload without subtracting the balloon's own mass loses the third mark.
(d) (M1) for the inside density at the doubled temperature, (A1) for the payload of about $1456\ \mathrm{kg}$, (A1) for the rejection of the $3000\ \mathrm{kg}$ claim with the negative density or the negative temperature as the reason. Condonation: a candidate who answers only that $3000\ \mathrm{kg}$ exceeds the bound from part (c) scores the third mark; the arithmetic with the negative density is an alternative route, not a requirement.
Follow-through: a candidate whose density in (a) is slightly different but who works consistently keeps the method marks in (b), (c) and (d). Forfeits: using degrees Celsius in the density ratio; forgetting that the balloon's own mass counts as part of what must be lifted; and treating the envelope as sealed, which would make the pressure inside rise with temperature and change the whole problem.

68. C.4 Standing waves on a string fixed at both ends

[13 marks]PHYS-C.4-102

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.4 (Standing waves and resonance: nodes and antinodes, boundary conditions on a string fixed at both ends, the harmonic series) and C.2 (the wave equation v = f lambda) · difficulty 4

(a) The string is fixed at both ends, so both ends must be nodes and the length of the string must contain a whole number of half-wavelengths. (M1) Counting the antinodes in the figure gives three, so the string holds three half-wavelengths and $3\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}\right) = 1.20$ m. Therefore $\lambda = \frac{2 \times 1.20}{3} = 0.800$ m. (A1) The nodes lie at the two ends and at 0.400 m and 0.800 m from one end, which is what the figure shows.

(b) The wave equation for a travelling wave is $v = f\lambda$, and the frequency of the standing wave equals the frequency of the travelling waves that compose it, so $v = 240 \times 0.800 = 192$ m s$^{-1}$. (M1)(A1) This is the speed at which a disturbance travels along the string, not the speed of any material point of the string, whose motion is transverse and much slower.

(c) The mass per unit length of the string is $\mu = \frac{m}{L} = \frac{3.60 \times 10^{-3}}{1.20} = 3.00 \times 10^{-3}$ kg m$^{-1}$. (M1) For a stretched string the wave speed is related to the tension by $v = \sqrt{T/\mu}$, so $T = \mu v^2$. (M1) Substituting, $T = (3.00 \times 10^{-3})(192)^2 = (3.00 \times 10^{-3})(3.6864 \times 10^4) = 111$ N. (A1) A tension of about a hundred newtons is plausible for a string weighing a few grams, which is a useful check on the arithmetic.

(d) The fundamental mode is the longest wavelength the string can support, with a single antinode and one half-wavelength across the fixed length. (M1) Hence $\lambda_1 = 2L = 2.40$ m and $f_1 = \frac{v}{\lambda_1} = \frac{192}{2.40} = 80.0$ Hz. (A1) Equivalently, the mode shown is the third harmonic, so $f_1 = \frac{240}{3} = 80.0$ Hz, and the two routes must agree.

(e) The standing waves possible on a string fixed at both ends have frequencies $f_n = \frac{nv}{2L}$, which are the integer multiples of the fundamental 80.0 Hz. (M1) Since $\frac{320}{80.0} = 4$ exactly, the driving frequency coincides with the fourth harmonic, so a standing wave with four half-wavelengths and four antinodes is produced. (A1) At any other frequency the waves reflected from the fixed ends return out of phase with the driver. (M1) The reflected wave then interferes destructively at the driver instead of reinforcing it, so the amplitude does not build up and only a small forced vibration remains. (A1) Resonance requires the round trip along the string to be a whole number of wavelengths, which is exactly the condition that the driving frequency be a harmonic; a frequency a few hertz away would leave the string forced but not resonant.

Markscheme notes

(a) M1 for using the fixed-end boundary condition, either by counting three half-wavelengths in the figure or by writing $L = n\lambda/2$ with $n = 3$; A1 for 0.800 m. A candidate who answers 0.400 m has read the figure as three whole wavelengths rather than three half-wavelengths, and scores 0 of 2. (b) M1 for using $v = f\lambda$, A1 for 192 m s$^{-1}$; follow through from the candidate's own wavelength in (a). (c) M1 for the linear density in kg m$^{-1}$, M1 for $v = \sqrt{T/\mu}$ rearranged or quoted correctly, A1 for 111 N. A candidate who uses the mass in grams obtains $1.11 \times 10^5$ N and scores 1 of 3. Accept 110.6 N to 111 N. (d) M1 for $\lambda_1 = 2L$ or for dividing the third-harmonic frequency by three, A1 for 80.0 Hz. Accept a route through $f_1 = f_3/3$. (e) M1 for stating that only integer multiples of the fundamental are supported; A1 for identifying the fourth harmonic, with the ratio $320/80$ shown; M1 for explaining the phase condition between the reflected wave and the driver; A1 for a conclusion that makes clear that a non-harmonic frequency produces forced vibration without resonance. Do not award the final A1 for a statement that the string would vibrate at the driving frequency regardless.

69. A.2 A pulley with a non-negligible moment of inertia, and why the two tensions differ

[12 marks]PHYS-A.2-201

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.2 Forces and momentum (Newton's second law; rotational form of the second law; connected bodies) · difficulty 4

(a) Let the tension on the hanging side be $T_{1}$ and on the table side $T_{2}$, and let the blocks accelerate at $a$.

For the hanging block, taking downwards as positive (M1):
$$m_{1}g - T_{1} = m_{1}a$$

For the table block, the only horizontal force is $T_{2}$ (M1):
$$T_{2} = m_{2}a$$

The pulley is a uniform solid disc, so $I = \tfrac{1}{2}Mr^{2} = \tfrac{1}{2}(1.0)(0.10)^{2} = 5.0 \times 10^{-3}\ \mathrm{kg\,m^{2}}$ (A1).

Because the string does not slip, $\alpha = a/r$, and the net torque on the pulley is $(T_{1} - T_{2})r$:
$$(T_{1} - T_{2})r = I\alpha = I\frac{a}{r} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad T_{1} - T_{2} = \frac{I}{r^{2}}a$$

Substituting $I = \tfrac{1}{2}Mr^{2}$ gives $T_{1} - T_{2} = \tfrac{1}{2}Ma$. Adding this to the two block equations eliminates both tensions (M1):
$$m_{1}g = \left(m_{1} + m_{2} + \tfrac{1}{2}M\right)a \quad\Longrightarrow\quad a = \frac{m_{1}g}{m_{1} + m_{2} + \tfrac{1}{2}M}$$

Numerically $\tfrac{1}{2}M = 0.50\ \mathrm{kg}$, so
$$a = \frac{(2.0)(9.81)}{2.0 + 3.0 + 0.50} = \frac{19.62}{5.50} = 3.57\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}} \tag{AG}$$

(b) From the hanging block (M1)(A1):
$$T_{1} = m_{1}(g - a) = 2.0\,(9.81 - 3.567) = 12.5\ \mathrm{N}$$

From the table block (M1)(A1):
$$T_{2} = m_{2}a = 3.0 \times 3.567 = 10.7\ \mathrm{N}$$

The difference is $T_{1} - T_{2} = 1.78\ \mathrm{N}$.

(c) The pulley is not in translational equilibrium in the rotational sense: it must gain angular speed at rate $\alpha = a/r = 35.7\ \mathrm{rad\,s^{-2}}$, and a body with moment of inertia needs a net torque to do that. The two tensions act on opposite sides of the axis, so their torques oppose; only their difference is left over to accelerate the pulley. If the tensions were equal the net torque would be zero and the pulley could never start turning. Because the string does not slip, the pulley must turn as the blocks move, so a non-zero net torque is unavoidable and the tensions cannot be equal.

(d) Energy lost by the falling block:
$$m_{1}gh = 2.0 \times 9.81 \times 1.0 = 19.6\ \mathrm{J}$$

Speed after falling $1.0\ \mathrm{m}$ from rest: $v^{2} = 2ah = 2(3.567)(1.0) = 7.13\ \mathrm{m^{2}\,s^{-2}}$, so $v = 2.67\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$.

Translational kinetic energy:
$$\tfrac{1}{2}(m_{1} + m_{2})v^{2} = \tfrac{1}{2}(5.0)(7.13) = 17.8\ \mathrm{J}$$

Rotational kinetic energy, with $\omega = v/r = 26.7\ \mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}$:
$$\tfrac{1}{2}I\omega^{2} = \tfrac{1}{2}(5.0 \times 10^{-3})(26.7)^{2} = 1.78\ \mathrm{J}$$

The sum is $17.8 + 1.78 = 19.6\ \mathrm{J}$, equal to the energy lost by the falling block (AG). The rotational term is exactly the $\tfrac{1}{2}M$ in the denominator of part (a): the pulley's share of the energy is the reason the acceleration is smaller than the ideal-pulley value.

Markscheme notes

Award M1 for each of the three force or torque equations in (a), A1 for the moment of inertia, M1 for eliminating the tensions, and AG for the final value; accept $3.57$ or $3.6\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$.

In (b) the two tensions must be different values. A candidate who gives $T_{1} = T_{2} = 11.8\ \mathrm{N}$ has assumed a massless pulley and scores 0 for the part, even if the arithmetic is perfect, because the physics is the thing being tested.

In (c) the mark is for a torque argument, not for a restatement of the numbers. "The pulley has mass" earns nothing on its own; "the net torque $(T_{1}-T_{2})r$ is needed to give the pulley angular acceleration" earns the mark. Accept a reference to rotational inertia or to $I\alpha$.

In (d) allow the energy audit to be done with $v$ found from $v^{2} = 2ah$ or from the kinetic energies alone. Follow-through applies from an incorrect $a$ in (a): if the three energies still sum to $m_{1}gh$, award the AG. Forfeits: quoting $I = Mr^{2}$ (that is a hoop), or omitting $\tfrac{1}{2}$ from the rotational kinetic energy.

Common condonations: using $g = 10\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ loses the accuracy marks in (a) and (b) but not the method marks; a candidate who carries full precision and rounds only at the end should be given the accuracy marks even if the intermediate values differ in the third figure.

70. B.4 Efficiency of a rectangular pV cycle, and why the Carnot value is not the answer

[12 marks]PHYS-B.4-201

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.4 Thermodynamics (first law; work done in a cyclic process; thermal efficiency; Carnot cycle as an upper bound) · difficulty 4

(a) On a $pV$ diagram the work done in a cycle is the area enclosed. The cycle is a rectangle spanning $\Delta p = 2.0 \times 10^{5}\ \mathrm{Pa}$ and $\Delta V = 2.0 \times 10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m^{3}}$ (M1), so
$$W = \Delta p\,\Delta V = (2.0 \times 10^{5})(2.0 \times 10^{-3}) = 400\ \mathrm{J} \tag{A1}$$

The cycle is traced clockwise, so the gas does net work on the surroundings; the isochoric legs contribute nothing because $\Delta V = 0$ (R1).

(b) Because $pV \propto T$, the four states have $pV$ products in the ratio $300 : 900 : 300 : 100$ in units of $\mathrm{J}$.

On the isobaric legs $Q = \tfrac{5}{2}\Delta(pV)$ and on the isochoric legs $Q = \tfrac{3}{2}\Delta(pV)$:

A$\rightarrow$B: $Q = \tfrac{5}{2}(900 - 300) = +1500\ \mathrm{J}$ (M1)(A1)
B$\rightarrow$C: $Q = \tfrac{3}{2}(300 - 900) = -900\ \mathrm{J}$
C$\rightarrow$D: $Q = \tfrac{5}{2}(100 - 300) = -500\ \mathrm{J}$
D$\rightarrow$A: $Q = \tfrac{3}{2}(300 - 100) = +300\ \mathrm{J}$ (M1)

The legs that supply energy are A$\rightarrow$B and D$\rightarrow$A, so
$$Q_{\mathrm{in}} = 1500 + 300 = 1800\ \mathrm{J} \tag{A1}$$

The check that the bookkeeping is right: the signed sum is $1500 - 900 - 500 + 300 = +400\ \mathrm{J}$, which is exactly $W$ from part (a), as the first law requires for a cycle.

(c) Thermal efficiency (M1)(A1):
$$\eta = \frac{W}{Q_{\mathrm{in}}} = \frac{400}{1800} = 0.222 = 22.2\ \%$$

Highest and lowest temperatures: $pV$ is greatest at B ($900$) and least at D ($100$), so the Carnot bound is (M1)
$$\eta_{C} = 1 - \frac{T_{D}}{T_{B}} = 1 - \frac{100}{900} = 0.889 = 88.9\ \%$$

(d) The Carnot efficiency is the bound for a cycle made of two isothermal and two adiabatic legs, which is the most efficient cycle possible between two reservoirs. This cycle has no isothermal legs at all: A$\rightarrow$B and C$\rightarrow$D are isobaric and B$\rightarrow$C and D$\rightarrow$A are isochoric. Heat is therefore transferred across a continuously changing temperature range rather than at two fixed temperatures, and part of the heat transfer is irreversible. The Carnot expression is an upper bound that this cycle does not approach, and quoting it as the answer confuses the bound with the value.

Markscheme notes

(a) M1 for the rectangle area $\Delta p\,\Delta V$ or for summing $\int p\,dV$ leg by leg, A1 for $400\ \mathrm{J}$, R1 for noting that the isochoric legs do no work or that the cycle is clockwise. Accept the sign as positive with "work done by the gas".

(b) M1 for using $\tfrac{5}{2}\Delta(pV)$ on the isobaric legs and $\tfrac{3}{2}\Delta(pV)$ on the isochoric legs, A1 for $+1500\ \mathrm{J}$ on A$\rightarrow$B, M1 for identifying the two legs that supply heat, A1 for $1800\ \mathrm{J}$. A candidate who sums all four legs and reports $400\ \mathrm{J}$ as the heat supplied has confused net work with heat input and scores 0 for (b); the distinction between $Q_{\mathrm{in}}$ and $|Q_{\mathrm{net}}|$ is the substance of the part.

(c) M1 for $\eta = W/Q_{\mathrm{in}}$, A1 for $22.2\ \%$, M1 for $1 - T_{D}/T_{B}$. Follow-through applies from incorrect values in (a) and (b) provided the ratio is formed correctly.

(d) The mark is for identifying that the Carnot cycle requires two isothermal legs and this cycle has none. A bare statement that "Carnot is the maximum" without saying why this cycle is not Carnot earns at most one of the two marks. Accept a reference to the reversibility of the heat transfer, or to the fact that heat is transferred over a range of temperatures.

Forfeits: using $C_{V} = \tfrac{5}{2}R$ for the isochoric legs (a diatomic slip); treating $pV$ as proportional to $T$ but forgetting the factor $nR$, which is harmless here because only ratios are needed. Condonation: a candidate who computes the efficiency from $1 - Q_{\mathrm{out}}/Q_{\mathrm{in}}$ with $Q_{\mathrm{out}} = 1400\ \mathrm{J}$ should be given full credit.

71. C.2 Intensity and intensity level from a point source, and adding a second source

[12 marks]PHYS-C.2-201

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.2 Wave model (intensity of a point source; intensity level in decibels; superposition of incoherent sources) · difficulty 4

(a) A point source spreads its power over a sphere of area $4\pi r^{2}$ (M1), so
$$I = \frac{P}{4\pi r^{2}} = \frac{0.50}{4\pi (2.0)^{2}} = \frac{0.50}{50.3} = 9.9 \times 10^{-3}\ \mathrm{W\,m^{-2}} \tag{A1}$$

The area is $4\pi(2.0)^{2} = 50.3\ \mathrm{m^{2}}$ (A1).

(b) The ratio to the threshold is
$$\frac{I}{I_{0}} = \frac{9.947 \times 10^{-3}}{1.0 \times 10^{-12}} = 9.9 \times 10^{9} \quad\text{(M1)}$$

$$L = 10\log_{10}(9.9 \times 10^{9}) = 10 \times 9.997 = 100\ \mathrm{dB} \tag{A1(A1)}$$

so $L \approx 1.0 \times 10^{2}\ \mathrm{dB}$ to two significant figures.

(c) Intensities of incoherent sources add, so the new intensity is $2I$ (M1):
$$I' = 2 \times 9.947 \times 10^{-3} = 1.99 \times 10^{-2}\ \mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$$

$$L' = 10\log_{10}\left(\frac{2I}{I_{0}}\right) = 10\log_{10}2 + 10\log_{10}\left(\frac{I}{I_{0}}\right) = 3.01 + 99.98 = 103\ \mathrm{dB} \tag{A1}$$

The increase is therefore
$$\Delta L = 10\log_{10}2 = 3.0\ \mathrm{dB} \tag{A1}$$

Doubling the acoustic power adds a fixed $3.0\ \mathrm{dB}$, whatever the original level.

(d) The level falls by $20\log_{10}(r/r_{1})$ when the distance increases from $r_{1}$ to $r$, because the intensity falls as $1/r^{2}$ and the logarithm brings down a factor of two (M1). Requiring $L = 90\ \mathrm{dB}$:
$$99.98 - 20\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{r}{2.0}\right) = 90 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 20\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{r}{2.0}\right) = 9.98$$

$$\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{r}{2.0}\right) = 0.499 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{r}{2.0} = 3.16 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad r = 6.3\ \mathrm{m} \tag{A1(A1)}$$

Check: at $6.3\ \mathrm{m}$, $I = 0.50/(4\pi \times 39.7) = 1.0 \times 10^{-3}\ \mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$, which is $10^{9}I_{0}$ and gives exactly $90\ \mathrm{dB}$.

Two results from this question are worth carrying away. Doubling the power of an incoherent source adds exactly three decibels whatever the starting level, because what doubles is the intensity and the level is logarithmic in intensity. And the level falls by about six decibels for every doubling of the distance from a point source, because the intensity falls by a factor of four and ten times the logarithm of four is six. Those two numbers between them cover most of the practical reasoning about sound levels, and neither can be reached without going through the intensity.

Markscheme notes

(a) M1 for the $4\pi r^{2}$ sphere, A1 for $9.9 \times 10^{-3}\ \mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$, A1 for the area $50.3\ \mathrm{m^{2}}$. A candidate who uses $\pi r^{2}$ has treated the source as a disc and scores 0.

(b) M1 for forming the ratio $I/I_{0}$, A1 for the logarithm, A1 for $100\ \mathrm{dB}$. Accept $99.98$, $100$ or $1.0 \times 10^{2}$.

(c) The mark is for adding intensities, not levels. A candidate who writes $100 + 100 = 200\ \mathrm{dB}$ scores 0 for the part; a candidate who writes $100 + 3 = 103\ \mathrm{dB}$ without justification scores A1 only. The general result $\Delta L = 10\log_{10}2$ should be credited even if the numerical level is wrong by follow-through.

(d) M1 for the $20\log_{10}$ distance relation (not $10\log_{10}$), A1 for the ratio $3.16$, A1 for $6.3\ \mathrm{m}$. A candidate who uses $10\log_{10}(r/r_{1})$ will get $r = 2.0 \times 10^{0.998} = 20\ \mathrm{m}$ and should be given the M1 only.

Forfeits: using $\log$ base $e$ instead of base $10$; omitting the factor $10$ in the definition of the level. Condonation: an answer of $103.0\ \mathrm{dB}$ with the increase quoted as $3.0\ \mathrm{dB}$ and the level quoted as $100\ \mathrm{dB}$ should not be penalised for the small rounding inconsistency.

72. D.2 A charged oil drop held at rest, and the p.d. that gives it an upward acceleration

[12 marks]PHYS-D.2-201

Theme D: Fields · D.2 Electric and magnetic fields (uniform field between parallel plates; force on a charge; equilibrium of a charged particle) · difficulty 4

(a) The field between parallel plates is uniform, so (M1)(A1)
$$E = \frac{V}{d} = \frac{480}{12 \times 10^{-3}} = 4.0 \times 10^{4}\ \mathrm{V\,m^{-1}}$$

with the field directed downwards if the upper plate is positive (A1).

(b) For the drop to be stationary the electric force must balance the weight exactly (M1):
$$qE = mg \quad\Longrightarrow\quad q = \frac{mg}{E} = \frac{(3.27 \times 10^{-15})(9.81)}{4.0 \times 10^{4}}$$

$$q = \frac{3.208 \times 10^{-14}}{4.0 \times 10^{4}} = 8.02 \times 10^{-19}\ \mathrm{C} \tag{A1}$$

The number of elementary charges (M1):
$$n = \frac{q}{e} = \frac{8.02 \times 10^{-19}}{1.602 \times 10^{-19}} = 5.01 \approx 5 \tag{A1}$$

The charge is $5e$, so the drop carries five excess electrons (or lacks five electrons, depending on the sign chosen).

(c) An upward acceleration of magnitude $g$ requires a net upward force equal to $mg$, so the electric force must be $2mg$ (M1):
$$qE' = 2mg \quad\Longrightarrow\quad E' = \frac{2mg}{q}$$

But $mg/q = E = 4.0 \times 10^{4}\ \mathrm{V\,m^{-1}}$ from part (b), so
$$E' = 2E = 8.0 \times 10^{4}\ \mathrm{V\,m^{-1}} \tag{A1}$$

$$V' = E'd = (8.0 \times 10^{4})(12 \times 10^{-3}) = 9.6 \times 10^{2}\ \mathrm{V} \tag{A1}$$

(d) The assumption is that the only forces are the electric force and the weight, so buoyancy and drag are negligible. If the drop also experienced an upward buoyant force $F_{b}$, the electric force required for equilibrium would be smaller, $qE = mg - F_{b}$, and the charge deduced in (b) would come out too small; the value of $n$ would then be less than $5$ and would no longer be an integer.

Part (c) can be seen without any arithmetic once the structure is clear. Equilibrium required the electric force to equal the weight, and an upward acceleration of magnitude $g$ requires a net force equal to the weight, so the electric force has to supply one weight to hold the drop up and a second weight to accelerate it. It must therefore double, and so must the field and the potential difference. The charge found in part (b) is then only needed to confirm the direction of the field, which is a useful reminder that a quantity can be known without being needed.

Markscheme notes

(a) M1 for $V/d$, A1 for $4.0 \times 10^{4}\ \mathrm{V\,m^{-1}}$, A1 for the direction. Accept $4 \times 10^{4}$ with the unit written as $\mathrm{N\,C^{-1}}$.

(b) M1 for equating $qE$ to $mg$, A1 for $8.02 \times 10^{-19}\ \mathrm{C}$, M1 for dividing by $e$, A1 for $n = 5$. A candidate who obtains $5.01$ and leaves it as $5.01$ without recognising that it must be an integer has missed the physics of charge quantisation and should be awarded the first A1 but not the second.

(c) M1 for requiring the net force to be $mg$ rather than the electric force to equal $mg$, A1 for $E' = 2E$, A1 for $960\ \mathrm{V}$. The commonest wrong answer is $480\ \mathrm{V}$, which is the p.d. already given in the stem: it satisfies $qE = mg$, which is equilibrium, not acceleration. A candidate who uses $q = 5e$ exactly obtains $961\ \mathrm{V}$; accept this, and note that $960\ \mathrm{V}$ follows from the charge found in (b), so the two are consistent to the precision of the data.

(d) The mark is for a named force that was neglected and a stated direction of the error. Accept buoyancy, drag, or the drop not being a point charge. A statement that the answer "would be different" without a direction earns 0.

Forfeits: using $g = 10\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$, which loses the accuracy marks; forgetting to convert $12\ \mathrm{mm}$ to metres. Condonation: a candidate who carries $q = 8.02 \times 10^{-19}\ \mathrm{C}$ through (c) and obtains $960\ \mathrm{V}$, or who uses $5e$ and obtains $961\ \mathrm{V}$, should both be given full credit.

73. D.3 A velocity selector, and the circular path of a singly and a doubly charged ion

[12 marks]PHYS-D.3-201

Theme D: Fields · D.3 Motion in electromagnetic fields (velocity selector; charged particle in a uniform magnetic field; radius of the circular path) · difficulty 4

(a) In the crossed fields the electric force $qE$ and the magnetic force $qvB$ act in opposite directions (M1). For the ion to pass undeflected the resultant must be zero:
$$qE = qvB \quad\Longrightarrow\quad v = \frac{E}{B} \tag{AG}$$

The charge cancels, so the condition is independent of $q$. Numerically (A1)(A1):
$$v = \frac{2.5 \times 10^{5}}{0.50} = 5.0 \times 10^{5}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$$

(b) In the second region the magnetic force is perpendicular to the velocity, so it supplies the centripetal force (M1):
$$qvB = \frac{mv^{2}}{r} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad r = \frac{mv}{qB} \tag{A1}$$

With $q = e = 1.602 \times 10^{-19}\ \mathrm{C}$ (M1):
$$r = \frac{(3.32 \times 10^{-26})(5.0 \times 10^{5})}{(1.602 \times 10^{-19})(0.50)} = \frac{1.66 \times 10^{-20}}{8.01 \times 10^{-20}} = 0.207\ \mathrm{m} \tag{A1}$$

(c) For the doubly charged ion, $q = 2e$, so (M1)
$$r_{2} = \frac{mv}{2eB} = \frac{r}{2} = \frac{0.207}{2} = 0.104\ \mathrm{m} \tag{A1}$$

The radius is exactly halved. The same speed and the same mass produce the same momentum, so doubling the charge doubles the centripetal force available and the ion curves twice as tightly (A1).

(d) The two forces in the selector are both proportional to the charge: the electric force is $qE$ and the magnetic force is $qvB$. Setting them equal divides the charge out, leaving $v = E/B$, which contains neither $q$ nor $m$. The selection is therefore a statement about speed alone, and ions of different mass and different charge all pass undeflected provided they are travelling at that one speed. This is what makes the device useful as the first stage of a mass spectrometer: it delivers a beam of known speed, so the radius in the second region can be interpreted as a measurement of $m/q$.

The halving in part (c) is exact rather than approximate, and that exactness is itself a check on the reasoning. Only one quantity changed, and the charge appears to the first power in the expression for the radius, so the radius must change by exactly the same factor. A student who obtains anything other than a clean factor of two has usually applied the magnetic force to the wrong region, or has forgotten that the force in the second region is the one supplying the centripetal acceleration.

Markscheme notes

(a) M1 for setting $qE = qvB$ with the two forces opposed, AG for $v = E/B$, A1A1 for $5.0 \times 10^{5}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. A candidate who writes $qvB = qE$ without stating that the forces oppose has the right algebra but should be asked to justify the directions; award the M1 and the AG, and reserve one A1.

(b) M1 for $qvB = mv^{2}/r$, A1 for $r = mv/(qB)$, M1 for substituting $q = e$, A1 for $0.207\ \mathrm{m}$. Accept $0.21\ \mathrm{m}$. Follow-through applies from an incorrect speed in (a).

(c) M1 for doubling the charge, A1 for $0.104\ \mathrm{m}$, A1 for the statement that the radius halves or the reasoning in terms of force. A candidate who recomputes from scratch with $q = 2e$ should be given full credit; a candidate who writes "the radius is unchanged because the mass and speed are the same" has not seen that the force also doubles and scores 0.

(d) The mark is for noting that both forces are proportional to the charge and that the charge therefore cancels. A statement that "the fields balance" without the cancellation earns 0; a statement that the ratio $E/B$ has units of speed earns the first mark only.

Forfeits: using the mass of an electron instead of the given ion mass; treating the magnetic force in the selector as centripetal, which is the error of a candidate who has not noticed that the ion is undeflected and therefore not moving in a circle. Condonation: a candidate who quotes $r = 0.21\ \mathrm{m}$ and $r_{2} = 0.10\ \mathrm{m}$ with the halving stated explicitly should be given full credit.

74. E.2 Stopping potential against frequency, with one anomalous reading

[12 marks]PHYS-E.2-201

Theme E: Nuclear and quantum physics · E.2 Quantum physics (photoelectric effect; stopping potential; Einstein's photoelectric equation) / experimental skills · difficulty 4

(a) Plot $V_{s}$ on the vertical axis against $f$ on the horizontal axis. Because $eV_{s} = hf - \phi$, this is linear, and comparing with $y = mx + c$ shows that the gradient is $h/e$, the Planck constant divided by the elementary charge. The intercept on the $V_{s}$ axis is $-\phi/e$.

(b) The five reliable points are at $f = 5.00, 6.00, 8.00, 9.00$ and $10.0$ in units of $10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$, with $V_{s} = 0.57, 0.98, 1.81, 2.22$ and $2.64\ \mathrm{V}$.

For these five points $\sum f = 38.0$, $\sum V_{s} = 8.22$, $\sum f^{2} = 306$ and $\sum f V_{s} = 69.59$ (M1)(A1). Hence
$$m = \frac{5(69.59) - (38.0)(8.22)}{5(306) - (38.0)^{2}} = \frac{347.95 - 312.36}{1530 - 1444} = \frac{35.59}{86.0} = 0.414\ \mathrm{V\,per\,}10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$$

The gradient is $h/e$, so (M1)
$$h = \frac{m e}{10^{14}} = \frac{0.414 \times 1.602 \times 10^{-19}}{10^{14}} = 6.63 \times 10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\,s}$$

which is within $0.1\ \%$ of the accepted value $6.626 \times 10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\,s}$ (A1).

(c) The intercept is
$$c = \frac{\sum V_{s} - m\sum f}{5} = \frac{8.22 - 0.4138 \times 38.0}{5} = -1.50\ \mathrm{V}$$

The threshold frequency is where $V_{s} = 0$ (M1):
$$f_{0} = -\frac{c}{m} = \frac{1.50}{0.4138} = 3.63 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz} \tag{A1}$$

The work function is the energy of a photon at the threshold (M1):
$$\phi = hf_{0} = (6.63 \times 10^{-34})(3.63 \times 10^{14}) = 2.41 \times 10^{-19}\ \mathrm{J} = \frac{2.41 \times 10^{-19}}{1.602 \times 10^{-19}} = 1.50\ \mathrm{eV} \tag{A1}$$

(d) The reading at $f = 7.00 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$ is inconsistent. Using $V_{s} = 0.4138f - 1.50$ in units of $10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$, the residuals are $+0.002, -0.002, +0.354, +0.000, -0.003$ and $+0.003\ \mathrm{V}$ for the six points in order (M1). The residual at $7.00$ is $+0.354\ \mathrm{V}$, more than a hundred times the largest of the others and some seventeen times the $0.02\ \mathrm{V}$ repeatability of the measurements, so it cannot be explained by the scatter of the apparatus (A1).

(e) A likely cause is that the violet or blue source used for that point was not clean monochromatic light, or that the cathode surface had been contaminated or oxidised since the previous reading, which would raise the apparent work function locally and hence the stopping potential. Another possibility is that the reverse potential was adjusted too quickly and the photocurrent had not fallen fully to zero when the reading was taken.

A repeat measurement would need to reproduce the raised value with the same source and a freshly cleaned cathode, and the residual would need to fall within the same few millivolts as the other points, before the reading could be accepted as evidence of a real departure from the linear relation.

Markscheme notes

(a) A1 for $V_{s}$ against $f$ (either axis assignment stated explicitly), A1 for the gradient being $h/e$. Accept "Planck constant divided by electronic charge" or "$h/e$" without a numerical value.

(b) M1 for a least-squares or gradient calculation from widely separated points, A1 for the sums, M1 for using gradient $= h/e$, A1 for $6.63 \times 10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\,s}$. A candidate who takes the gradient from adjacent table rows will be pulled by the anomalous point and should not receive the final A1. Accept a gradient in the range $0.410$ to $0.418$ and $h$ in the range $6.57$ to $6.70 \times 10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\,s}$.

(c) M1 for $f_{0} = -c/m$ or for reading the intercept from the graph, A1 for $3.6 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$, M1 for $\phi = hf_{0}$, A1 for $1.5\ \mathrm{eV}$. Follow-through applies from the gradient and intercept in (b). Accept $1.49$ to $1.51\ \mathrm{eV}$.

(d) A1 for naming $f = 7.00 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$ (or the $1.75\ \mathrm{V}$ reading), A1 for justifying it with a residual or with the deviation from the line. Naming the point by eye with no numerical justification earns only the first mark: the part exists to test whether the student can quantify rather than eyeball.

(e) A1 for a physically sensible cause that would raise the stopping potential, A1 for a statement of what the repeat must show. A candidate who suggests "human error" with no mechanism earns 0.

Forfeits: fitting all six points, which lowers the gradient to about $0.404$ and gives $h = 6.47 \times 10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\,s}$, a $2.4\ \%$ error; using $\mathrm{Hz}$ without the $10^{14}$ factor, which changes the gradient by a factor of $10^{14}$ and must be penalised as a unit error. Condonation: a candidate who works in SI frequencies throughout and obtains the same $h$ should be given full credit.

75. B.1 Ice added to warm water when the ice does not all melt

[11 marks]PHYS-B.1-201

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.1 Thermal energy transfers (specific heat capacity; specific latent heat of fusion; thermal equilibrium) · difficulty 4

(a) Cooling the water from $10.0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ to $0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ (M1)(A1):
$$Q_{\mathrm{avail}} = m_{w}c_{w}\Delta T = 0.200 \times 4180 \times 10.0 = 8.36 \times 10^{3}\ \mathrm{J}$$

(b) Melting the whole $0.050\ \mathrm{kg}$ of ice at its melting point (M1)(A1):
$$Q_{\mathrm{need}} = m_{\mathrm{ice}}L_{f} = 0.050 \times 3.34 \times 10^{5} = 1.67 \times 10^{4}\ \mathrm{J}$$

(c) Compare the two energies before doing anything else: $8.36 \times 10^{3}\ \mathrm{J}$ is available and $1.67 \times 10^{4}\ \mathrm{J}$ is needed, so only part of the ice can melt (R1). The mixture therefore settles at the melting point of ice, and the final temperature is
$$T_{f} = 0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C} \tag{A1}$$

The justification is that the water cannot cool below $0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ while ice is still present: any further energy it released would have to come out of the ice, which is already at its melting point and cannot get colder without freezing (R1)(A1).

(d) All of the energy released by the water goes into melting ice at $0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$, so (M1):
$$m_{\mathrm{melted}} = \frac{Q_{\mathrm{avail}}}{L_{f}} = \frac{8.36 \times 10^{3}}{3.34 \times 10^{5}} = 0.0250\ \mathrm{kg} \tag{A1}$$
