The ice remaining is
$$m_{\mathrm{left}} = 0.050 - 0.0250 = 0.0250\ \mathrm{kg} \tag{A1}$$

The original water has cooled all the way to $0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ and no further; it is now in equilibrium with ice at the melting point, so the mixture consists of $0.225\ \mathrm{kg}$ of water and $0.0250\ \mathrm{kg}$ of ice, both at $0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$.

The wider lesson is worth stating plainly, because this is the part that generalises. A mixture of ice and water has two possible regimes, and the numbers decide which one applies rather than the student. If the warm body holds more energy than the fusion of all the ice requires, the ice melts completely and the final temperature follows from a single equation. If it holds less, the ice survives and the temperature is pinned at the melting point instead. The algebra of the first regime is the one everybody learns, and it is also the one that gives a nonsensical answer here, so the check has to come before the equation rather than after it.

Markscheme notes

(a) M1 for $m c \Delta T$ with $\Delta T = 10.0$, A1 for $8.36 \times 10^{3}\ \mathrm{J}$. Accept $8.4 \times 10^{3}\ \mathrm{J}$.

(b) M1 for $m L_{f}$ with $m = 0.050$, A1 for $1.67 \times 10^{4}\ \mathrm{J}$.

(c) The mark here is for the comparison, not for the arithmetic. A candidate who writes $0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ with no comparison of $Q_{\mathrm{avail}}$ against $Q_{\mathrm{need}}$ scores at most half the marks, because the temperature is only correct for the reason that the comparison establishes. Award R1 for explicitly stating that not all the ice can melt, A1 for the temperature, and R1A1 for the argument that the water cannot go below the melting point while ice remains.

A candidate who assumes all the ice melts gets a final temperature of about $-8.0\,^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ and scores 0 for (c). That negative answer is self-evidently wrong and should be treated as a signal that the assumption was not tested, not as an arithmetic slip to be condoned.

(d) M1 for using $Q_{\mathrm{avail}}/L_{f}$ rather than $Q_{\mathrm{need}}/L_{f}$, A1 for $0.0250\ \mathrm{kg}$ melted, A1 for $0.0250\ \mathrm{kg}$ remaining. Follow-through applies from an incorrect (a).

Forfeits: using the latent heat of vaporisation; treating the water as cooling to some intermediate temperature and then solving a second equation, which double-counts the energy. Condonation: a candidate who finds the final temperature by solving the general mixture equation and then observes that the answer is below zero, and corrects it, should be given full credit if the reasoning is shown.

76. C.4 Resonance positions in a closed tube, and the end correction that cancels

[11 marks]PHYS-C.4-201

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.4 Standing waves and resonance (resonance in a tube closed at one end; end correction) · difficulty 4

(a) A tube closed at one end resonates when its length is an odd number of quarter wavelengths, but the antinode at the open end lies a small distance $e$ beyond the tube, so
$$L_{n} = (2n-1)\frac{\lambda}{4} - e$$

Subtracting consecutive lengths removes $e$ exactly (M1):
$$L_{2} - L_{1} = \frac{\lambda}{2} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \lambda = 2(L_{2} - L_{1}) = 2(0.500 - 0.160) = 0.680\ \mathrm{m} \tag{A1(A1)}$$

Using $L_{1}$ alone would require knowing $e$, which is not given; the difference is used precisely because the unknown cancels.

(b) (M1)(A1)
$$v = f\lambda = 512 \times 0.680 = 348\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$$

to three significant figures (A1).

(c) From the first resonance (M1):
$$e = \frac{\lambda}{4} - L_{1} = \frac{0.680}{4} - 0.160 = 0.170 - 0.160 = 0.010\ \mathrm{m} \tag{A1}$$

The end correction is $0.010\ \mathrm{m}$, or $10\ \mathrm{mm}$, of the order of the tube radius.

The data are consistent: $\dfrac{3\lambda}{4} - e = 0.510 - 0.010 = 0.500\ \mathrm{m} = L_{2}$ and $\dfrac{5\lambda}{4} - e = 0.850 - 0.010 = 0.840\ \mathrm{m} = L_{3}$, both exact.

(d) Using $L_{1}$ alone assumes the antinode is exactly at the open end, so it takes $\lambda = 4L_{1} = 0.640\ \mathrm{m}$ (M1). Then
$$v = 512 \times 0.640 = 328\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} \tag{A1}$$

The percentage error is
$$\frac{348.16 - 327.68}{348.16} \times 100 = 3.0\ \%$$

too low. The error is entirely in the wavelength, because the neglected end correction shortens the apparent quarter wavelength by $0.010\ \mathrm{m}$ out of $0.170\ \mathrm{m}$, which is $5.9\ \%$ (A1).

The strongest evidence that the model is the right one is that two fitted numbers, the wavelength and the end correction, reproduce all three measured resonance lengths exactly. Three independent measurements have been reduced to two parameters with nothing left over, which a wrong model would not achieve. That is also why the data were given as three lengths rather than one: a single length can always be explained by a single fitted quantity, but it cannot be checked.

Markscheme notes

(a) M1 for using a difference of two resonance lengths, A1A1 for $\lambda = 0.680\ \mathrm{m}$. The reasoning must mention that the end correction is the same for every resonance and therefore cancels; an answer that simply computes $2(L_{2}-L_{1})$ with no justification earns M1 and the first A1 only. Accept the use of $L_{3} - L_{2}$ instead, which gives the same wavelength.

(b) M1 for $v = f\lambda$, A1 for $348\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. Follow-through applies from an incorrect $\lambda$.

(c) M1 for $\lambda/4 - L_{1}$ or for any consistent route, A1 for $0.010\ \mathrm{m}$. Accept a value between $0.008$ and $0.012\ \mathrm{m}$; the tube radius is not given, so a candidate who argues the correction should be about $0.6r$ is reasoning correctly and should not be penalised.

(d) M1 for recognising that $\lambda = 4L_{1}$ ignores the end correction, A1 for $328\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ or for the $3.0\ \%$ error, A1 for attributing the whole discrepancy to the wavelength. A candidate who quotes $2.8\ \%$ (the error in $v$ measured against $328$) should be credited if the working is consistent.

Forfeits: treating the tube as open at both ends, which gives $\lambda = L_{2} - L_{1}$ and a speed half the correct value; assuming the first resonance is at $\lambda/2$ rather than $\lambda/4$. Condonation: quoting the speed as $348\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ or $3.5 \times 10^{2}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ are both acceptable; a candidate who then compares it with the accepted $343\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ and comments on the discrepancy should be credited for the judgement.

77. C.4 Two determinations of the same speed by standing waves in a tube, where the neglected end correction is worth more than either measurement's uncertainty

[11 marks]PHYS-C.4-202

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.4 Standing waves and resonance, with C.2 Wave model · difficulty 4

(a) Successive resonances in a tube closed at one end occur at $L = \dfrac{\lambda}{4} - e$, $\dfrac{3\lambda}{4} - e$, $\dfrac{5\lambda}{4} - e$, so consecutive positions differ by $\dfrac{\lambda}{2}$ (M1).

$$0.494 - 0.161 = 0.333\ \mathrm{m}, \qquad 0.827 - 0.494 = 0.333\ \mathrm{m} \;\; (A1).$$
$$\lambda = 2 \times 0.333 = 0.666\ \mathrm{m}, \qquad v = f\lambda = 512 \times 0.666 = 341\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} \;\; (A1).$$
To three significant figures, $v = 341\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. The two spacings agree to the nearest millimetre, so the random uncertainty on $\lambda$ is about $\dfrac{0.002}{0.333} = 0.6\%$ at worst, and on $v$ it is smaller still.

(b) The first resonance occurs where $L + e = \dfrac{\lambda}{4}$ (M1), so
$$e = \frac{\lambda}{4} - L_{1} = 0.1665 - 0.161 = 0.0055\ \mathrm{m} = 5.5\ \mathrm{mm} \;\; (A1).$$

The spacing cannot give it because $e$ is common to every resonance position. Subtracting one position from the next removes $e$ entirely: $L_{2} - L_{1} = \dfrac{\lambda}{2}$ whatever the end correction is. The spacing therefore determines $\lambda$ but carries no information about $e$, and it is only by referring a position to the *beginning* of the pattern -- which requires knowing that the first resonance is a quarter wavelength -- that $e$ can be extracted. For this tube the correction is $5.5\ \mathrm{mm}$, which is about $0.3$ of the $18\ \mathrm{mm}$ radius, as expected for a tube of this diameter.

(c) Two of the three frequencies should differ by twice the fundamental. Using the first and third,
$$\frac{843 - 169}{4} = \frac{674}{4} = 168.5\ \mathrm{Hz} \;\; (M1),$$
so the middle resonance should be at $169 + 2(168.5) = 506\ \mathrm{Hz}$, and $510\ \mathrm{Hz}$ is the inconsistent reading (A1). A $4\ \mathrm{Hz}$ error is four times the generator's $1\ \mathrm{Hz}$ resolution.

Student B used $v = 4f_{0}L$ with the first resonance at the fundamental:
$$v = 4 \times 168.5 \times 0.500 = 337\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} \;\; (A1).$$

His value is lower by $341 - 337 = 4\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, which is $1.2\%$. The cause is that he omitted the end correction. The correct relation is $v = 4f_{0}(L + e)$, so
$$v = 4 \times 168.5 \times (0.500 + 0.0055) = 341\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}},$$
which is Student A's value to three significant figures. Omitting $e$ is not a random error: it always makes the effective length too short, so the inferred speed is always too low, and the size of the effect is fixed by the tube rather than by the care taken in reading the dial. The anomaly at $510\ \mathrm{Hz}$ is a separate matter -- probably the reading was taken while the generator's display was still settling -- and it does not affect the value used, because the fundamental was obtained from the first and third readings.

(d) Student A's value should be quoted (A1).

His random uncertainty is dominated by the $\pm 1\ \mathrm{mm}$ reading of the positions, which over a $333\ \mathrm{mm}$ spacing is $0.3\%$, and the tuning fork's frequency is a specification held to better than $0.1\%$. His systematic term is handled rather than ignored: the end correction is obtained from the data and applied, so what remains is the assumption that $e$ is the same at every resonance, which is good to well under a millimetre.

Student B's random uncertainties are comparable -- $1\ \mathrm{Hz}$ in $169\ \mathrm{Hz}$ is $0.6\%$ -- but he carries an unaddressed systematic term of $1.1\%$ from the omitted end correction, and a systematic term is not reduced by repeating the measurement. His result is therefore both lower than Student A's and less trustworthy, and the fact that correcting $e$ brings his value onto Student A's is itself evidence that the correction is the right explanation rather than an arbitrary adjustment (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for recognising that consecutive resonance positions differ by half a wavelength, (A1) for $\lambda = 0.666\ \mathrm{m}$, (A1) for $v = 341\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ with unit. A candidate who divides the full span by two instead of by four -- using $0.827 - 0.161$ as a single wavelength -- obtains $0.333\ \mathrm{m}$ and cannot score the last two marks.

(b) (M1) for the relation $L_{1} + e = \lambda/4$, (A1) for $5.5\ \mathrm{mm}$. The explanation is required for the second mark: a bare number with no statement about why the spacing is blind to $e$ scores (M1) only. Accept $5$ or $6\ \mathrm{mm}$ from a candidate who rounds $\lambda/4$ first; the argument is what is being marked.

(c) (M1) for using the first and third readings to obtain the spacing, (A1) for identifying $510\ \mathrm{Hz}$, (A1) for a value of $v$ determined from the remaining readings. A candidate who identifies the anomaly but then uses all three readings -- averaging, or fitting through all three -- forfeits the third mark; the question asks for the value from the remaining two. The explanation of the direction of the discrepancy is required somewhere in the response for full credit; a value of $337\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ with no comment about the end correction scores the three marks but the candidate should be reminded of the cause.

(d) (A1) for choosing Student A, (A1) for the comparison involving both a random and a systematic term. "Student A is more accurate" alone scores the first mark only; the comparison must reach the point that a systematic term is not reduced by repetition, or the equivalent statement that the discrepancy in (c) is predicted rather than observed.

Condonation: $v = 340\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ is accepted in (a) from a candidate who uses the accepted value of the speed of sound as a check; the calculated value must appear. Follow-through: a candidate whose $f_{0}$ in (c) is obtained from the first and third readings but whose arithmetic is wrong is credited the (M1) and, if the anomaly is correctly named, the (A1); the value mark follows their own $f_{0}$.

Forfeits: treating the $510\ \mathrm{Hz}$ reading as a random error to be averaged out, which removes the anomaly without explaining it; and quoting a percentage uncertainty from the three readings without noticing that the discrepancy between the two students is systematic.

78. D.3 A cyclotron, whose exit energy is fixed by the flux density and the radius of the dees and not by the accelerating potential difference, so that raising the voltage changes the number of revolutions rather than the energy

[11 marks]PHYS-D.3-202

Theme D: Fields · Theme D: Fields, D.3 Motion in electromagnetic fields (charged particles in a uniform magnetic field, circular motion, the cyclotron); Theme A: A.3 Work, energy and power (energy gained across a potential difference). · difficulty 4

(a) Inside a dee the only force is the magnetic force, and it acts at right angles to the velocity, so it supplies the centripetal force (D.3):
$$qvB = \frac{mv^{2}}{r} \qquad \Longrightarrow \qquad r = \frac{mv}{qB}.$$
The circumference of the circular path is $2\pi r$, and the proton covers it at speed $v$, so
$$T = \frac{2\pi r}{v} = \frac{2\pi}{v} \cdot \frac{mv}{qB} = \frac{2\pi m}{qB} \;\; (M1)(A1).$$
The speed cancels. That is the whole reason the machine works: a faster proton has a larger radius but covers the larger circumference in the same time, so a single fixed frequency accelerates the proton all the way out (A1).

Numerically,
$$T = \frac{2\pi \times 1.67 \times 10^{-27}}{1.60 \times 10^{-19} \times 1.2} = 5.47 \times 10^{-8}\ \mathrm{s},$$
so the alternating potential difference must be supplied at
$$f = \frac{1}{T} = 1.83 \times 10^{7}\ \mathrm{Hz} \approx 18\ \mathrm{MHz}.$$
The proton crosses the gap twice in each revolution, so the potential difference must complete one full cycle in the same time $T$; the required frequency is therefore $f = qB/2\pi m$, about $18\ \mathrm{MHz}$.

(b) The protons leave when the radius of the path has grown to the radius of the dees, $r = R$ (D.3). From $r = mv/qB$,
$$v_{\max} = \frac{qBR}{m} = \frac{1.60 \times 10^{-19} \times 1.2 \times 0.35}{1.67 \times 10^{-27}} = 4.02 \times 10^{7}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} \;\; (M1)(A1).$$
As a fraction of the speed of light this is
$$\frac{v_{\max}}{c} = \frac{4.02 \times 10^{7}}{3.00 \times 10^{8}} = 0.134,$$
about thirteen per cent of $c$. That justifies the non-relativistic treatment, since the mass of a proton at $0.134c$ is only about $0.9\%$ greater than its rest mass.

(c) The maximum kinetic energy follows from the speed in part (b):
$$E_{k} = \tfrac{1}{2}mv_{\max}^{2} = \tfrac{1}{2} \times 1.67 \times 10^{-27} \times (4.02 \times 10^{7})^{2} = 1.35 \times 10^{-12}\ \mathrm{J} \;\; (A1).$$
Equivalently, and more directly from the geometry,
$$E_{k} = \frac{q^{2}B^{2}R^{2}}{2m} = \frac{(1.60 \times 10^{-19} \times 1.2 \times 0.35)^{2}}{2 \times 1.67 \times 10^{-27}} = 1.35 \times 10^{-12}\ \mathrm{J}.$$
In electronvolts,
$$E_{k} = \frac{1.35 \times 10^{-12}}{1.60 \times 10^{-19}} = 8.4 \times 10^{6}\ \mathrm{eV} = 8.4\ \mathrm{MeV} \;\; (A1).$$

A proton gains energy $qV$ each time it crosses the gap, and it crosses the gap twice per revolution, so it gains $2qV = 2 \times 1.60 \times 10^{-19} \times 5.0 \times 10^{4} = 1.60 \times 10^{-14}\ \mathrm{J}$ per revolution. The number of revolutions is therefore
$$N = \frac{1.35 \times 10^{-12}}{1.60 \times 10^{-14}} = 84.5,$$
that is about $85$ revolutions (A1). The protons cross the gap about $169$ times. Since the period does not change as the protons speed up, the total acceleration time is simply $N T = 84.5 \times 5.47 \times 10^{-8} \approx 4.6\ \mu\mathrm{s}$.

(d) The protons leave when the radius has grown to the fixed radius of the dees, and the speed at that moment is fixed by the fields through $v = qBR/m$ (D.3). Because $R$, $B$, $m$ and $q$ are all properties of the apparatus or the particle, the exit speed is fixed too, and so is the exit energy $\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}B^{2}R^{2}/m$ (A1).

The voltage never appears in that expression. What the voltage sets is the *energy per crossing*, $qV$, and so the number of crossings needed, $E_{k}/qV$. Raising the voltage from $50\ \mathrm{kV}$ to $100\ \mathrm{kV}$ would double the energy gained at each crossing and halve the number of revolutions, from about $85$ to about $42$, leaving the energy the protons leave with unchanged (A1). The product of the two is the same because both are constrained by the same geometry: the protons have to reach the same radius, and reaching it requires the same total energy however it was delivered.

To raise the maximum energy, the ceiling itself has to be moved. Since $E_{k} = \tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}B^{2}R^{2}/m$, a larger flux density $B$ or a larger dee radius $R$ would do it; both appear squared, so a $10\%$ increase in either raises the maximum energy by about $21\%$ (A1). Increasing $B$ is the cheaper change in practice because it does not require a larger magnet, but it runs into the relativistic limit: once $v$ is an appreciable fraction of $c$, the proton's mass increases and its period $T = 2\pi m/qB$ is no longer constant, so the fixed frequency falls out of step with the protons and the acceleration stalls. A cyclotron can only be extended in $B$ or $R$ until that happens, which is why higher-energy machines use a varying frequency.

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for equating the magnetic force to the centripetal force, (A1) for cancelling $v$ to obtain $T = 2\pi m/qB$, (A1) for the value $5.47 \times 10^{-8}\ \mathrm{s}$ and the frequency $1.83 \times 10^{7}\ \mathrm{Hz}$ (accept $18\ \mathrm{MHz}$ or $1.8 \times 10^{7}\ \mathrm{Hz}$). The independence of the speed must be stated, not merely shown by cancellation; a candidate who derives $T$ but does not draw the conclusion forfeits the third mark.

(b) (M1) for the substitution $r = R$ into $r = mv/qB$, (A1) for $4.02 \times 10^{7}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ (accept $4.0 \times 10^{7}$). The fraction $0.134$ is not separately marked but is expected as the justification for the non-relativistic treatment; a candidate who used a relativistic mass without being asked does not lose marks, provided the value is consistent.

(c) (A1) for $1.35 \times 10^{-12}\ \mathrm{J}$ (accept $1.4 \times 10^{-12}$), (A1) for $8.4\ \mathrm{MeV}$, (A1) for $84.5$ or about $85$ revolutions. A candidate who obtains the energy by multiplying $qV$ by a *fixed* number of crossings has made the central error of the item and will not match the energy; if by luck they do match, the third mark is still forfeited because the reasoning is wrong.

(d) (A1) for stating that the exit speed and therefore the energy are fixed by $B$, $R$ and the particle's $q/m$. (A1) for explaining that the voltage controls the energy per crossing and hence the number of revolutions, with the product unchanged. (A1) for naming a change to the apparatus that raises the ceiling, correctly to $B$ or $R$ (not the voltage). A candidate who says only "increase the voltage" scores at most the first mark.

Forfeits: using $r = m v / q B$ with the wrong mass, such as the electron mass; treating $T$ as depending on $v$; using $E_k = qV$ with $V = 50\ \mathrm{kV}$, which gives $8.0 \times 10^{-15}\ \mathrm{J}$ and is three orders of magnitude out; giving the frequency as $2 \times 18\ \mathrm{MHz}$ on the argument that the proton crosses the gap twice per revolution, which confuses crossings with cycles. Condonation: energies quoted as $8.4$ or $8.45\ \mathrm{MeV}$ are both accepted; the number of revolutions may be given as $84$, $84.5$ or $85$ provided the method is shown, since the proton leaves part-way through its final revolution.

79. A.2 An impulse obtained as the area under a force-time record whose sampling interval changed part-way through, so that the area depends on reading the time column rather than assuming a constant step

[10 marks]PHYS-A.2-202

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.2 Forces and momentum, with A.1 Kinematics · difficulty 4

(a) Reading the time column: the first four intervals are $10\ \mathrm{ms}$ each, the intervals $30 \to 35 \to 40$ are $5\ \mathrm{ms}$ each, and the last two are $10\ \mathrm{ms}$ (A1). So there are four intervals of $10\ \mathrm{ms}$ ($0$-$10$, $10$-$20$, $20$-$30$, $40$-$50$, $50$-$60$ is five) -- counting them explicitly: $0$-$10$, $10$-$20$, $20$-$30$, $40$-$50$ and $50$-$60$ are five intervals of $10\ \mathrm{ms}$, and $30$-$35$, $35$-$40$ are two intervals of $5\ \mathrm{ms}$ (A1). Total record length $60\ \mathrm{ms}$.

The trapezium rule approximates $\int F\,dt$ by $\sum \tfrac{1}{2}(F_{i} + F_{i+1})\Delta t_{i}$, so each pair of forces must be weighted by the time difference that actually separates them. The force is not a function of sample number; it is a function of time, and the samples are not equally spaced in time. Using a single step $\Delta t = 10\ \mathrm{ms}$ for all seven intervals would assign the same weight to a $5\ \mathrm{ms}$ interval as to a $10\ \mathrm{ms}$ one, which inflates the area near the peak where the sampling was fastest.

(b) Apply the trapezium rule interval by interval (M1):
$$\begin{aligned}
0\text{-}10: &\ \tfrac{1}{2}(0.0 + 4.2)(10) = 21.0 \\
10\text{-}20: &\ \tfrac{1}{2}(4.2 + 9.8)(10) = 70.0 \\
20\text{-}30: &\ \tfrac{1}{2}(9.8 + 15.6)(10) = 127.0 \\
30\text{-}35: &\ \tfrac{1}{2}(15.6 + 19.4)(5) = 87.5 \\
35\text{-}40: &\ \tfrac{1}{2}(19.4 + 18.2)(5) = 94.0 \\
40\text{-}50: &\ \tfrac{1}{2}(18.2 + 11.0)(10) = 146.0 \\
50\text{-}60: &\ \tfrac{1}{2}(11.0 + 3.4)(10) = 72.0
\end{aligned}$$
The sum is in $\mathrm{N\,ms}$, so
$$\sum = 21.0 + 70.0 + 127.0 + 87.5 + 94.0 + 146.0 + 72.0 = 617.5\ \mathrm{N\,ms} \;\; (A1).$$
$$J = 617.5 \times 10^{-3} = 0.6175\ \mathrm{N\,s} \approx 0.62\ \mathrm{N\,s} \;\; (A1).$$
The unit is the newton second, $\mathrm{N\,s}$, equivalently $\mathrm{kg\,m\,s^{-1}}$.

(c) The impulse equals the change in momentum, $J = \Delta p = m(v - u)$ with $u = +0.450\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ (M1):
$$v = u + \frac{J}{m} = 0.450 + \frac{0.6175}{0.850} = 0.450 + 0.7265 = 1.18\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} \;\; (A1).$$

A candidate who assumes a constant $10\ \mathrm{ms}$ interval weights the two short intervals as though they were twice as long:
$$\sum = 21.0 + 70.0 + 127.0 + 175.0 + 188.0 + 146.0 + 72.0 = 799.0\ \mathrm{N\,ms},$$
so $J = 0.799\ \mathrm{N\,s}$ and $v = 0.450 + \dfrac{0.799}{0.850} = 1.39\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ (A1).

The error is $\dfrac{799.0 - 617.5}{617.5} = 29\%$ in the impulse and $($1.39 - 1.18$)/$1.18$ = 18\%$ in the speed. It arises entirely from the two $5\ \mathrm{ms}$ intervals at the peak, where the force is largest and where doubling the assumed width adds the most area.

(d) The sampling interval should have been fixed by the operator and recorded with the data, together with a note of any change; on an automatic rate the interval is a property of the logger rather than of the experiment, and a record whose abscissa is not documented cannot be integrated unambiguously. A second clock channel, or a fixed rate with the interval written in the log, would settle it (A1).

Given the record as it stands, the honest estimate of the uncertainty in the impulse comes from the force resolution: each sample carries $\pm 0.2\ \mathrm{N}$, and the trapezium rule weights each sample by half the sum of the two intervals that touch it, namely $\tfrac{1}{2}(10 + 10) = 10\ \mathrm{ms}$ for an interior sample at a $10\ \mathrm{ms}$ step and $\tfrac{1}{2}(10 + 5) = 7.5\ \mathrm{ms}$ for one at the junction. Adding the contributions in quadrature gives a few tenths of a per cent of the total, so the resolution is not the limiting factor -- the sampling interval is (A1). The duration itself has no quoted uncertainty, so a defensible statement is that the impulse is known to about $1\%$ from the record and to whatever the undocumented interval change contributes on top.

Markscheme notes

(a) (A1) for both intervals $10\ \mathrm{ms}$ and $5\ \mathrm{ms}$ located in the right part of the record, (A1) for the reason the actual differences must be used. A candidate who says only "the intervals are not constant" scores the first mark if the two values are named.

(b) (M1) for the trapezium rule applied interval by interval with the correct widths, (A1) for $617.5\ \mathrm{N\,ms}$, (A1) for $0.6175\ \mathrm{N\,s}$ or $0.62\ \mathrm{N\,s}$ with the unit. Working left in $\mathrm{N\,ms}$ and converted at the end is the expected route; a candidate who converts each term to $\mathrm{N\,s}$ first and reaches $0.6175$ scores the same. A candidate who obtains $799\ \mathrm{N\,ms}$ scores the method mark only, and this is the discriminating case for part (c).

(c) (M1) for $J = m\Delta v$ with the correct sign convention and $u = 0.450\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, (A1) for $1.18\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, (A1) for the constant-interval value $0.799\ \mathrm{N\,s}$ and $1.39\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. A candidate who adds the impulse to the initial momentum in the wrong direction -- obtaining $0.450 - 0.7265$ -- forfeits the second mark; the trolley bounces off the sensor and its speed increases, which the data support since the force is always positive.

(d) (A1) for a concrete suggestion about recording the interval, (A1) for an uncertainty estimate that names the resolution and the weights. A candidate who computes a percentage from the resolution alone, without noticing that the interval is the dominant unknown, scores the first mark only.

Condonation: a candidate who writes the answer to (b) as $0.618\ \mathrm{N\,s}$ or $0.62\ \mathrm{N\,s}$ scores full marks; two or three significant figures are both acceptable here, since the forced resolution is $0.2\ \mathrm{N}$. Follow-through: a candidate whose (b) is $799\ \mathrm{N\,ms}$ and who then obtains $1.39\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ in (c) is fully credited the (M1) and (A1) for the method and the arithmetic, and is expected to identify $1.18$ in the second half of (c).

Forfeits: treating the force-time record as a triangle and using $\tfrac{1}{2}F_{\max}t$; taking the sum of the forces as the impulse, which omits the time dimension entirely.

80. E.4 The fuel-cycle arithmetic of a fission reactor carried through from the energy per fission to the mass of enriched uranium per year, and the sensitivity of the comparison with coal to the assumptions behind it

[10 marks]PHYS-E.4-201

Theme E: Nuclear and quantum physics · E.4 Fission, with E.3 Radioactive decay and B.1 Thermal energy transfers · difficulty 4

(a) The number of $^{235}\mathrm{U}$ nuclei in $1.00\ \mathrm{kg}$ (M1):
$$N = \frac{1.00}{235 \times 10^{-3}}\ \mathrm{mol} \times 6.02 \times 10^{23}\ \mathrm{mol^{-1}} = \frac{1000}{235} \times 6.02 \times 10^{23} = 2.56 \times 10^{24}.$$

The energy released per fission is
$$202\ \mathrm{MeV} = 202 \times 10^{6} \times 1.60 \times 10^{-19} = 3.23 \times 10^{-11}\ \mathrm{J},$$
so
$$E = 2.56 \times 10^{24} \times 3.23 \times 10^{-11} = 8.28 \times 10^{13}\ \mathrm{J} \approx 8.3 \times 10^{13}\ \mathrm{J} \;\; (A1).$$

(b) The electrical output is
$$P_{e} = 0.33 \times 3.0 \times 10^{9} = 9.9 \times 10^{8}\ \mathrm{W} \;\; (M1).$$
In one year the electrical energy delivered is
$$E_{e} = 9.9 \times 10^{8} \times 3.15 \times 10^{7} = 3.12 \times 10^{16}\ \mathrm{J} \;\; (M1).$$
All of this comes from fission, so the mass of $^{235}\mathrm{U}$ fissioned is
$$m = \frac{3.12 \times 10^{16}}{8.28 \times 10^{13}} = 377\ \mathrm{kg} \;\; (A1).$$

It is worth noting that the efficiency cancels out of nothing here: the thermal power of $3.0\ \mathrm{GW}$ is what the fuel must supply, so an alternative route is to use $3.0 \times 10^{9}\ \mathrm{W}$ directly and compute $m = \dfrac{3.0 \times 10^{9} \times 3.15 \times 10^{7}}{8.28 \times 10^{13}} = 1141\ \mathrm{kg}$ of $^{235}\mathrm{U}$ -- but that is the mass fissioned to supply the *thermal* output, and the electrical output is what the question asks about. Working in electrical energy throughout, as above, avoids the confusion.

(c) The $377\ \mathrm{kg}$ is $^{235}\mathrm{U}$ alone. Since the fuel contains only $4.0\%$ $^{235}\mathrm{U}$ by mass (M1):
$$m_{\text{fuel}} = \frac{377}{0.040} = 9.4 \times 10^{3}\ \mathrm{kg} \approx 9.4\ \mathrm{tonnes} \;\; (A1).$$
So just under ten tonnes of enriched uranium per year, of which about $9.0\ \mathrm{t}$ is $^{238}\mathrm{U}$ that does not fission.

For coal, the energy must be supplied as chemical energy and the conversion is less efficient:
$$m_{\text{coal}} = \frac{3.12 \times 10^{16}}{0.38 \times 2.4 \times 10^{7}} = 3.4 \times 10^{9}\ \mathrm{kg} = 3.4 \times 10^{6}\ \mathrm{tonnes} \;\; (A1).$$

The ratio of the two masses is $\dfrac{3.4 \times 10^{9}}{9.4 \times 10^{3}} \approx 3.6 \times 10^{5}$: the coal station consumes about three hundred and sixty thousand times the mass of fuel, each year.

(d) The comparison is a ratio of two masses and it is a real effect -- five and a half orders of magnitude is not a rounding difference -- but it is not a measure of environmental impact, and the assumptions that break the identification are worth naming.

First, the comparison counts only the fuel delivered to the station. It ignores everything upstream of that point, and the two chains are not alike. The coal figure of $3.4 \times 10^{6}$ tonnes is coal that has been mined, washed and transported, and those steps consume energy and produce emissions of their own; but the nuclear figure of $9.4$ tonnes is *enriched* uranium, and enrichment is an energy-intensive process that consumes electricity, historically generated by burning fossil fuel. A fair comparison adds the enrichment energy and the mining and milling energy to the nuclear side, and adds the transport energy to the coal side, before comparing (A1).

Second, the comparison assumes the two stations are otherwise equivalent in what they produce, and they are not. The coal station's $3.4$ million tonnes leave the plant every year as $\mathrm{CO_{2}}$ -- roughly $9 \times 10^{6}$ tonnes of it, since each tonne of coal of this energy content gives about $2.6$ tonnes of $\mathrm{CO_{2}}$ -- which is the environmental advantage the comparison is meant to capture, and it is a genuine one. But the nuclear station produces about $9.4$ tonnes of spent fuel per year which is intensely radioactive for decades and must be stored and eventually disposed of, and the comparison assigns that waste a mass of zero (A1).

Third, the numbers themselves are conditional. The $202\ \mathrm{MeV}$ per fission and the $4.0\%$ enrichment are both stated, and both could be different; more importantly the comparison assumes continuous operation at full output for a whole year, whereas a real reactor is shut for refuelling and maintenance and runs at part load at other times, so the $377\ \mathrm{kg}$ is a lower bound on the $^{235}\mathrm{U}$ consumed per year for a station of this rating. A station with a load factor of $80\%$ would consume about $80\%$ of the fuel but also generate $80\%$ of the energy, so the ratio in (c) is unchanged -- which is worth pointing out, because it shows the ratio is robust to that particular assumption even though the absolute masses are not (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for the number of nuclei from the molar mass and Avogadro's constant, (A1) for the energy, quoted as $8.3 \times 10^{13}\ \mathrm{J}$ or $8.28 \times 10^{13}\ \mathrm{J}$. A candidate who uses $235\ \mathrm{u}$ converted to kilograms ($3.90 \times 10^{-25}\ \mathrm{kg}$) reaches the same answer by dividing rather than by using the mole, and scores full marks.

(b) (M1) for the electrical power, (M1) for the energy in one year, (A1) for $377\ \mathrm{kg}$. A candidate who obtains $1141\ \mathrm{kg}$ by using the thermal power has misread which output the question asks about and scores the first method mark and nothing else; the phrase "same electrical energy" in (c) settles it. Accept values in the range $3.7$ to $3.8 \times 10^{2}\ \mathrm{kg}$.

(c) (M1) for dividing by the enrichment fraction, (A1) for $9.4 \times 10^{3}\ \mathrm{kg}$; (A1) for the coal mass $3.4 \times 10^{9}\ \mathrm{kg}$. The coal calculation can use either the electrical energy (dividing by $0.38$ as well) or the thermal energy; a candidate who uses the electrical energy without dividing by the lower efficiency obtains $1.3 \times 10^{9}\ \mathrm{kg}$ and forfeits the third mark, because the whole point of the comparison is that the coal station wastes more of its input. Follow-through from (b) is allowed.

(d) (A1) for a first assumption stated and used, (A1) for a second, (A1) for the reasoning that connects the assumption to the conclusion -- that is, a candidate must say not only what the comparison ignores but whether ignoring it strengthens or weakens the claimed advantage. Naming the waste as an unaccounted cost scores the mark; listing "waste" and "emissions" and stopping does not.

Forfeits: treating the $4.0\%$ as the fraction of the uranium that fissions rather than as the fraction of the fuel that is $^{235}\mathrm{U}$; using $238$ in the molar mass because "most of the fuel is $^{238}\mathrm{U}$", which confuses the mass loaded with the mass fissioned. Condonations: answers quoted in tonnes or in kilograms are both accepted, and the ratio in (c) may be given as an order of magnitude ($\sim 10^{5}$).

81. Stopping potential against frequency for two metals

[10 marks]PHYS-E.2-402

Theme E: Nuclear and quantum physics · E.2 Quantum physics: the photoelectric effect, the stopping potential, Einstein's photoelectric equation, the threshold frequency and the work function, and the interpretation of a graph of stopping potential against frequency · difficulty 4

(a) The graph is a straight line for each metal, so the gradient is the same at every point. Reading from the line for metal X: at $f = 10 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$ the stopping potential is about 1.8 V, and the line meets the frequency axis at about $5.5 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$ (M1).

$$\text{gradient} = \frac{1.8 - 0}{10 \times 10^{14} - 5.5 \times 10^{14}} = \frac{1.8}{4.5 \times 10^{14}} = 4.0 \times 10^{-15}\ \mathrm{V\ s}$$ (A1)

The gradient has the unit of volts per hertz, which is V s. Since $eV_s = hf - \phi$, the gradient is $h/e$, so

$$h = e \times \text{gradient} = 1.60 \times 10^{-19} \times 4.14 \times 10^{-15} = 6.6 \times 10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\ s}$$ (A1)

which agrees with the accepted value.

(b) The threshold frequency is where the line meets the frequency axis, since at that frequency the stopping potential is zero and the photon energy is exactly the work function.

For metal X, $f_0 \approx 5.5 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$ (A1). Its work function is

$$\phi_X = h f_0 = 6.63 \times 10^{-34} \times 5.55 \times 10^{14} = 3.68 \times 10^{-19}\ \mathrm{J} = \frac{3.68 \times 10^{-19}}{1.60 \times 10^{-19}} = 2.3\ \mathrm{eV}$$ (A1)

For metal Y, $f_0 \approx 7.7 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$, giving

$$\phi_Y = \frac{6.63 \times 10^{-34} \times 7.72 \times 10^{14}}{1.60 \times 10^{-19}} = 3.2\ \mathrm{eV}$$ (A1)

(c) The photoelectric equation is $eV_s = hf - \phi$, which rearranges to

$$V_s = \frac{h}{e} f - \frac{\phi}{e}$$

The gradient of the graph is therefore $h/e$ (M1). Both graphs are measurements of the same physical process on different metals, and neither $h$ nor $e$ depends on which metal is used, so the gradient is a constant of nature and must be identical for both lines. This is why the lines are parallel (A1).

The intercept on the frequency axis is the threshold frequency $f_0 = \phi/h$. This does depend on the metal, because the work function is the energy needed to remove an electron from that particular metal, and different metals hold their outer electrons more or less tightly (A1). Metal Y has the higher threshold frequency, so its work function is larger; more energy per photon is needed before any electron can escape. The intercept is therefore a property of the metal while the gradient is a property of nature.

(d) Using $V_s = (h/e)f - \phi/e$ with $f = 9.00 \times 10^{14}\ \mathrm{Hz}$:

$$V_s(X) = 4.14 \times 10^{-15} \times 9.00 \times 10^{14} - 2.30 = 3.73 - 2.30 = 1.43\ \mathrm{V}$$ (M1) (A1)

For metal Y the same photon energy is available but the work function is larger by 0.90 eV, so

$$V_s(Y) = 3.73 - 3.20 = 0.53\ \mathrm{V}$$ (A1)

The difference between the two stopping potentials is exactly the difference of the work functions, 0.90 V, which is the vertical separation of the two parallel lines at that frequency.

Markscheme notes

Part (a): M1 for reading two points off a line, A1 for the gradient 4.1 x 10^-15 V s, A1 for the value of h. Accept any gradient from 3.9 to 4.4 x 10^-15 V s and any h from 6.2 to 7.0 x 10^-34 J s, since the graph must be read by eye. The unit must be given; a bare number scores M1 only.

Part (b): A1 for each threshold frequency read from the intercepts, A1 for one correct work function. Accept 5.4 to 5.6 x 10^14 Hz for X and 7.6 to 7.8 x 10^14 Hz for Y, with work functions 2.2 to 2.4 eV and 3.1 to 3.3 eV. A candidate who reads the intercepts from the wrong axis scores nothing here.

Part (c): M1 for identifying the gradient as h/e, A1 for the argument that h and e are constants and so the gradient cannot depend on the metal, A1 for the intercept as phi/h, which does depend on the metal. The second and third marks are independent; a candidate who says only that the lines are parallel because they are parallel scores nothing.

Part (d): M1 for the equation or for using the gradient, A1 for 1.43 V, A1 for 0.53 V. Accept 0.9 V for the difference as an alternative to the second value if both are shown to be consistent.

82. A.3 The force needed to push a block up a ramp is greatest at an intermediate angle, while the work needed falls steadily as the ramp gets steeper

[7 marks]PHYS-A.3-401

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.3 Work, energy and power: work done by a force, conservation of energy. With A.2 Forces and momentum: resolution of forces on an inclined plane, friction · difficulty 4

(a) At constant speed the resultant force along the ramp is zero, so the applied force balances the component of the weight down the slope and the friction: $F = mg\sin\theta + f$ (M1). The normal reaction is $R = mg\cos\theta$ and $f = \mu R$, so

$$F = mg\sin\theta + \mu mg\cos\theta = mg(\sin\theta + \mu\cos\theta) \quad \text{(A1)}.$$

(b) Differentiate with respect to $\theta$:

$$\frac{\mathrm{d}F}{\mathrm{d}\theta} = mg(\cos\theta - \mu\sin\theta).$$

Setting this to zero gives $\tan\theta = \tfrac{1}{\mu} = 2$, so $\theta = 63.4^{\circ}$ (M1) (A1). For $\mu = 0.50$ the second derivative is negative there, so it is a maximum: at $\theta = 30^{\circ}$ the force is $183\ \mathrm{N}$, at $63.4^{\circ}$ it is $219\ \mathrm{N}$ and at $90^{\circ}$ it is $196\ \mathrm{N}$, so the force rises and then falls again.

(c) The distance travelled along the ramp is $s = \tfrac{h}{\sin\theta}$, so

$$W = Fs = mg(\sin\theta + \mu\cos\theta)\frac{h}{\sin\theta} = mgh\left(1 + \mu\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\right) = mgh(1 + \mu\cot\theta) \quad \text{(M1)}.$$

As $\theta$ increases, $\cot\theta$ decreases, so $W$ falls towards its limiting value $mgh$, the work against gravity alone (A1). Numerically, $W = 1098\ \mathrm{J}$ at $30^{\circ}$, $736\ \mathrm{J}$ at $63.4^{\circ}$ and $589\ \mathrm{J}$ at $90^{\circ}$. The two quantities move in opposite directions below $63.4^{\circ}$ because they are different things: the force is what the push must match at each instant, while the work is that force multiplied by a distance that shrinks as $\tfrac{1}{\sin\theta}$ as the ramp steepens. Between $30^{\circ}$ and $63.4^{\circ}$ the force grows by about 20 per cent while the distance falls by about a third, and the distance wins (A1).

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for resolving along the ramp with zero resultant, (A1) for the normal reaction and the friction law. A candidate who omits friction on the grounds that the block is moving at constant speed has confused constant speed with zero friction and cannot be credited beyond the first mark.

(b) (M1) for differentiating and setting the derivative to zero; (A1) for 63.4 degrees. Stating that the answer is arctan(1/mu) without the differentiation earns the method mark only if the result is justified. The check that it is a maximum rather than a minimum may be done by citing the values at the ends of the range, which is the safer route for most candidates.

(c) (M1) for the substitution s = h over sin theta and the simplification; (A1) for the observation that cot theta decreases so the work falls monotonically; (A1) for the explanation in terms of force and distance moving in opposite directions. The third mark is for the explanation, not the arithmetic: a candidate who produces the formula and says nothing about the opposite movement has answered a different question.

83. A.1 Kinematics: reading a velocity-time graph

[5 marks]PHYS-A.1-102

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.1 (Kinematics: velocity-time graphs, displacement from the area under a graph, average velocity, acceleration as the gradient) · difficulty 4

(a) A. The displacement is the signed area between the graph and the time axis. From 0 to 3.0 s the region is a triangle, $\frac{1}{2}(3.0)(9.0) = 13.5$ m; from 3.0 s to 8.0 s it is a rectangle, $(5.0)(9.0) = 45$ m; from 8.0 s to 11 s it is a triangle, $\frac{1}{2}(3.0)(9.0) = 13.5$ m; from 11 s to 12 s it is a triangle below the axis, $\frac{1}{2}(1.0)(-3.0) = -1.5$ m. Adding, $13.5 + 45 + 13.5 - 1.5 = 70.5$ m. (A1)

(b) A. The acceleration is the gradient of the graph. From 8.0 s to 12 s the graph is one straight line, so the gradient is constant over that whole interval and is $(v_{12} - v_8)/(12 - 8) = (-3.0 - 9.0)/4.0 = -3.0$ m s$^{-2}$. The velocity happens to be zero at t = 11 s, but that says nothing about the gradient: the object is passing through rest, not sitting at it. (A1)

(c) A. Average velocity is displacement divided by time, $70.5/12 = 5.875$, i.e. 5.88 m s$^{-1}$. (A1)

(d) A. Distance travelled treats every area as positive, so $13.5 + 45 + 13.5 + 1.5 = 73.5$ m. The difference between this and the answer to (a) is twice the final area, 3.0 m, which is the ground covered while moving backwards and then given up again. (A1)

(e) A. On 8.0 s to 11 s the velocity falls at 3.0 m s$^{-2}$, so $v(9.5) = 9.0 - 3.0(9.5 - 8.0) = 9.0 - 4.5 = 4.5$ m s$^{-1}$. Since the velocity is still positive there, the speed equals the velocity. (A1)

Markscheme notes

One mark per question, no negative marking, and no method marks: the key is the answer. (a) Accept 70.5 m. The distractors are all arithmetic consequences of a defensible misreading, so a candidate who answers B has found the distance and a candidate who answers C has stopped the motion at t = 11 s. (b) This is the hardest question in the cluster and the one most often failed: accept only -3.0 m s$^{-2}$. An answer of 0 m s$^{-2}$ is the error the question exists to detect, because it confuses a zero value of the function with a zero value of its gradient. (c) Accept 5.88 m s$^{-1}$. An answer of 6.13 m s$^{-1}$ is the average speed and shows the candidate has not separated the two averages. (d) Accept 73.5 m. (e) Accept 4.5 m s$^{-1}$; accept -4.5 m s$^{-1}$ only if the candidate has explicitly read the gradient as positive, since the question asks for speed and the velocity at 9.5 s is positive. Where a candidate's two answers to (a) and (d) differ by exactly 3.0 m, the pair is internally consistent and both are likely to be right.

84. A.1 Kinematics

[5 marks]PHYS-A.1-101

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.1 (Kinematics: displacement, velocity, acceleration, projectile motion, relative velocity) · difficulty 4

(a) A. Resolve the launch velocity: the horizontal component is $30\cos 40^{\circ}=22.98$ and the vertical component is $30\sin 40^{\circ}=19.28$. The horizontal component is constant throughout, and at the highest point the vertical component has been reduced to zero by gravity, so the speed there is simply $22.98$, i.e. $23.0\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (A1) The near miss is C, which reports the component that has vanished rather than the one that remains.

(b) A. The range is $v^{2}\sin 2\theta/g$, and $\sin\bigl(2(90^{\circ}-\theta)\bigr)=\sin(180^{\circ}-2\theta)=\sin 2\theta$, so the two ranges are equal. The flight time is $2v\sin\theta/g$, which is larger for the larger angle. (A1) Distractor D inverts both conclusions and C gets only the time right.

(c) A. With drag $\mathbf{F}=-k\mathbf{v}$ the component equations are separate: vertically, $m\dot v_{y}=-kv_{y}-mg$ for both balls, with the same initial condition $v_{y}=0$. The horizontal motion therefore cannot influence the descent, and both land together. (A1) This is a genuinely counter-intuitive result and B, C and D are all attempts to make the longer or faster trajectory cost time.

(d) A. Distance $=8.0(3.0)+4.0(2.0)=24+8=32\ \mathrm{m}$; displacement $=24-8=16\ \mathrm{m}$. Dividing by $5.0\ \mathrm{s}$ gives an average speed of $6.4$ and an average velocity of $3.2\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (A1) The pair is what is being tested: many candidates compute one correctly and then quote it twice.

(e) A. The crossing time is the width divided by the component of the boat's velocity perpendicular to the bank. That component is greatest, at the full $4.0\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, when the boat points straight across, so $t=120/4.0=30\ \mathrm{s}$ and the drift is $3.0\times30=90\ \mathrm{m}$. (A1) D is the trap: $45\ \mathrm{s}$ and landing opposite is a correct calculation of the *shortest path*, since the resultant speed is then $\sqrt{4.0^{2}-3.0^{2}}=2.65\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ and $120/2.65=45\ \mathrm{s}$, but the question asks for the shortest time.

Markscheme notes

One mark per question, no negative marking. (a) Accept $23.0$ or $23.1\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (b) The reasoning mark is implicit in the key: a candidate who states equal ranges but equal times has not earned it. (c) This is the hardest item in the cluster and the one most likely to be guessed wrong; accept any answer that identifies the independence of the components, and note that the result would be different for drag proportional to the square of the speed, where the components no longer separate and the fired ball would in fact land later. (d) Accept fractions; the unit mark is not separate here. (e) Award no follow-through from a mistaken minimum-time aim: the $45\ \mathrm{s}$ figure belongs to a different optimisation and using it for the drift gives $135\ \mathrm{m}$, which is not offered. Candidates who eliminate C and D first, on the grounds that cancelling the current must cost time, are using the strongest route to the key.

85. B.4 Thermodynamics

[5 marks]PHYS-B.4-101

Theme B: The particulate nature of matter · B.4 (Thermodynamics: first law, internal energy, work, heat engine efficiency, second law) · difficulty 4

(a) A. Adiabatic means $Q=0$, so the first law $\Delta U = Q - W_{\text{by}}$ becomes $\Delta U = -W_{\text{by}} = W_{\text{on}}$. Compressing the gas means work is done *on* it, so $\Delta U>0$ and the temperature rises. (A1) B confuses adiabatic with isothermal and D then inherits that confusion.

(b) A. With $W_{\text{by}}=+200\ \mathrm{J}$ and $Q=+500\ \mathrm{J}$, $\Delta U = 500-200=+300\ \mathrm{J}$. (A1) The sign convention is the whole question: B adds, C keeps the magnitude but flips the sign.

(c) A. The achieved efficiency is $\eta = W/Q_{\text{in}} = 300/900 = 0.333$. The Carnot limit depends only on the reservoir temperatures: $\eta_{C}=1-T_{C}/T_{H}=1-300/600=0.500$. (A1) The two numbers answer different questions and D, at $67\%$, comes from subtracting the achieved efficiency from one and so reporting the rejected fraction of the input as if it were the useful output.

(d) A. For an isothermal change $pV=nRT$ is constant, so $W=\int p\,dV = nRT\ln(V_{2}/V_{1}) = 8.31\times300\times\ln 2 = 1.73\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{J}$. (A1) B is $nRT$ with the logarithm omitted, and C treats the pressure as falling linearly so that a mean pressure could be used.

(e) A. Free expansion into a vacuum does no work and involves no heat transfer, so $\Delta U=0$ for an ideal gas, but the entropy increases because the gas occupies a larger volume. The second law forbids the other three: B is the Clausius statement, C is the Kelvin statement, and D contradicts the entropy form directly. (A1)

Markscheme notes

One mark per question, no negative marking. (a) Accept any answer that identifies the internal energy as increasing by the work done on the gas. (b) Candidates using the opposite sign convention consistently, so that work done on the gas is positive, should still be credited if their arithmetic and conclusion match their convention. (c) Accept $33$ or $33.3\%$ and $50\%$; a candidate who quotes the Carnot value as the achieved efficiency has not distinguished the two ideas and earns nothing. (d) Accept $1.7$ to $1.73\ \mathrm{kJ}$; note that $\ln 2$ is the only place a logarithm appears in this subtopic and it is frequently forgotten. (e) Accept any correct statement of why free expansion is permitted; a candidate who says it is permitted because no heat flows has the right answer for the wrong reason, since the decisive point is that the entropy increases. Note that B and C are both statements of the second law rather than the first, so a candidate who justifies rejecting them by saying energy would not be conserved has used the wrong law and should not be credited. Candidates often find (e) the hardest item despite it requiring no calculation, because it asks which process is permitted rather than which is forbidden; three of the four options are familiar prohibitions, so the correct answer looks wrong at first reading.

86. C.2 Wave model

[5 marks]PHYS-C.2-101

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.2 (Wave model: reflection, refraction, superposition, interference, polarisation) · difficulty 4

(a) A. A clamped end is a fixed boundary: the displacement there must be zero at every instant, so the reflected pulse must cancel the incident one at that point, which means it is inverted. A free end gives an uninverted reflection instead. (A1) The distinction is the boundary condition, not the size of the pulse.

(b) A. Frequency is set by the source and is unchanged on crossing the boundary. With $v=f\lambda$, the deep-water wavelength is $0.80/2.0=0.40\ \mathrm{m}$ and the shallow-water wavelength is $0.60/2.0=0.30\ \mathrm{m}$. (A1) C is the common error: it is the frequency that is invariant, not the wavelength.

(c) A. A path difference of $1.5\lambda$ equals three half-wavelengths, an odd number, so the phase difference is $3\pi$ radians, equivalent to $\pi$, and the superposing arrivals cancel. (A1) C is the trap worth noting: it names the correct phase difference and then draws the opposite conclusion.

(d) A. Amplitudes add before squaring: the resultant is $3A+A=4A$, so the resultant intensity is proportional to $16A^{2}$ while the larger wave alone is proportional to $9A^{2}$, giving $16/9=1.78$. (A1) B omits the squaring and C treats the waves as incoherent, which would give $(9+1)/9=1.11$ and would be right only if the phase relationship were random.

(e) A. The first polariser transmits half the unpolarised intensity, giving $I_{0}/2$ of plane-polarised light. Malus' law then multiplies by $\cos^{2}60^{\circ}=0.25$, so the final intensity is $I_{0}/2\times0.25=0.125\,I_{0}$. (A1) B, at $0.25\,I_{0}$, is Malus' law applied without the initial halving.

Markscheme notes

One mark per question, no negative marking. (a) Accept any statement that the pulse is reflected and inverted; a candidate who adds that the reflection at a free end is not inverted shows the contrasting case and should not be penalised for the extra material. (b) Accept $0.40$ and $0.30\ \mathrm{m}$; the mark is for holding the frequency constant, so a candidate who changes the frequency and gets both wavelengths wrong earns nothing. (c) Accept any justification in terms of an odd number of half-wavelengths; note that $1.5\lambda$ and $2.5\lambda$ give the same verdict, so the answer does not depend on which odd multiple was chosen. (d) Accept $1.7$ to $1.8$, or the exact fraction $16/9$; a candidate who writes $16$ has done the hard part and then omitted the division, and should be given the benefit of the doubt only if the ratio was clearly intended. (e) Accept $0.125$ or $I_{0}/8$; the two-stage structure is the point, so a single-stage answer earns nothing.

87. A.5 Galilean and special relativity

[5 marks]PHYS-A.5-101

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.5 (Galilean and special relativity) · difficulty 4

(a) A. The second postulate fixes the speed of light at $c$ for every inertial observer, so the source's motion is irrelevant and no transformation is needed. (A1) B and C are the two Galilean habits, and they are listed because $1.60c$ and $0.40c$ are the numbers a student actually produces rather than numbers chosen at random.

(b) A. The lifetime quoted for the muon at rest is the proper time, so it is the shortest of all measured lifetimes and the laboratory value is larger: $\gamma = 1/\sqrt{1-0.98^{2}} = 5.03$, giving $5.03 \times 2.2 = 11\ \mathrm{\mu s}$. (A1) This is the effect that lets muons created in the upper atmosphere reach sea level at all.

(c) A. The proper length is measured in the rod's own frame, and the moving observer measures $L = L_{0}/\gamma = 100/1.67 = 60\ \mathrm{m}$. (A1) The identification of which length is proper is the whole question; the division is trivial once that is settled.

(d) A. Neither spacecraft's frame is the Earth frame, so the speeds must be combined with $u' = (u+v)/(1+uv/c^{2}) = 1.50/1.5625 = 0.96c$. (A1) The denominator is what keeps the result below $c$.

(e) A. $\gamma = E/E_{0} = 1.533/0.511 = 3.00$, and inverting $\gamma = 1/\sqrt{1-\beta^{2}}$ gives $\beta = \sqrt{1-1/9} = 0.943$. (A1) The three distractors are all the same omission at different depths.

Markscheme notes

One mark per question, no negative marking. (a) Accept $3.00 \times 10^{8}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ written in full; no credit for any option that depends on the source speed. (b) Accept $11$ to $11.1\ \mathrm{\mu s}$; award nothing if the candidate quotes $\gamma$ correctly and then divides, since the direction of the effect is the point being tested. (c) Accept $60\ \mathrm{m}$ or $60.0\ \mathrm{m}$; a candidate who states that the transverse dimensions are unchanged shows useful extra knowledge and should not be penalised. (d) Accept $0.96c$ or $2.88 \times 10^{8}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$; any answer above $c$ is wrong on principle and should be treated as evidence the transformation was not used, whichever sign error produced it. (e) Accept $0.94c$; the exact surd $\sqrt{8/9}$ is preferable but not required. Watch for candidates who convert $1.533\ \mathrm{MeV}$ to joules and then lose a factor of $10^{6}$; the ratio method avoids this entirely and is worth encouraging in feedback.

88. C.5 Doppler effect

[5 marks]PHYS-C.5-101

Theme C: Wave behaviour · C.5 (Doppler effect) · difficulty 4

(a) A. A moving source compresses the wavefronts ahead of it, so the wavelength in the medium is shortened while the observer samples at the usual rate: $f' = fv/(v-u_{s}) = 500 \times 340/310 = 548\ \mathrm{Hz}$. (A1) Note that the denominator contains the source speed, because it is the spacing of the crests that has changed.

(b) A. A moving observer meets unchanged wavefronts more often, so it is the numerator that changes: $f' = f(v+u_{o})/v = 500 \times 370/340 = 544\ \mathrm{Hz}$. (A1) The two answers differ by about $4\ \mathrm{Hz}$ for identical speeds, and that difference is the whole point of asking both questions: the situations are physically different even though the relative speed is the same.

(c) A. The wavelength is longer than the laboratory value, so the galaxy is receding. The redshift is $z = (689-656)/656 = 0.0503$, and since $z \ll 1$ the recession speed is $zc = 1.51 \times 10^{7}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$. (A1) Direction first, magnitude second: the sign of the wavelength change carries the physics and is worth stating before any arithmetic.

(d) A. Reflection imposes the shift twice and only the along-beam component contributes, so $\Delta f = 2fv\cos 60^{\circ}/c = 2 \times 5.0\times10^{6} \times 0.40 \times 0.500/1540 = 1.30\ \mathrm{kHz}$. (A1) The factor of two is not a correction: the blood is first a moving observer and then a moving source.

(e) A. The wavelets emitted along the flight path superpose on a cone whose half-angle satisfies $\sin\theta = v_{\text{sound}}/v_{\text{source}} = 340/510 = 0.667$, giving $\theta = 41.8^{\circ}$. (A1) A faster aircraft makes the cone narrower, which is the check that rules out the cosine.

Markscheme notes

One mark per question, no negative marking. (a) Accept $548$ to $549\ \mathrm{Hz}$. (b) Accept $544\ \mathrm{Hz}$; if a candidate gives $548\ \mathrm{Hz}$ here, note that they have used the source formula and consider whether part (a) should also be reviewed, since the same error produces both. (c) Accept $1.5 \times 10^{7}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$ and require the direction; a magnitude alone is not the answer to 'what is the motion'. Accept the relativistic expression if offered, but note that it changes the third figure only. (d) Accept $1.29$ to $1.30\ \mathrm{kHz}$, and accept $1299\ \mathrm{Hz}$. Award nothing if the factor of two is missing, even if the cosine is handled correctly, because the reflection is the part of the physics that is specific to this technique. (e) Accept $42^{\circ}$ or the exact statement $\sin\theta = 2/3$; a candidate who writes $\sin\theta = 3/2$ and then notes the impossibility has understood the geometry better than one who produces $41.8^{\circ}$ by rote, and should be credited.

89. E.1 / E.2 Photons, the photoelectric effect and matter waves

[5 marks]PHYS-E.2-101

Theme E: Nuclear and quantum physics · E.1 (Structure of the atom), E.2 (Quantum physics) · difficulty 4

(a) $E=\dfrac{hc}{\lambda}=\dfrac{6.63\times10^{-34}\times3.00\times10^{8}}{420\times10^{-9}}=4.74\times10^{-19}$ J, which is option A. (A1) Option B is the same number in electron-volts, so it catches anyone who converts and then forgets which unit they are in; option C comes from multiplying by the wavelength; option D is the frequency, one step short. (a) A

(b) $hf=6.63\times10^{-34}\times8.00\times10^{14}=5.304\times10^{-19}$ J $=3.315$ eV. Subtracting the work function gives $K_{\max}=3.315-2.28=1.035$ eV $=1.66\times10^{-19}$ J, option A. (A1) The trap in option B is subtracting $2.28\times10^{-19}$ J, which is not the same quantity as $2.28$ eV. (b) A

(c) $eV_s=K_{\max}$ in electron-volts, so $V_s=1.035$ V, option A. (A1) The rule is that one electron-volt corresponds to one volt for a singly charged particle, which is why the conversion is trivial once (b) is in eV and impossible if it is left in joules. (c) A

(d) $p=\sqrt{2\,m\,eV}=\sqrt{2\times9.11\times10^{-31}\times1.60\times10^{-19}\times150}=6.61\times10^{-24}$ kg m per second, so $\lambda=\dfrac{h}{p}=\dfrac{6.63\times10^{-34}}{6.61\times10^{-24}}=1.00\times10^{-10}$ m, option A. (A1) The factor of two is the discriminator here, and it is worth noting that $0.10$ nm is of the order of an atomic spacing, which is why electron diffraction works. (d) A

(e) $E=\dfrac{6.63\times10^{-34}\times3.00\times10^{8}}{640\times10^{-9}}=3.11\times10^{-19}$ J, so the rate is $\dfrac{2.0\times10^{-3}}{3.11\times10^{-19}}=6.4\times10^{15}$ per second, option A. (A1) Option B is the most dangerous distractor in the cluster because the arithmetic is identical and only the prefix differs. (e) A

Markscheme notes

One mark per item and there is no negative marking, so a candidate should be advised to commit to an answer rather than leave a blank. In (a) condone $4.7$ to $4.8\times10^{-19}$ J. In (b) allow the answer in eV provided it is identified as such, and note that a candidate who works entirely in joules must convert the work function first; that conversion is where the mark is actually won or lost. In (d) allow $0.10$ nm or $1.0\times10^{-10}$ m, and credit a candidate who reaches $1.42\times10^{-10}$ m only if they show the factor of two explicitly, since otherwise the working is wrong even though the answer looks close. In (e) accept $6.4\times10^{15}$ to $6.5\times10^{15}$. It is worth telling candidates explicitly that the electron-volt is doing real work in this cluster rather than being decoration: in (b) the subtraction is only possible after the work function has been brought into the same unit as the photon energy, and in (c) the answer in volts is the answer in electron-volts, which is the sense in which the unit simplifies rather than complicates the arithmetic.

90. A.3 A vehicle driven at constant power, whose driving force therefore falls as the speed rises, so that the constant-acceleration equations do not apply

[13 marks]PHYS-A.3-201

Theme A: Space, time and motion · A.3 Work, energy and power: power as the rate of doing work, applied to a vehicle whose engine delivers constant power, and the relationship between driving force, power and speed · difficulty 3

(a) Using $P = Fv$ with $P = 6.0 \times 10^{4}\ {\rm W}$:
$$F_{10} = \frac{6.0 \times 10^{4}}{10} = 6.0 \times 10^{3}\ {\rm N}, \qquad F_{20} = \frac{6.0 \times 10^{4}}{20} = 3.0 \times 10^{3}\ {\rm N} \;(M1)(A1).$$
The driving force halves when the speed doubles, even though the power is the same throughout.
(b) With no resistance the net force is the driving force, so $F = ma$ and
$$a = \frac{F}{m} = \frac{P}{mv} \;(M1).$$
Since $P$ and $m$ are both constant, $a \propto 1/v$ as required (A1). Numerically,
$$a_{10} = \frac{6.0 \times 10^{3}}{1200} = 5.0\ {\rm m\,s^{-2}}, \qquad a_{20} = \frac{3.0 \times 10^{3}}{1200} = 2.5\ {\rm m\,s^{-2}} \;(A1).$$
(c) The work done by the engine in time $t$ at constant power is $Pt$, and this equals the gain in kinetic energy:
$$Pt = \tfrac{1}{2}m\bigl(v^{2} - u^{2}\bigr) \;(M1)$$
$$t = \frac{\tfrac{1}{2}(1200)\bigl(20^{2} - 10^{2}\bigr)}{6.0 \times 10^{4}} = \frac{600 \times 300}{6.0 \times 10^{4}} = \frac{1.80 \times 10^{5}}{6.0 \times 10^{4}} = 3.0\ {\rm s} \;(M1)(A1).$$
(d) Using $t = (v-u)/a$ with $a = 5.0\ {\rm m\,s^{-2}}$ gives
$$t = \frac{20 - 10}{5.0} = 2.0\ {\rm s} \;(A1),$$
which is too short. The constant-acceleration equations require the acceleration to be constant, and part (b) showed that it is not: the driving force is $P/v$, so as the car speeds up the force, and hence the acceleration, falls continuously from $5.0$ to $2.5\ {\rm m\,s^{-2}}$ over the interval (M1)(A1). Because the acceleration is falling, the car spends longer at the slower end of the range than a constant-acceleration model allows, so the true time exceeds $2.0\ {\rm s}$. The mean acceleration over the interval is
$$\bar{a} = \frac{v - u}{t} = \frac{10}{3.0} = 3.3\ {\rm m\,s^{-2}} \;(A1),$$
which does lie between $2.5$ and $5.0\ {\rm m\,s^{-2}}$ as it must, and is closer to the smaller of the two because the car spends more time near the lower end of the speed range.
(e) At the maximum speed the driving force has fallen to equal the resistive force, so the net force, and hence the acceleration, is zero (M1). With $F = 500\ {\rm N}$:
$$v_{\max} = \frac{P}{F} = \frac{6.0 \times 10^{4}}{500} = 120\ {\rm m\,s^{-1}}, \qquad a = 0 \;(A1).$$
(This is about $430\ {\rm km\,h^{-1}}$, which is far faster than any ordinary car; the figures were chosen to make the arithmetic clean rather than realistic.)

Markscheme notes

(a) (M1) for using $P = Fv$, (A1) for both forces. Accept $6.0\ {\rm kN}$ and $3.0\ {\rm kN}$. A candidate who gives the same force at both speeds has assumed constant force and should be followed through into (b) but loses this mark.
(b) (M1) for combining $F = ma$ with $P = Fv$, (A1) for stating the inverse proportionality explicitly, (A1) for both accelerations. Candidates may instead note that doubling the speed halves the force and therefore halves the acceleration; this is acceptable as the demonstration of proportionality.
(c) (M1) for the energy statement $Pt = \Delta E_{k}$, (M1) for substituting with the squares of the speeds taken correctly, (A1) for $3.0\ {\rm s}$. A candidate who writes $\tfrac{1}{2}m(v-u)^{2}$ instead of $\tfrac{1}{2}m(v^{2}-u^{2})$ should be treated as having made a physics error rather than an arithmetic one and loses the second mark.
(d) (A1) for stating $2.0\ {\rm s}$, (M1) for identifying that the acceleration is not constant, (A1) for explaining the direction of the error in terms of varying acceleration, (A1) for the mean acceleration $3.3\ {\rm m\,s^{-2}}$ and the observation that it lies between the two endpoint values. Four fields; credit three. Do not accept 'the acceleration averages out' without the remark that it is the time spent at each speed that matters.
(e) (M1) for recognising that driving force equals resistive force at the maximum speed, (A1) for $120\ {\rm m\,s^{-1}}$ and $a = 0$. Accept the speed in kilometres per hour if the conversion is shown.
